Abstract (英文): Video LLMs fail conspicuously at counting how many times an object crosses the screen, a failure attributed to missing object permanence. Using construction-grounded synthetic testbeds with independently validated labels, we show this attribution is incomplete: the count signal is present but unread - linear probes decode per-window presence at 93% while global count decoding collapses to a duration confound. Concurrent work reports the same representation-output dissociation for repetition counting; we show that in exit-and-reappear event counting it is repairable: two readout-directed repairs (episode-localization supervision and a discrete event-symbol side channel) raise same-object counting from 46.7% to 65.0% (oracle 66.7%) on a chance-33% benchmark across 7B-30B systems. Transfer to an independent factory costs nothing, and an audit of the measurement apparatus exposes four data-integrity failures that had obscured the boundary. A real-video audit (REALCOUNT, n=18, blind-audited) shows the boundary persists - best 55.6% at chance 33.3% - and symbol transfer fails zero-shot. A 1-epoch in-domain adaptation bench is negative (six levers at 28-50%), but a 3-epoch schedule shows this is budget-limited for the symbol channel: with a 3-epoch schedule and sequence-disjoint oracle timelines the reader reaches 94.4% on REALCOUNT, and with the deployed detector's timelines a 2-epoch run reaches 72.2%. The residual gap is detector evidence and the reader's match to detector noise, not reader capacity: a frozen-feature boundary probe finds the signal weak (AUC 0.62 over a 0.51 permutation control) and non-transferring across clips (0.77 within-clip vs. 0.62 across windows).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Humans answer complex visual questions by inspecting short moments, updating what they know, and redirecting attention toward missing evidence. Existing video benchmarks, however, often provide the relevant video, evaluate only final answers or isolated localization, or permit textual shortcuts, leaving multi-round visual evidence seeking underexplored. We introduce Video-DCI, a diagnostic benchmark and functionally matched human-agent protocol for Direct Video-Corpus Interaction. It comprises DCI-Loc for temporal evidence localization and DCI-QA for end-to-end discovery, retention, and composition of cross-video evidence. In DCI-QA, videos within each pool are topically related, yet every answer requires hidden temporal evidence from multiple videos. Given only the question and video pool, humans and agents iteratively inspect bounded local windows, retain supporting evidence, and answer with cited temporal spans. Human trajectories provide reference curves for evidence acquisition and use across rounds. Experiments reveal a consistent retrieval-to-grounding gap. On DCI-Loc, frontier VLMs often retrieve and expose the reference moment, yet convert far less of this exposure into precise final citations than the human reference. On DCI-QA, the loss begins earlier and compounds across videos. Ablation studies further confirm that iterative search outperforms one-shot retrieval. Multi-round search is therefore necessary but insufficient: current agents accumulate candidates and observations more readily than precise, jointly sufficient evidence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Handwritten character recognition systems can achieve high accuracy while retaining concentrated errors among visually similar classes. Motivated by residual letter confusions in earlier geometry-based recognition work, we investigate whether these errors can be addressed through the training objective. We introduce Confusion-Adaptive Margin Learning (CAML), which constructs a sparse weighted confusion graph from a discovery partition held out from pilot training and applies pair-specific cosine-separation constraints during final training. CAML adds no classifier, routing rule, or inference-time parameters.
  We evaluate CAML on EMNIST Letters against a strictly matched cross-entropy CNN baseline, using the same architecture, final training data, deterministic augmentation, optimizer, and checkpoint selection. Eight confusion pairs are targeted: I/L, G/Q, U/V, R/V, D/O, C/E, I/J, and H/N. On the official clean test set, accuracy changes from 95.6346% to 95.6731%, and macro-F1 from 0.956326 to 0.956724. Mean accuracy across ten predefined corrupted conditions increases from approximately 95.3812% to 95.4303%; symmetric errors within targeted pairs decrease from 614 to 605 (1.47%).
  The global differences are small and not statistically reliable. CAML fixes 124 baseline errors but introduces 116 new errors on clean data (exact McNemar p=0.6515), and no corruption-specific difference remains significant after Holm correction. Thus, CAML does not establish global superiority, but shows that a leakage-controlled, pair-specific margin objective can alter targeted error structure with limited overall benefit for an already strong classifier.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Federated Learning (FL), Split Learning (SL), and Split Federated Learning (SplitFed) enable collaborative medical image analysis without sharing raw patient data. However, intermediate features and gradients exchanged during SplitFed training may reveal sensitive information through reconstruction attacks. To address this vulnerability, we propose \textbf{DiffusedSplitFed}, a SplitFed framework that integrates Latent Denoising Diffusion Models (LDDMs) at client--server split points to obfuscate transmitted representations while preserving segmentation performance. Three architectural variants (V1--V3) are developed using different combinations of dual conditioning and global feature fusion to investigate trade-offs among reconstruction resistance, segmentation accuracy, and computational complexity. Experiments on three medical imaging datasets (HAM10K, Blastocysts, and FHPsAOPMSB), supported by multiple independent runs and paired statistical analyses, demonstrate improved resistance to the evaluated image reconstruction attacks compared with Baseline SplitFed, while maintaining competitive segmentation performance. Comparisons with existing diffusion-based segmentation methods further demonstrate competitive segmentation accuracy under the evaluated settings. A conditional convergence analysis is also provided to characterize the effects of LDDM-induced gradient perturbations and local optimization on training. These findings highlight the potential of latent diffusion-based representation obfuscation for reconstruction-resilient collaborative medical image segmentation. The implementation is available at https://github.com/ChamaniS/DiffusedSplitFed.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Chest radiograph interpretation errors often arise from failures in visual search: although trainees may correctly identify an abnormality once pointed out, they frequently fail to fixate on the relevant region, while expert search strategies (where radiologists look, in what sequence, and for how long) remain largely implicit. Eye movement modeling examples (EMME), which replay a recorded expert's gaze over the image, have been shown to improve novice detection of findings on chest radiographs, but producing one requires an eye-tracking session on that specific case, so teaching sets are limited to recorded sessions by experts. In this work, RadAI is presented, the first end-to-end framework for transforming predicted radiologist gaze into narrated instructional videos, enabling scalable gaze-guided training without collecting new eye-tracking data. Given only a chest X-ray and its report, RadAI predicts an expert fixation sequence, associates each interval of that sequence with the diagnostic finding it pursues, extracts the corresponding image region, and renders the result as an EMME-style video in which each highlighted finding is narrated with synthesized speech. RadAI is evaluated on 97 cases from the EGD-CXR dataset, assessing whether the highlighted regions correspond to annotated abnormality locations. We position this work as a framework for scalable generation of educational content for residents and medical students in the field of radiology.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dataset distillation (DD) synthesizes a small training set from a large one, but applying it to 3D medical classification is constrained by the memory and optimization costs of synthesizing full volumetric samples. Under a storage-equivalent budget, distilling in the latent space of a frozen autoencoder reduces this cost and fits more synthetic samples (prototypes) than in voxel space. However, class-level matching, such as distribution matching, causes prototypes to concentrate in dense latent regions while underrepresenting sparser intra-class regions, a consistent imbalance that we term prototype concentration. To address this, we propose MAPA (Mode-Aware Prototype Allocation), a latent distillation method that spreads prototypes across intra-class modes while preserving class-level matching. MAPA marks these modes with fixed anchors estimated by clustering the real latents and distributes prototypes over them through a soft assignment, together with a lightweight anti-collapse regularizer and curriculum weighting that stabilize the optimization. Across five 3D medical classification tasks, MAPA improves the aggregate balanced accuracy by +4.2 points over the strongest latent baseline while using the same number of prototypes. An exploratory severity analysis on MosMedData suggests improved worst-stratum recall without loss of normal-case specificity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in multimodal tasks. Nevertheless, their outputs remain prone to object hallucinations that are inconsistent with visual content. Contrastive decoding, as a training-free inference-time mitigation approach, typically intervenes in the output distribution at every decoding step. However, such indiscriminate full-stage intervention may disrupt originally correct generations and even induce new hallucinations. To address this issue, we first analyze the relationship between internal attention features and hallucinated token segments in LVLMs, revealing that hallucination-related abnormalities already emerge progressively in preceding token segments. Based on this observation, we propose HALMES, a lightweight plug-and-play module that identifies hallucination precursor segments from attention features during generation and selectively triggers contrastive decoding. HALMES can be integrated with various full-stage contrastive decoding methods, transforming indiscriminate intervention into on-demand targeted correction. Extensive experiments across different LVLM architectures and multiple benchmarks show that HALMES further improves the hallucination mitigation performance of existing contrastive decoding methods while reducing the negative impact of full-stage intervention on normal outputs, validating the importance of modeling and selectively intervening on hallucination-prone segments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hippocampal atrophy is among the earliest structural markers of Alzheimer's disease, and reliable hippocampal segmentation across serial MRI visits is a prerequisite for measuring it. We present HippoMamba, a network that combines a 3D convolutional encoder and decoder with a Mamba selective state space block at the bottleneck, designed to process one or more MRI visits of a subject. On the Medical Segmentation Decathlon hippocampus dataset (260 subjects, five-fold cross-validation), HippoMamba obtained a mean Dice similarity coefficient of 0.8861 $\pm$ 0.0094, the highest among six convolutional and transformer-based methods trained with the same folds and patch size, and its per-subject Dice was significantly higher than that of each method (Holm-corrected Wilcoxon tests, $p \leq 0.013$). Its mean 95th percentile Hausdorff distance (1.45 mm) and average surface distance (0.44 mm) were the lowest of the compared methods, although they did not differ significantly from those of Attention U-Net, and the Hausdorff distance did not differ significantly from that of SegResNet. Without retraining, the five-fold ensemble was applied to 600 T1-weighted sessions from 150 participants of the OASIS-3 cohort. In sessions meeting a registration quality criterion, predicted hippocampal volumes ranked consistently with FreeSurfer volumes (Pearson r = 0.778, 95% CI 0.699 to 0.838; ICC(3,1) = 0.772) but were systematically smaller by about 27%. In a pre-specified analysis of participants followed for at least two years, annualised volume change derived from per-visit segmentation was moderately correlated with that of FreeSurfer (r = 0.503, 95% CI 0.319 to 0.659), with a larger mean rate. These results support the use of HippoMamba for hippocampal segmentation and for ranking hippocampal volume within a cohort, with absolute volumes and rates of change interpreted relative to the reference method.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This note describes a multi-view video dataset of a deformable object driven by a measured input, with the measured pose of every camera, and a first 3D result obtained on it with LaGSplat. LaGSplat learns from video a low-dimensional latent state that is both the generalized coordinate of a learned dissipative Lagrangian and the conditioning variable of a Gaussian Splatting decoder; fitting the decoder in 3D calls for synchronized multi-view footage, which the original paper only had from synthetic renderings. Here a robot arm moves a flexible strip by oscillating along a one-dimensional path, and four iPhones, three fixed and one handheld, film it for ten minutes. The videos are synchronized, the cameras are posed from the scene, and the position of the gripper is given per frame as the input of the system. LaGSplat fitted on this data with the gripper acceleration as a forcing term renders held-out views at 26 to 33 dB PSNR and runs as a real-time interactive demo in a web browser. Dataset, checkpoints and training code are public.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Although audio-driven human animation has achieved impressive realism, it still lacks effective Multi-Scene Temporal Control: orchestrating background transitions at precise timestamps while preserving a coherent foreground subject. We show that this challenge stems from the diffusion transformer's self-attention mechanism, and addressing it requires two capabilities: (1) Semantic-Aware Temporal Decoupling, which isolates background attention across scenes while retaining cross-scene foreground attention for subject consistency; and (2) Foreground Motion Localization, which accurately tracks the foreground subject across latent frames, especially under large motion. To address these challenges, we introduce SceneShifter, a training-free framework for precise multi-scene control in audio-driven human animation. SceneShifter guides spatio-temporal self-attention by suppressing cross-scene attention among background tokens while preserving cross-scene attention among foreground tokens. To support this guidance under large motion, SceneShifter extracts dynamic foreground masks from the self-attention layers and heads that best capture subject trajectories. We further introduce SceneShifterBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate scene-timing accuracy, foreground preservation, and visual quality under large motion, multiple subjects. Experiments show that SceneShifter achieves frame-level scene timing, strong subject preservation, and high visual quality, outperforming existing baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/JamesLong199/SceneShifter.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pretrained vision language action (VLA) models show strong zero-shot performance, but are rarely deployed directly and are often first post-trained on demonstrations from the target setting. Yet VLAs are typically compared by zero-shot or full-data fine-tuned success, revealing little about the data and compute required for adaptation. We study adaptation efficiency for six VLAs ($π_{0}$, $π_{0.5}$, and GR00T N1-N1.7) on four LIBERO suites, varying both demonstrations and training steps. This reveals differences hidden by full-data fine-tuning: models within two points on the full LIBERO-Spatial dataset differ by up to $3\times$ in success with 50 demonstrations. $π_{0.5}$ needs $2$--$5\times$ fewer demonstrations than $π_{0}$ and is the most sample-efficient model at every budget, with the largest gap on the long-horizon suite. Within GR00T, N1.5 adapts more efficiently than N1.6 and N1.7 on three of four suites, despite their stronger zero-shot performance. We study one pretraining factor behind these differences by freezing the VLM during finetuning: this severely degrades the $π$ models, whose VLM is jointly trained with the action expert during pretraining, but barely affects GR00T models. Finally, validation loss on held-out demonstrations tracks closed-loop success within a suite, but understates how much more sample-efficient $π_{0.5}$ is than $π_{0}$.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Objective: It is not known whether the input representation governs the quality of explanations of deep learning models for electrocardiogram analysis. Risk of dangerous ventricular repolarisation is judged from the QT-interval and the shape of the T-wave, features that are hard to assess visually and harder still to verify in a model's reasoning. We evaluate a pseudo-colour encoding filling the area beneath the QT interval, its colour sequence designed from clinical electrophysiology knowledge and mapped to a validated risk prediction model, the QT-nomogram. Methods: Two identically configured residual networks were trained on single-heartbeat images from a clinical trial, differing only in whether pseudo-colour encoding was applied, so that any difference is attributable to the chromatic channel alone. To externally validate the effect, both models were applied without fine-tuning or threshold tuning to a previously unseen epilepsy cohort from the UK-Biobank at two visits, and eight explanation methods compared on faithfulness, localisation and stability. Results: The pseudo-colour model achieved areas under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.903 and 0.914 at the first and follow-up visits, against 0.719 and 0.720 for the monochrome model, recovering three to five times as many at-risk heartbeats at comparable specificity. Pseudo-colour encoding improved explanation localisation and stability for almost every method, and the improvement reproduced across both visits. Conclusion: The input representation is a controllable and previously overlooked determinant of both predictive performance and explanation quality. Significance: Treating interpretability as a representation-design problem offers a route towards explanations clinicians can check against known electrophysiology.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autoregressive video diffusion models produce large KV caches during multi-round video generation. Keeping all these KV chunks would consume substantial memory, while rebuilding discarded caches would significantly delay video chunk generation. This paper presents ReelCache, a KV cache compression system for efficient multi-round, long video generation. ReelCache combines an intra-chunk compressor with an inter-chunk cache manager. The compressor applies low-rank decomposition across channels and video coding to the resulting coefficients, predicting the decomposition rank per transformer layer online to meet retained-energy targets. The cache manager shares basis directions across consecutive chunks and organizes chunk states in a tree, allowing generation branches to share historical state while supporting restoration for resume, rewind, and fork. Experiments across five video generation models show that ReelCache reduces KV storage by 14.46-21.13x relative to baselines and accelerates next-chunk generation by 1.25-3.36x, with less than 1% drop in six representative VBench metrics. GitHub: https://github.com/Tianyu9748/ReelCache

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language models are widely used as synthetic respondents in surveys, behavioral experiments, and market research. Most of these respondents read text, whereas shoppers in a store choose by looking at shelves, which makes vision-language models (VLMs) natural synthetic shoppers. We test whether VLM shoppers follow real consumers at two levels. At the purchase level, VLM shoppers built from household records plan their trips from a store directory and select products from shelf images, and we compare their purchases with six public datasets from three countries. The synthetic baskets hit held-out purchases as often as the households' own past trips, and synthetic group differences correlate with real ones twice as strongly as those of a trained regression. Without fitting, VLM shoppers reproduce alcohol purchases by gender and age in Russia and trip sizes by store distance in Taiwan, and respond to situations, prices, and displays within measured ranges. At the level of a single shelf choice, a model's answer confounds the product with its position and letter label. We therefore introduce ShelfSight, shelf renderings that vary products, positions, labels, viewpoints, and prices independently, together with a factor model that separates product preferences from the effects of label and position, which we call answer effects. On ShelfSight, hosted VLMs read the shelf, follow stated needs, keep their product preferences across views, and respond to price as budget shoppers. These answer effects, a preference for the letter A and for positions in the image, are estimated once per image type and removed. VLMs are thus good synthetic shoppers whose corrected shelf choices reflect product preferences.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We introduce P-IMLA, a preconditioned implicit-midpoint Langevin sampler for high-dimensional log-concave targets with non-smooth regularization. The obstruction is that the large steps enabled by matrix preconditioning are precisely where preconditioned Euler-Maruyama inflates stationary variance. Implicit midpoint removes this surplus via a Cayley-transform identity, making P-IMLA Gaussian-exact at any step size. Beyond Gaussians, whitening transfers the sharp IMLA contraction rate to $κ_{\mathrm{eff}}$, and backward-error analysis gives drift-only rather than Euler-type diffusion bias under explicit smoothness assumptions. An $M$-norm Moreau envelope yields a proximal non-smooth variant with a three-term error budget. Controlled Gaussian and TV-regularized imaging experiments confirm the predicted separation: the unpreconditioned midpoint scheme retains long transients as $κ_f$ grows, whereas P-IMLA remains $κ_{\mathrm{eff}}$-governed and near variance-exact.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Inference-time steering in vision-language models often entangles query-relevant reasoning dynamics with low-level perceptual noise, causing collateral degradation on correct predictions. We propose Question-Contrastive Visual Steering (QCVS) to isolate task-specific intervention directions via double-differential activation probing. By contrasting spatial layout perturbations under the target query against a generic description reference, QCVS cancels task-agnostic visual artifacts without ground-truth supervision. A dual-threshold gating mechanism then evaluates relative interaction strength and signal amplitude, selectively applying a single bounded hidden-state update only when necessary. Empirical evaluations across multiple scales of Qwen3.5 (2B to 9B) and DeepSeek-VL2 on DynaMath and We-Math demonstrate that QCVS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy gains under sparse coverage (approximately 22%-30%). Crucially, QCVS yields repair rates up to 13.98% while suppressing collateral damage down to 0.41%, proving high intervention precision and computational efficiency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision Transformers (ViTs) perform strongly in face recognition (FR), yet their fixed-depth inference limits deployment in resource-constrained settings. We propose SD-ViT, a training framework that gives a usable exit at every block depth by making every depth produce discriminative embeddings, for budget-selected fixed-depth exits. Standard ViTs supervise intermediate layers only through backpropagation, so their intermediate representations are not directly optimized to form usable identity embeddings. SD-ViT adds a stochastic auxiliary signal: at each iteration a randomly selected intermediate block is passed through the same embedding head as the final layer and trained with a weighted margin-penalty softmax loss. The objective is thus stochastically sampled deep supervision through a shared classifier. Because both loss terms share that head and classifier, intermediate tokens are trained to map into the same embedding space as final-layer tokens. This matters for FR, where matching is by similarity: a probe processed at an early exit can be compared directly against full-depth gallery embeddings, with no gallery recomputation or adaptation layers. On established benchmarks, SD-ViT stays within 0.4 IJB-C points of the same backbone trained conventionally at full depth, while its early-exit probes recover over 96% of their own full-depth performance at 50% of the computational cost without modifying the reference gallery. A single verification threshold calibrated at full depth remains usable from quarter depth onward.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Chest radiograph report generation is hampered by retrieved reports that resemble the current image but are clinically inconsistent with it, and by diagnostic-label imbalance. We present RG-MVR, a retrieval-grounded framework comprising Retrieval-Augmented Evidence Grounding, which combines dense memory summaries with visually gated sparse retrieved evidence; Adaptive Multi-view Evidential Reasoning, which weights image, memory, and retrieved evidence per case; and a Clinical Prompt Learner, which models finding mention separately from positive, negative, or uncertain status. On MIMIC-CXR, RG-MVR achieved a clinical-label F1 of 0.483 and a BLEU-4 of 0.118 (IU X-ray: 0.202 and 0.110). In an exploratory external cohort of 22 examinations, clinical-label agreement declined markedly (F1, 0.071) and unsupported statements remained common, although physicians found fewer unsupported temporal comparisons (9 vs. 20 cases) and device-related statements (7 vs. 15) than with the baseline. Evidence-aware retrieval and structured diagnostic prompting may reduce specific reporting errors, but broader external validation is required.

Abstract (英文): Diffusion-based anomaly detectors reconstruct a test image towards normality, yet how much of an anomaly survives, its leak, remains uncharacterized. We show that a first-order deterministic sampler obeys an exact recursion along the anomaly, whose continuous limit is a survival law. The anomaly decays exponentially as the noise falls, at a rate set by the retention $r$, the share the model keeps on the states it visits. Because $r$ depends on the noise level and not on the step count, one measured path predicts the leak of other step counts, samplers and schedules from a nearby start to within a few hundredths, on four diffusion models and real defects. The law also shows that the leak's growth over steps is exposure bias and that normal details obey the same law, so changing the path or the training trades leak for detail error. We introduce SurvAD, which fine-tunes the reconstructor on inference-like starts and fits a comparator that adds evidence to the residual map without suppressing its ranking elsewhere. For under $2\%$ more inference time, SurvAD raises the multi-class pixel average precision (P-AP) of DeCo-Diff from $76.8$ to $80.7$ on MVTec AD and from $57.5$ to $60.0$ on VisA. It matches CostFilter-AD on the same reconstructor with $9\times$ fewer trainable parameters and, under Dinomaly's protocol, exceeds its reported P-AP on Dinomaly on both benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The segmentation of brain tumors from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is an essential step for computer-aided neuro-oncology, treatment planning and follow-up. While effective encoder-decoder representations are achieved by U-Net and Attention U-Net, the skip sections of the network can generate weak-contrast or noisy responses in the case of heterogeneous boundaries of the tumor. In this paper, we introduce a Quality-Governed Deep Learning Network (QGDL-Net) that can be used for binary tumor segmentation from FLAIR MRI slices. The proposed model is able to predict the slice quality based on contrast and SNR statistics and to fine-tune the encoder features prior to decoding by learnable quality gates. We used the 80:20 train-validation split for experiments with BraTS 2021. QGDL-Net achieved a Dice of 0.9747, IoU of 0.9506, precision of 0.9755, recall of 0.9739, F1 of 0.9747 and accuracy of 0.9982, outperforming U-Net and Attention U-Net. The EigenCAM maps and segmentation overlays provide better tumor localization and interpretable activation around the lesion regions.

Abstract (英文): Expert annotation in digital pathology is expensive, and low-label regimes remain common in realistic histopathology workflows. Self-supervised learning (SSL) offers a practical way to learn transferable representations from unlabeled pathology images, but performance-only evaluation can hide unreliable confidence behavior under domain shift. We present a reliability-aware benchmark of SimCLR, BYOL, and an MAE-inspired masked-reconstruction baseline on PatchCamelyon (PCam; 96x96) and PANDA (prostate cancer) under 1%, 5%, and 10% labeled regimes with frozen-encoder linear probing. To address common single-run concerns, ResNet-18 results are repeated over 3 splits x 3 seeds (n=9) and ViT-B/16 over 1 split x 3 seeds (n=3) under practical compute constraints, with mean+/-std reporting and per-run logs. Beyond AUROC, accuracy, and F1-score, we report Expected Calibration Error and Brier score, OOD confidence diagnostics for cross-dataset PCam<->PANDA scoring, federated-vs-centralized SimCLR comparisons on PCam, and cross-dataset transfer to colorectal histology tiles. SSL improves label efficiency on the more controlled PCam task, while PANDA's noisier patch-level proxy can remain near coin-flip with higher variance. The study highlights that histopathology SSL should be assessed not only by ranking performance but also by calibration, robustness, and transparent reproducibility diagnostics before clinical interpretation.

Abstract (英文): Few-shot fine-grained video action recognition requires recognizing novel action classes from only a few labeled examples. The difficulty is that visually similar actions often share actors, scenes, poses, and objects, differing only in the order, timing, and short duration of motion changes. We study this problem under three practical constraints: few tunable parameters, low compute cost, and use of the video modality alone. We propose Motion-aligned Temporal Adapter (MATA), a parameter-efficient adapter on top of a frozen CLIP backbone. MATA specializes CLIP features along the temporal dimension using two components: temporal phase alignment through learnable temporal positional embeddings, and a local motion transition operator through depth-wise convolution. With only ~3.6M tunable parameters, MATA achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple fine-grained benchmarks while remaining robust to both visual corruptions and temporal disruptions induced by frame dropping. Code is available at https://github.com/prakashchhipa/MATA-Motion-aligned-Temporal-Adapter

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing visual question answering (VQA) requires locating fine-grained evidence in large scenes under limited visual input budgets. Active observation addresses this challenge through selective exploration, but repeated generative judgments can make region search costly. Its decisions also concern distinct prediction targets: spatial pointer validity, evidence presence, and reasoning correctness. We propose EarthJev, a framework that separates region scoring from answer generation and uses target-specific decision signals while keeping the answering model frozen. A spatial pointer verifier uses visual and geometric cues to guide exploration or bypass search. An evidence-aware decision mechanism scores compact candidate views in batches using a lightweight head on frozen representations, without generating textual region judgments. Format-specific confidence calibration then guides selection between candidate answers generated from memory and overview presentations of the acquired evidence. Across five benchmarks, EarthJev achieves a mean accuracy of 53.06% with Qwen3.5-9B, outperforming ZoomSearch with the same answering model by 2.33 to 14.73 percentage points per benchmark while reducing mean latency from 11.30 - 1.67 seconds per question. With four additional frozen answering models, model-specific fitting of the decision modules and calibration mappings yields mean accuracy gains of 3.41 - 6.53 percentage points over the corresponding single-pass baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/Da1daidaidai/EarthJev

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many deep learning architectures for computer vision tasks rely on features extracted by a pre-trained encoder on large databases of natural 2D RGB images. However, the wide domain gap with medical images hampers the direct transfer of optimized deep learning models from computer vision to clinical applications. Recently, self-supervised pre-training and foundation models emerged as promising alternatives to mitigate these limitations. Yet, constructing such models for medical imaging faces critical challenges, including designing and adapting training strategies for large 3D volume datasets, and the need for standardized benchmarks to evaluate feature extraction performance. In this study, we propose an extensive comparison of vision encoders with a focus on 3D brain MRI. First, we consider 8 fully and self-supervised models pretrained on natural images (e.g. ResNet, DINO, CLIP) and 10 backbone architectures trained on medical images, either based on supervised (e.g. RadImageNet) or self-supervised (e.g. BiomedCLIP, SimCLR) tasks. All pre-trained encoders are evaluated on various downstream tasks to assess the representation power of their latent representations, including age and sex prediction as well as disease classification on the ADNI and PPMI datasets. Our study shows that 2D encoders pre-trained on natural images perform the most favorably, even compared to models pre-trained on more than 100k brain MRI volumes, highlighting the need for more suitable and robust methods for representation learning in 3D medical imaging. The source code for this study is publicly available at: https: //github.com/PierreFalconnier/brain-representation-benchmark.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative world models and 3D Gaussian Splatting promise to turn ordinary video into explorable 3D scenes, but little is reported on how they behave on uncurated, in-the-wild footage. We study what these models recover when the only input is a publicly available news walkthrough of a food bank warehouse, with presenters walking through every shot and no drawings, scans or site visit, and we use the result as the geometry of a digital twin. A commercial world model given 20 frames recovered camera poses and metric depth for three areas, which we fused into about 747,000 Gaussian splats for 200 credits (20 US cents). Splat models optimized locally on a CPU, at no credit cost, were photorealistic near the filmed path but degraded quickly away from it. On held-out frames, scored in pixels not covered by people, they reached 21.2 and 23.1 dB PSNR from 64 and 43 training frames, and lost 3.8 and 5.4 dB when given only the 8 frames a world model accepts. Removing the trainer's background fill and person-hull carving raised held-out PSNR, showing that cleaning the space behind moving people costs fidelity in observed pixels while its gain lies where no ground truth exists. Asking the world model to generate views of areas the video never showed failed on the provider's side twice, so extrapolation could not be evaluated and unseen space was filled by a scripted model instead. We also describe the corrections needed to turn world-model output into a valid, metric Universal Scene Description (OpenUSD) asset with its native splat schema, which serves as the only interface to a data layer, a browser viewer, Blender and NVIDIA Omniverse. The findings suggest that today's models recover the observed parts of a real scene cheaply and at metric scale, while few-view fidelity and extrapolating unobserved space remain open. The work is an exploration of these methods, not a deployed product.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We introduce nanopath, an open-source framework and benchmark for rapidly training and evaluating small-scale pathology foundation models. Using a single H100 GPU, a typical nanopath model trains in a little over an hour on up to one million TCGA pathology tile presentations and evaluates within 25 minutes on a broad suite of tile-level and slide-level tasks. Nanopath enables rapid exploration of research ideas at small scale, so promising training recipes can subsequently be tested with more data and compute. To enable fair comparisons between ideas, we constrain submissions to fixed data and compute budgets while keeping the downstream evaluation protocol fixed. Anyone may clone the codebase, automatically download the data, reproduce or modify existing models, and submit a run to Labless, our public experiment ledger where researchers and AI agents can search through prior attempts. Since its public release in June 2026, nanopath has received 1,269 experiments from 20 contributors. Here, we detail its design and assess how well its rapid evaluation tracks standardized THUNDER, HEST, and PathoBench (CPTAC) benchmarks across six nanopath and 14 reference foundation models. Nanopath's fast evaluation suite strongly tracked these standardized benchmarks, and a few 22M parameter nanopath models even outperformed giant 1.1B parameter models on a subset of downstream tasks. We subsequently discuss how we have since optimized nanopath's evaluation suite to be an even more faithful, quick proxy for external benchmarking. Nanopath provides an accessible testbed for students, researchers, and AI agents to explore pathology foundation modeling through fair, transparent, and directly comparable experiments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The detection of previously unidentified Child Sexual Abuse Material (CSAM) remains a significant challenge for law enforcement authorities, online service providers, and child protection organizations, as traditional hash-matching technologies are primarily designed to identify previously verified material. This paper reviews and classifies existing AI-based technologies that could complement hash-based approaches and support the detection, analysis, and prioritization of potentially harmful content. The reviewed approaches are organized into three categories: real-time image and video analysis, image and video classification and tagging, and AI-based behavioral monitoring. A comparative analysis examines the capabilities, limitations, and potential integration of general-purpose computer vision platforms, content moderation services, and specialized CSAM detection tools within existing detection workflows. An experimental evaluation was conducted using Google Cloud Vision AI and Sightengine to investigate sexual-content classification and child-adult age estimation. The results highlight differences in classification taxonomies and outputs across providers, as well as significant limitations in age classification, particularly for adolescents. These findings highlight the importance of using AI-based assessments as complementary tools for analysis and prioritization, rather than as definitive determination of CSAM. Finally, the paper examines the relevant European Union regulatory framework, with particular attention to the EU AI Act, the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), fundamental rights, transparency, accountability, and human oversight. The legal analysis highlights how regulatory obligations and safeguards may differ depending on the deployment context, potentially creating inconsistencies across interconnected detection and reporting workflows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mamba has the capability to capture global context in linear time for 3D medical image segmentation. However, existing methods employ Mamba with full-width directional scans at every resolution, keeping the model large (64-138M parameters and high FLOPs). We propose Grouped Multi-Directional (GMD) Mamba, a lightweight network based on the GMD scan that divides input channels $C$ into groups $G$ and flattens each group in different directions in one pass. This gives multi-directional context at $O(C^2/G)$ instead of $O(K \cdot C^2)$, where $K$ is the number of scan directions. Additionally, we employ Mamba only at the deepest encoder stages, resulting in 19.2M parameters. On BraTS2023, it achieves the best average Dice of 92.0% and the lowest HD95 of 2.7 at $\sim$1/7 the parameters of SegMamba-V2. For model validation, we also ran experiments on the MSD Spleen dataset, achieving competitive performance. The implementation details and code are publicly available at https://github.com/2802990u/GMD-Mamba.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mamba is a neural network architecture that reduces computational complexity, and has been shown to be effective for 3D medical image segmentation. However, existing models still remain computationally expensive. We propose compressing Mamba by distillation while achieving competitive performance. Specifically, we demonstrate compression of a 139M-parameter SegMamba-V2 teacher into a lightweight TinyUNet3D student with about 0.96M parameters, achieving about 145$\times$ compression with a performance drop of just over one Dice point. We further investigate the utility of knowledge distillation in a range of data-labeling scenarios under identical experimental settings, and show that its advantage is most prominent when labeled data is scarce. To demonstrate the generalizability of our approach, experiments are conducted on three datasets: BraTS 2023, MSD Spleen and MSD Hippocampus. Abstract (英文): Evaluation of 3D medical foundation models is typically dominated by downstream task scores, which entangle representation quality with adaptation choices. This is especially critical in volumetric imaging, where spacing and anisotropy can substantially alter image statistics while the anatomy itself remains unchanged. We therefore introduce \textbf{REFORM-3D}, a \textbf{R}epresentation-centric \textbf{E}valuation \textbf{F}ramew\textbf{OR}k for benchmarking frozen 3D medical vision foundation \textbf{M}odels across three questions: Acquisition Robustness, asking whether features remain stable when spacing varies while anatomy is fixed; Cross-Modal Anatomical Alignment, asking whether labeled anatomical structures remain recoverable across modalities under balanced bidirectional retrieval; and Anatomical Transfer, asking whether organ identity remains recoverable from limited labeled support under case-disjoint evaluation. These are also pretraining questions, because what a 3D encoder learns depends on how volumes are geometrically presented during self-supervised pretraining. We further introduce Med3DINO encoder family, pretrained on $62.6$K unlabeled MR, CT, PET, CTA, and CBCT volumes under three explicit geometric assumptions: isotropic normalization, voxel-relative sampling, and spacing-aware resampling. Across Med3DINO and heterogeneous external encoders, REFORM-3D reveals module-specific strengths rather than a single model ordering. Within the controlled Med3DINO comparison, voxel-relative sampling yields lower embedding drift, whereas spacing-aware resampling favors anatomical transfer; semantic-transfer advantages depend on the dataset and feature type. Project link: \href{https://endarzboy.github.io/reform3d-website/}{\highlightLR{REFORM-3D}}.

Abstract (英文): Text-guided image editing requires realizing the requested semantic change while preserving content unrelated to the instruction. FlowEdit provides an efficient inversion-free solution, but its source-anchored coupling can become overly restrictive, causing zero-edit, where the output remains close to the source and the requested change fails to emerge. We find that source coupling directly governs the preservation--editing trade-off, while a fixed coupling schedule cannot adapt to timestep-specific editing needs. Motivated by this observation, we propose V2C-Edit, a training-free VLM-steered velocity control framework. V2C-Edit constructs complementary coupling-controlled velocities and dynamically selects among them using Instruction-Level Similarity (ILS), which evaluates whether the predicted transformation follows the editing instruction. Adaptive spatial masking further preserves regions unrelated to the edit. Experiments on MagicBrush and PIE-Bench with FLUX and Stable Diffusion 3 show that V2C-Edit consistently improves instruction alignment over FlowEdit while maintaining competitive structural preservation, and that ILS better reflects editing effectiveness than target-caption CLIP similarity. Our code is available at https://github.com/Kuanshen77/V2C-Edit.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image vectorization is often evaluated by rendering fidelity, which cannot distinguish documents that render identically but support different edits. Edit-based evaluation tests outcomes, but an oracle that gathers a part's fragments can conceal the cost of selecting them. We introduce Pass@k, which tests whether a prescribed edit can succeed using objects selectable within k clicks under a specified interaction model. Eight document diagnostics characterize properties relevant to editing beyond these tasks. We assess structural sensitivity through controlled perturbations, practical relevance through designer operations, and reference dependence through alternative drawings. We evaluate twelve open and commercial methods on 211 references, with 635 edit tasks on 93 of them. In each of four reference sets, including two drawn by designers, 64-88% of oracle-passed tasks on parts composed of multiple shapes fail at Pass@1, against 6-40% for single shapes, pooling counts over configurations. Higher fidelity does not guarantee higher Pass@1, while selected diagnostics are associated with designers' effort in our study.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Promptable segmenters of the SAM family turn a click or a phrase into an instance mask, so the costly part of a label is now its class. However, each annotation project draws its own class boundary, often through objects these models already know: people from cars, sedans from trucks, one sedan model from another. A generic model's boundary is fixed by its training vocabulary. We present SNAP, an annotation pipeline that learns the project's boundary from the labels the annotator draws anyway. On one frozen SAM2.1 pass per image, we fit a per-project head, SnapHead, that classifies every proposal and refits in seconds after each labeled image. Where SAM 3 is available, the same labels decide class by class whether to keep its boundary or ours. Given the true masks of eight look-alike cell lines, SAM 3 assigns classes at Cohen's kappa = 0.038 and SnapHead at 0.754 (60 labeled images), and when only the class names change, SAM 3 falls to chance and SnapHead's score is unchanged. End to end, with 20 labeled images, SNAP beats a SAM 3 oracle tuned on the evaluation labels on five microscopy projects (0.484 against 0.441 F1), and run unchanged on five held-out datasets, it beats SAM 3 as shipped on four. Our results suggest that frozen foundation features already encode the class boundaries a generic vocabulary misses, and that a project's own labels are enough to read them out.

</details>

We introduce SPECTRA (Scanner and Pathology Embedding Contrastive Tuning for Robust Alignment), a model-agnostic contrastive learning post-training framework which explicitly encourages scanner- and stain-robust representations, to optimize performance and generalization on downstream applications. Using parameter-efficient LoRA adapters trained on matched multi-scanner and multi-stain whole-slide images sourced from the PLISM dataset, our method aligns spatially corresponding regions across acquisition conditions (scanner, stain), using other locations within each candidate condition as negatives. SPECTRA improved PathoROB robustness index, gene-expression prediction on HEST, slide-level classification AUC on CPTAC, and held-out PLISM retrieval for every model. Overall tile-level classification on THUNDER also improved, with exceptions for segmentation and calibration tasks. Abstract (英文): Facial Expression Recognition (FER) traditionally relies on raw RGB images, introducing privacy risks, sensitivity to illumination variations, and susceptibility to identity-confounding visual shortcuts. As an alternative, this work proposes a lightweight, privacy-aware pipeline using exclusively geometric representations-facial landmarks (478 points) and blendshapes (52 coefficients, ARKit standard)-eliminating persistent raw video storage. Six neural architectures trained from scratch on fused CREMA-D and RAVDESS datasets were benchmarked against an ImageNet-pretrained ResNet50 baseline under a 5-fold subject-independent cross-validation protocol. Geometric models consistently outperformed the pixel baseline on unseen subjects with 12-124 times fewer parameters, while demonstrating superior robustness on subtle emotional expressions such as sadness. Ablation studies reveal that blendshapes capture the primary discriminative signal, while landmarks provide essential complementary spatial topology. These results demonstrate that compact geometric representations preserve affective semantics, eliminate downstream transfer learning dependency on visual backbones, and enable efficient, privacy-aware emotion recognition.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Latent diffusion models paired with a variational auto-encoder (VAE) tokenizer have shown promising performance for efficient text-driven human motion generation. However, motion latent diffusion suffers from a generation-reconstruction trade-off: simply increasing tokenizer capacity enhances reconstruction but fails to proportionally improve text-conditioned generation quality. We assume this trade-off stems from a spectral mismatch inside the VAE tokenizer: the encoder yields a latent space biased toward high frequencies, and the decoder introduces temporal artifacts. In this paper, we propose the Diffusable Motion Tokenizer (DiMoT) to improve the diffusability of the tokenizer, rather than scaling up the diffusion backbone. To improve text-conditioned optimization, Critic Spectral Adaptation (CSA) leverages a pretrained diffusion model as the critic to suppress high-frequency latent energy. To preserve motion dynamics, the Alpha-Flow Motion Decoder (AMD) performs generative decoding via average-velocity prediction, enabling efficient one-step decoding. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiMoT achieves state-of-the-art performance across various challenging text-to-motion benchmarks, yielding superior motion fidelity and text-motion alignment. Project page: https://githubhgh.github.io/dimot/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Chest X-ray (CXR) interpretation remains challenging because subtle pathology can be obscured by overlapping anatomy, image-quality variation, and limited visual evidence in isolated radiographs. Although image-only deep learning models provide useful baselines, they discard the complementary diagnostic context available in radiology reports, while large vision-language models can be computationally expensive for practical deployment. This paper presents CXR-CMFA, a lightweight cross-modal feature aggregation network for binary CXR pathology classification. The proposed framework combines a dual-stream visual encoder, using EfficientNet-B0 for global anatomical representation and DenseNet-121 for local pathological detail, with a TF-IDF-MLP report encoder. Two fusion modules integrate these modalities: the Dual-Modal Spatial Attention Module (DMSAM) uses the report embedding to gate global and local visual features, and the Cross-Modal Gated Feature Fusion (CGFF) module learns per-instance weights for visual and textual contributions. Experiments on the MIMIC-CXR benchmark, using the provided train, validation, and test splits, show that CXR-CMFA achieves 93.45% accuracy, 99.88% precision, 91.8% recall, 95.7% F1-score, and 99.19% AUC on the held-out test set. The model produces only four false positives among 984 normal test cases, supporting its suitability for high-specificity screening workflows. An ablation study further demonstrates a 22.47 percentage-point AUC improvement over the global-image-only variant, confirming the value of adaptive cross-modal fusion. These results indicate that compact report-conditioned fusion can provide strong discriminative performance without relying on large pretrained language models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recognizing anatomical structures during minimally invasive surgery (MIS) could support intraoperative navigation and improve patient safety, but progress is limited by small, single-procedure datasets and by segmentation methods developed for natural scenes. This work addresses both limitations through a new dataset and model. First, we introduce ATLAS-120k, a clip-level semantic segmentation dataset of 121,018 annotated frames from 100 surgical videos, covering 14 laparoscopic and robot-assisted procedures and 41 classes. Annotations were created with a pipeline that combines manual labeling, tracker-based propagation, and review by surgeons. Second, we propose ATLAS (Anatomy Recognition with Context Learning using Foundation Representations), a video semantic segmentation model for surgical anatomy recognition. ATLAS builds on in-domain foundation-model embeddings and adds context queries for the procedure and surgical phase, together with query propagation between frames for short-term memory. ATLAS outperforms state-of-the-art natural-image and in-domain foundation models in both segmentation accuracy and temporal consistency, achieving a Dice score of 0.70 with ViT-L while running in real time at 76.2 FPS. Ablations show that the context queries improve accuracy and that query propagation improves temporal consistency. ATLAS also generalizes well, matching a dedicated model zero-shot on CholecSeg8k and improving phase recognition on AutoLaparo over SurgeNet initialization. The model weights, dataset annotations, and annotation tool are publicly available at https://github.com/TimJaspers0801/ATLAS.

Abstract (英文): Interactive VR/MR applications demand photorealistic 3D representations that support real-time rendering and object-level manipulation. While 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) achieves high rendering quality, it represents scenes as unstructured, monolithic primitives. Existing object-aware 3DGS methods jointly optimize geometry and semantic states but suffer from two key failure modes: noisy foundation-model masks on complex geometry, and label-geometry coupling, in which co-optimized identity gradients mislabel boundary Gaussians. We introduce RigidGS, an end-to-end pipeline that decouples geometric learning from object identity. We propose a depth- and temporally-aware mask refinement module that addresses two complementary SAM2 failures: over-inclusion on thin structures and under-inclusion on multimodal-depth objects. We then determine object identities entirely upstream via COLMAP projection voting and freeze them during training, directing mask supervision solely toward geometric optimization. Refined masks alone substantially improve competing methods, yet a residual gap in label-geometry coupling persists that our fixed-identity design eliminates. RigidGS outperforms prior object-aware 3DGS methods on LLFF/NVOS, Mip-NeRF~360, and iNeRF360, improving segmentation and boundary IoU by 7.7 and 14.5 points on LLFF/NVOS. Finally, RigidGS enables real-time rigid-body manipulation on Meta Quest~2 without mesh extraction. Our code and project page are available at https://gyreyirisansah.github.io/rigidgs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unpaired medical image-to-image translation requires synthesizing target-modality images from source-modality images without aligned supervision. Existing methods learn a direct source-to-target mapping that entangles structure preservation and appearance generation within a single model, often distorting clinically meaningful anatomy in the translated output. We argue that these two objectives should be decoupled entirely: anatomical structure should be captured independently of generation, and the generator should operate only on a frozen structural representation. To this end, we propose a two-stage framework for unpaired medical image translation. In the first stage, we learn a shared structure space by training a structure encoder-decoder on source images, target images, and structural masks jointly, using mask reconstruction to anchor the latent space to modality-agnostic anatomy. For source images without mask annotations, a target-domain memory bank provides soft structural supervision via prototype retrieval. In the second stage, a conditional flow model learns to render target-domain appearance under fixed structural conditions. This ensures the generator inherits target appearance statistics and can focus entirely on structural conditioning rather than learning appearance from scratch. We validate our framework on OCT-to-OCTA synthesis and brain MR-to-CT translation. Our method outperforms all unpaired baselines on both tasks and, despite requiring no paired supervision, also surpasses paired baselines, improving PSNR by up to 11.2% and SSIM by up to 26.4% on OCT-to-OCTA, and achieving consistent gains across whole-image, soft-tissue, and bone regions on MR-to-CT. The project repository is available on GitHub.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Computer vision (CV) for policing has centred on recognising objects such as seized weapons, while body-worn video (BWV) is still reviewed by people, with existing AI tools using only its audio. Multimodal video AI could make BWV review faster and cheaper. At the object level, we present the KnifeHunter dataset, developed with UK law enforcement, with over 25,000 images of 543 knife classes and a protocol reflecting investigative conditions, and KHNet, a retrieval architecture fusing local and global features, which with about 125,000 distractor images attains 85.7% and 63.4% mean Average Precision (mAP) on the medium and hard protocols. At the event level, we present an evaluation protocol for video understanding pipelines, which describe a video as events and a transcript, that scores transcription, speaker attribution, event detection, temporal localisation and unsupported events. Whether each event is supported by the recording is labelled by a judge validated against blinded human labels. We apply it to complete pipelines, three 2026 omni-modal models and our hybrid NarrativeBridge-PAL-GPT-5 pipeline, on UKBWV-27, a pilot stress-test set of 27 publicly released UK BWV recordings (118 minutes). On this set the hybrid pipeline leads on five of six measures, yet it leaves 13.6% of predicted events unsupported and misattributes 27.7% of utterances, and every pipeline degrades on recordings from edited releases, long recordings and noisy audio. We propose readiness levels separating assistive tasks from those requiring verification of every output.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deepfake videos generated by advanced generative models pose serious threats to digital security, misinformation control, and media forensics. Although numerous detection methods have been proposed, their reported performances are often based on heterogeneous preprocessing pipelines, inconsistent data splits, and model-specific training protocols, hindering fair architectural comparison. This work presents a controlled and reproducible comparative study of convolutional neural networks (ResNet-18 and EfficientNet-B0) and a transformer-based architecture (ViT-B/16) within a unified, face-centric deepfake detection framework. All models are trained using identical preprocessing, augmentation, optimization settings, and data splits on Celeb-DF v2 and FaceForensics++, and results are validated across three independent random seeds with bootstrapped confidence intervals. Experimental results show that while CNN-based models perform competitively at the frame level, their performance degrades after video-level aggregation. In contrast, ViT-B/16 demonstrates more stable video-level performance, achieving 92.8\% [95\% CI: 91.7--94.0] video-level accuracy and an AUC of 0.950 [95\% CI: 0.934--0.964] on Celeb-DF v2 (3-seed bootstrapped). Cross-dataset evaluation across the same three random seeds reveals a large, asymmetric generalization gap (AUC 0.79 FF++$\rightarrow$Celeb-DF vs. 0.64 Celeb-DF$\rightarrow$FF++), indicating that in-domain video-level stability does not transfer to unseen manipulation domains. These findings indicate that global self-attention improves robustness and video-level stability for deepfake detection within a given data domain, while highlighting the need for further work on cross-domain generalization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accelerated magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) forms images from incomplete Fourier measurements, shifting acquisition burden to computational reconstruction. Deep unrolled networks interleave learned regularization and data consistency, yet typically estimate the full unmeasured spectrum at every cascade. We propose STAIRS (Staging the Inverse Problem via Admissible-Support Scheduling), which projects intermediate reconstructions onto nested Fourier supports that expand to the full spectrum and conditions each update on its support transition. Conditional increments of a regularized log-determinant score of training covariance define a normalized spectral coordinate, accounting for dependencies between newly and previously admitted frequencies. Dyadic targets allocate larger coordinate increments to earlier cascades and are mapped to fixed supports before training, enabling coarse-to-fine reconstruction with unchanged sampling masks and data consistency. On fastMRI and Stanford2D, STAIRS improves matched Transformer (HUMUSNet) and convolutional (NAFNet) backbones in single- and multi-coil reconstruction, including radiologist-annotated pathology regions. It also outperforms predictor-based progression with lower parameter overhead and inference latency. Stage-wise analysis shows repeated error correction by projection and lower intermediate reconstruction errors than the backbones alone throughout support expansion. Ablations favor dyadic allocation over uniform expansion for square and line-band supports and over manual front-loading for square windows. Support projection alone improves reconstruction, and transition conditioning strengthens the gain. Cross-dataset evaluation shows improvements for both backbones in both directions. Support allocation thus complements regularizer design in unrolled MRI reconstruction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Domain generalization (DG) for object detection aims to improve model performance on unseen target domains. Existing detectors, however, suffer substantial degradation on visually distinct unseen domains, as models confined to limited training styles tend to overfit superficial statistical cues rather than object-level semantics. Motivated by human perception, which attends primarily to semantically relevant features while ignoring irrelevant style cues, we propose Origin-Anchored Multi-Style Consistency (OAMSC), a source-only framework that couples multi-style data synthesis with cross-style consistency learning to suppress style-sensitive shortcut learning. For data preparation, we leverage off-the-shelf diffusion models to expand the source datasets into 20 distinct styles, with all generated images manually verified to ensure that object instances and annotations remain consistent across style variants. During training, we impose Origin-anchored consistency constraints at scene and object levels to reinforce the learning of domain-stable features while suppressing the influence of style-sensitive cues. Extensive experiments and ablation studies on multiple challenging DG benchmarks demonstrate the consistent effectiveness and strong generalization performance of OAMSC across diverse domain shifts. Notably, our approach applies to both one-stage and two-stage detectors, while its data-centric design is complementary in principle to other DG methods. The training code and two style-transfer datasets constructed for the experiments, comprising approximately 180K images in total, are publicly available on GitHub.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A camera becomes an absolute pose sensor when its images can be relocalized against a prebuilt map of the scene. On mobile robots and augmented-reality devices, that map must be small and quick to build. Scene coordinate regression (SCR) stores the scene in a compact network that predicts a 3D coordinate for every pixel, and mixture-of-experts (MoE) variants extend its capacity to larger outdoor scenes. In existing MoE-based SCR, however, the router assigns each pixel to an expert from the same local descriptor that the experts regress from, so repeated facades and windows at different locations receive similar routing inputs. We present DAC-MACE, which conditions soft expert routing on recalibrated and contextualized features. A residual context adapter (RCA) reweights the channels and locations of frozen encoder features, a global context encoder (GCE) mixes them with multi-scale self-attention, and an image-level pooled descriptor is added to the router input. A dual-threshold RANSAC--Levenberg--Marquardt estimator recovers the pose. On Cambridge Landmarks, DAC-MACE attains a mean per-scene median translation error of 13.0 cm over three seeds, the lowest among the RGB-only SCR methods compared (GLACE 13.6 cm, MACE 14.4 cm), and reduces the error on the largest scene from 24 to 19 cm relative to MACE. In a cumulative study, the MoE head, GCE, RCA, and the dual-threshold estimator lower the mean error from 17.0 cm to 14.4, 14.0, 13.6, and 13.0 cm. On 7-Scenes, adding GCE to a single ACE head raises Recall@(5 cm, 5$^\circ$) from 86.56% to 87.60%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models (VLMs) in medical retrieval-augmented generation must be able to say when a retrieved report contradicts the X-ray, so understanding whether they can detect such conflicts is important for safe deployment. The sycophancy mask hypothesis holds that these models internally detect conflicts but refuse to report them, concealing a detected contradiction. We introduce a three-part diagnostic that separates concealment from uncertainty: representation probing, activation steering, and two-way forced choice. Across four instruction-tuned VLMs on chest X-rays, we confirm behavioral suppression of contradiction and find that a linear probe separates matched from mismatched inputs, beyond what a simple CLIP model achieves. Steering the learned conflict direction never elicits contradiction, and forcing a binary choice does not recover one; instead, cross-modal attention to the image drops on mismatched pairs. We conclude that these models fail to contradict because the mismatch registers as hedged, gated uncertainty rather than concealed knowledge, calling for re-grounding and uncertainty surfacing. Code is available at https://github.com/mohamed-eldagla/Uncertainty-Not-Concealment/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Due to supply constraints, replacing damaged automotive components can be expensive and time-consuming, while replacing the entire component can be wasteful when much of it remains usable. This paper presents a pipeline to investigate whether missing geometry can be reconstructed as a repair patch using paired observations of the intact models and synthetically damaged components. Noise-free color, depth, and silhouette images were produced using the camera viewpoints. Depth differences were then used to identify the potential missing regions, while multi-view voxel reconstruction was used to estimate the missing portion. A pretrained DINOv2 model was also added to validate missing candidates and determine whether pretrained visual features could improve reconstruction. We tested the pipeline on 20 automotive CAD models, each with a synthetically removed portion. Each model was tested under four different conditions using two reconstruction methods for a total of 160 trials. The predictions were compared with the missing geometry of the unchanged ground-truth using predefined thresholds of the voxel intersection-over-union over 0.85 and the absolute relative volume error less than 15%. Of the 160 experimental trials, 109 of them satisfied both thresholds. Geometry-only reconstruction passed 16 of 20 components with eight views but only 10 of 20 with four adjacent views. Further analysis showed that 24 out of the 37 failed reconstructions could be categorized as unsuccessful because extra material was incorrectly added to areas that should have been empty. The implementation of DINOv2 into the pipeline did not improve the overall reconstruction accuracy and made prediction generation slower. For 13 verified tests, printing only the repaired sections instead of the entire parts reduced filament use by 93.08% and printing time by 89.39% based on the median results.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-Vocabulary Multi-Label Recognition (OV-MLR) aims to identify all queried semantic concepts present in an image. Existing methods mainly improve global image-text matching or text-side representations, leaving local prediction largely under-explored. In this paper, we revisit local prediction and reveal that it can serve as a strong discriminative branch once two properties are properly handled: spatial consistency of local visual features and compatibility-aware local competition. This perspective is motivated by local semantic sparsity: each image region is related to only a small subset of queried concepts, while compatible concepts of different granularities or types may still coexist locally. We instantiate this perspective with LoCo, a selective Local Competition framework for OV-MLR. LoCo adopts a spatially consistent visual encoder to reduce local feature entanglement, and introduces Selective Softmax to impose competition only among locally incompatible categories while preserving compatible responses. Multi-scale inference further improves local prediction at different scales. LoCo can also be ensembled with an existing global predictor to provide complementary image-level context. Experiments on four multi-label benchmarks show that standalone LoCo improves average F1-score and mAP over TagCLIP by 3.4% and 3.8%, respectively. LoCo + DualPrompt achieves the highest averages among the compared methods, with gains of 4.8% and 5.2% over TagCLIP. Our results suggest that local semantic sparsity offers a useful basis for developing more discriminative open-vocabulary multi-label recognition methods. The code is available at https://github.com/lee-plus-plus/LoCo.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Streaming video understanding (SVU) requires models to answer user queries over continuously evolving video streams. Unlike offline video QA, SVU must handle queries whose evidence may lie in the past, appear in the current scene, or emerge only after future observations. Existing streaming video systems often rely on compact visual memories or fixed evidence-access schemes, which can lose fine-grained details and fail to adapt the answering process to the temporal intent of each query. In this paper, we propose TimeTraveler, a streaming VQA framework that performs temporal strategy planning with a Time Dictionary. Inspired by the need to search across different temporal regions of a stream, TimeTraveler stores each observed moment as a timestamp-indexed structured caption and uses this dictionary as an explicit source of queryable evidence. Given a query, TimeTraveler determines whether to recall past evidence, read the present context, or wait for future observations, and then applies a strategy-specific evidence acquisition process. Comparative experiments on streaming video QA benchmarks show that TimeTraveler improves streaming question answering by preserving fine-grained temporal evidence and selecting the appropriate temporal strategy for each query. The project page is available at https://geppa.github.io/TimeTraveler.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show strong visual perception capabilities, but existing evaluations largely focus on single-view or multi-view settings with shared camera parameters, leaving the challenge of multi-agent collaborative scenarios underexplored. In such settings, multiple agents observe a shared environment from independent viewpoints, and reliable decisions require reasoning across these independent observations of the same environment. To study this problem, we introduce MaRiO, a benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaborative reasoning, and evaluate 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs on it. Our analysis reveals three key findings: (1) tasks governed by static perception are generally easier than tasks that require transformational event sequences; (2) most models struggle to resolve cross-agent correspondences, with errors increasing with scene complexity; and (3) models show limited ability to leverage geometric cues present in the scene. As an initial step toward addressing these challenges, we explore geometry-guided synthetic view augmentation that generates intermediate transition frames between agent observations, providing auxiliary spatial context that improves performance in certain categories. Overall, our results highlight that multi-agent collaborative reasoning remains an open challenge and an important direction for multimodal embodied systems. The code is available on the project page: https://omarftt.github.io/MaRiO-page/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pose awareness characterizes the extent to which a learned feature space aligns with the underlying object-pose manifold, such that observations with similar poses exhibit greater feature similarity than those with increasingly different poses. While such structure is desirable for pose-sensitive 3D vision tasks, visual foundation models pre-trained on individual 2D images typically lack explicit 3D or pose supervision. This raises a fundamental question: Do 2D visual foundation models exhibit pose awareness after large-scale pretraining? To answer this question, we introduce PoseBench, a systematic benchmark to probe pose awareness in pretrained visual features. Our evaluation follows a progressive design: held-out rendered views probe intrinsic pose-aware structure under controlled conditions, while real-image queries evaluate how robustly this structure remains accessible under real-world variations. We further introduce pose-graph-based refinement as a complementary probe of compatibility between feature neighborhoods and the underlying pose manifold. Across 31 publicly available checkpoints from 10 pretraining families, PoseBench reveals that pose awareness is an implicit but non-uniform property of 2D visual foundation models, and consistent gains from pose-graph refinement suggest that feature spaces retain structure compatible with the underlying pose manifold. PoseBench therefore provides a systematic basis for understanding and selecting pretrained features for pose-sensitive 3D vision tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal fusion requires not only exchanging information across modalities but also determining which representations should participate in the interaction and how complementary cross-modal views should be combined. We introduce Guided Co-Attention with Adaptive Gating, a fusion framework that integrates token-wise guidance, bidirectional cross-attention, and sample-specific fusion. The proposed method constructs two directional representations: an image-conditioned text representation, obtained by allowing textual tokens to query visual features, and a text-conditioned image representation formed in the reverse direction. Token-wise guidance modulates the representations before cross-attention, while an adaptive gate learns the relative contribution of the two resulting directional representations for each sample. We evaluate the approach using paired chest radiographs and radiology reports as an image-text test bed for binary (normal vs. abnormal) classification. The two cross-attention directions exhibit distinct performance characteristics, demonstrating that reversing the query and source modalities does not produce interchangeable representations. Ablation experiments further show that bidirectional interaction, adaptive gating, and token guidance provide complementary benefits. The proposed model achieves 96.05% accuracy, 96.10% F1-score, 98.37% AUC, 95.60% Sens@95Spec, and 96.51% Spec@95Sens, yielding the highest mean performance across all evaluated metrics. These findings support explicitly modeling interaction direction, token relevance, and sample-dependent integration when constructing multimodal representations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Domain adaptive point cloud completion (DA PCC) aims to bridge the geometric discrepancy between labeled source domains and unlabeled target domains for accurate 3D shape completion. Existing methods based on CNNs, Transformers, and Mamba-like sequence models still face challenges from limited receptive fields, high computational complexity, insufficient geometric bias, and serialization sensitivity. Recently, Receptance Weighted Key Value (RWKV) provides a linear-complexity alternative for long-range sequence modeling. However, adapting RWKV to DA PCC remains challenging due to the lack of explicit geometric priors, the mismatch between recurrent modeling and coarse-to-fine generation, and unstable target-domain adaptation. To address these challenges, we propose DAPointRWKV, a novel RWKV-based framework for DA PCC. DAPointRWKV consists of three components: Geometry-Aware RWKV (GA-RWKV) Backbone, RWKV-Oriented Progressive Decoder (RWKV-PD), and Dual Structural Regularization (DSR). Specifically, GA-RWKV introduces geometry-aware recurrent representation learning, RWKV-PD bridges RWKV token representations with coarse-to-fine reconstruction through local token-guided refinement, and DSR stabilizes target-domain transfer through visible-structure anchoring and complete-shape hypothesis stabilization. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that DAPointRWKV consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods.

</details>
