Abstract (英文): Pretrained encoders from self-supervised representation learning are increasingly applied to sonar imagery of the seafloor, where they are evaluated with protocols that take one embedding per target. A target patch of seabed, by contrast, is re-imaged from an alternate look on overlapping survey passes, so each embedding is one measurement of that target. Standard protocols cannot say how consistent those measurements are, or how much of their spread belongs to the patch and how much to the acquisition geometry. We treat this as a measurement-reliability problem. Using a synthetic-aperture sonar (SAS) survey, we measure the repeat-look reliability of embeddings from a frozen natural-image encoder (DINOv3 ViT-S/16). Standard single-look metrics, effective rank, clustering and a linear probe for coral, are all stable to the look, and the probe performs well (AUC 0.93). Yet the repeat-look reliability of the same embeddings is poor, with a distance-based intraclass correlation (dbICC) of 0.20 and nearest neighbours and clusters that barely agree across looks. This contradiction resolves as we find the probe learns a stable direction, whose ICC of 0.60 is 3.0 times that of the full embedding. Variance components, calculated from generalizability theory, attribute under a tenth of the within-cell spread to look acquisition geometry. The rest is cell-specific and cannot be separated without repeat passes at matched geometry. A sonar-pretrained encoder (BenthicDINO) and a classical SAS texture descriptor show the same pattern. For this dataset and the tested representations, uses that depend on patch identity fail, such as cross-pass comparison and retrieval, while uses at the level of habitat type (e.g. coral classification) do not. Because the standard metrics did not detect this, we argue that repeat-look reliability should accompany any evaluation of representations of the seafloor.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual generative models increasingly blur the line between synthesizing observations and simulating environments. Yet visual plausibility alone does not constitute successful world modeling: compelling outputs may lack persistent state, physical dynamics, or causally consistent responses to interactions. We define a generative world model as a predictive dynamical system that maintains a persistent, history-conditioned representation of an environment, updates that representation in response to actions or interventions, and generates temporally coherent trajectories that reflect outcomes under observed and counterfactual interventions. From this definition, we derive an intelligence-oriented framework spanning perceptual grounding, persistent state modeling, controllable transformation, dynamics-aware prediction, and closed-loop interaction. We present WorldFoundry, open-source infrastructure for reproducible inference and evaluation across video, 3D and 4D, embodied action, and interactive world systems. WorldFoundry unifies model and benchmark discovery, runtime and asset preparation, execution orchestration, artifact management, and interactive visualization, while modality-specific adapters preserve native inputs, execution semantics, output representations, and evaluation protocols. Explicit manifests, durable artifact contracts, and evidence-aware scorecards separate execution readiness from evaluative claims, making each claim traceable to its supporting artifacts. The catalog contains 270 model entries, 74 benchmarks, and 62 registered metric entries, with readiness and execution evidence recorded separately. WorldFoundry enables reproducible comparison of persistent, controllable dynamics across heterogeneous systems, advancing the field from perceptual synthesis toward measurable world intelligence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Camera rigs with identical measurement budgets can have different velocity estimation rates when exposure timing is uncertain. We establish this distinction for rolling-shutter reconstruction with two calibrated moving anchors, unknown scene points, and independently unknown auxiliary poses and clocks. On the same fixed local physical class near a separated lateral rig, repeated readouts have worst-case squared velocity risk at least of order $σρ_{\mathrm{eff}}$, whereas complementary readouts have minimax risk of order $σ^2$, for a fixed admissible clock displacement $ρ_{\mathrm{eff}}>0$ as the noise standard deviation $σ$ tends to zero. A finite physical compensation path gives the lower bound; a nonlinear velocity inverse proves complementary stability. All motion, structure, pose, and clock nuisances remain unknown. We identify mounting configurations in which both designs become regular and quantify the risk floor induced by metric uncertainty. We quantify full-parameter domains with independent 6-ms clocks and test in-domain reconstruction under paired noise and a depth-offset intervention. Raster studies examine image formation and calibration sensitivity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video fusion aims to integrate complementary cues from heterogeneous sources, producing visually informative videos with coherent spatio-temporal structures. Jointly learning cross-modal complementarity and temporal dependencies typically relies on large-scale, high-quality video datasets, whose storage and repeated use during optimization incur substantial costs, making data-efficient learning highly desirable. To address this challenge, we rethink video fusion from the perspective of dataset condensation and propose STAR-VF, the first framework that condenses spatio-temporal representations for data-efficient video fusion. STAR-VF condenses the original training set into a compact set of learnable spatio-temporal representations, which are decoded into synthetic video clips and optimized using feature-level supervision provided by a frozen proxy model. The resulting condensed data preserves fusion-relevant spatial and temporal cues without requiring ground-truth fused videos as supervision. To adapt fusion learning to condensed representations, we equip the final fusion model with flow-free inter-frame temporal modeling and query-driven intra-frame aggregation, enabling direct temporal interaction and adaptive selection of fusion-relevant information in representation space. Extensive experiments on infrared-visible, multi-exposure, multi-focus, and medical video fusion benchmarks demonstrate that STAR-VF, trained with only approximately 1/16 of the original temporal training set, achieves fusion quality comparable to methods trained on the full data. Further analyses validate the contributions of learned condensation and flow-free temporal modeling, and demonstrate the robustness of the condensed supervision across different proxy encoders and downstream fusion models. Project page: https://zju-xyc.github.io/STAR-VF-Project-Page/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent progress in vision foundation models (VFMs) has made automated open-vocabulary 3D bounding box annotation increasingly feasible by jointly leveraging LiDAR point clouds and images. However, partial observations remain a major challenge in sparse driving scenes, where visible LiDAR points often cover only a small fraction of the object's surface and provide insufficient cues for estimating its full spatial extent. As a result, existing methods often produce mislocalized boxes or leave objects unlabeled. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{\Ours{}}, an automated annotation framework that leverages image-generated 3D assets as amodal geometric priors for partially observed objects. By establishing correspondences between input images and rendered views of 3D assets, \Ours{} robustly aligns canonical amodal geometry with partially observed world-space LiDAR point clouds. To improve temporal consistency and reduce computational cost, \Ours{} generates a single asset per instance, estimates its metric scale once at a single frame and propagates it across frames for dynamic instances, and aggregates multi-frame observations for static instances. 3D object detectors trained on annotations from \Ours{} improve performance over strong baselines by 14.1 mAP on the nuScenes dataset and by 12.45 AP at IoU 0.5 for the vehicle class on the Waymo Open Dataset (WOD). Our project page is available at: \href{https://oliver0922.github.io/AmodalBox/}{https://oliver0922.github.io/AmodalBox/}

Abstract (英文): Parcel-scale land-use data is missing for most rapidly urbanizing Global South cities, as current segmentation architectures rely on multispectral imagery and planned layouts. We introduce a hand-annotated six-class land-use corpus covering 202 km$^2$ of Kathmandu Valley across 408 fine-resolution RGB scenes. The dataset is uniquely challenging: RGB spectral similarity is high, class imbalance approaches 100:1, and key structural features like rivers measure just two to three pixels wide at a standard hierarchical decoder's prediction stride. Evaluating five architectures reveals that standard random data splitting conceals massive spatial leakage. Switching to a strict sequence-block split drops performance by up to 0.095 mIoU. This penalty exceeds the performance gap between the architectures themselves. We modified the SegFormer baseline by integrating a half-resolution residual detail path, boundary-reliability reweighting, a topology-aware loss, and a presence-balanced sampler. Adding only 1.9% more parameters, the enhanced architecture improves mean IoU across every test fold, with gains concentrated in rare, thin-structure classes. However, both models achieve identical results on the external LoveDA benchmark. Finally, augmenting with diffusion-generated pairs at matched compute degraded accuracy across all architectures, especially harming rare classes it aimed to improve. This demonstrates that blindly incorporating synthetic data can severely damage performance, highlighting a critical need to rigorously evaluate and filter generated samples before training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image fingerprinting assigns each distributed copy a user-specific watermark for source tracing. Averaging collusion is a central threat: several recipients can average their differently marked copies to suppress each individual mark. Discrete bit-string watermarks are especially vulnerable because averaging drives bit evidence toward ambiguous decisions. We introduce \textbf{VectorMark}, which maps user keys to near-orthogonal high-dimensional unit vectors and identifies colluders by cosine similarity to the extracted watermark direction. Under ideal averaging of $K$ vector keys, the normalized average preserves a cosine signal of order $1/\sqrt{K}$ for each colluder, while unrelated keys remain concentrated near zero. The spherical geometry of random directions gives a shifted-Beta null distribution for cosine similarities, enabling analytic per-key false-positive-rate control after validation on unwatermarked images. VectorMark uses a neural watermark encoder and centered extractor trained with JND masking and a progressive curriculum for photometric, geometric, and collusion robustness. Experiments on MS-COCO, OpenImages, and AI-generated images show that VectorMark maintains robust watermark attribution under photometric and geometric distortions, and achieves the best strict all-colluder detection among evaluated methods, while preserving high visual quality. Our code is publicly available: https://github.com/nurlanov-zh/VectorMark

</details>

Abstract (英文): Traditional text-based person retrieval (TPR) assumes that a witness provides a complete description at once, whereas interactive person retrieval (Inter-PR) refines a partial description through question-answer exchanges. In this setting, the questioner inspects retrieved candidates to decide what evidence to request next, while the answerer sees only the witness-side memory and cannot report candidate-specific visual differences. Existing methods mainly learn offline question orderings, leaving this visual-feedback loop underexplored. To address this limitation, we introduce visual-feedback interactive person retrieval (VFI-PR), a new interactive retrieval paradigm in which retrieved candidates produce visual feedback that becomes evidence for subsequent questioning and re-ranking. To support training and evaluation in this setting, we construct VFI-PEDES, a dataset and evaluation protocol for visual-feedback interaction, and propose VFI-ReID, a framework that learns to use visual feedback during interactive person retrieval. Through the Dynamic Accumulated-evidence Retriever (DAR) and retrieval-aware question optimization, VFI-ReID adapts retrieval and question generation to evolving evidence states, turning visual feedback into complementary evidence for subsequent retrieval. Experiments show that VFI-ReID consistently improves retrieval across VFI-PR interaction stages, demonstrating the utility of visual feedback for interactive person retrieval and outperforming existing Inter-PR baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Industrial anomaly detection is challenged by the scarcity and diversity of real defects, leaving insufficient anomalous data to effectively train downstream models. Existing anomaly generation methods often require product-specific fine-tuning, target mask annotation, or costly procedures to achieve realistic and diverse defects. We propose REDiff-AD, a framework that requires neither model training nor target masks and introduces Rectified Flow MMDiT into industrial anomaly generation for efficient sampling with fewer generation steps. Given a normal product image and a single masked reference anomaly, REDiff-AD employs in-context learning to transfer the defect appearance from the reference into the normal image. Internal attention signals generated during this process are further reused to localize the synthesized anomaly, while edge and color cues refine the localization into pixel-level pseudo-masks. Two lightweight modules further improve generation quality: adaptive reference conditioning enhances defect fidelity, while diversity guidance promotes varied defect patterns without additional training. Experiments demonstrate that REDiff-AD can well address the tradeoff between realism and diversity for anomaly pattern generation and achieve competitive downstream anomaly classification, detection, and localization performance. These results show that REDiff-AD is a simple, efficient, and effective solution for large-scale anomaly data augmentation and pixel-level data construction. Github code: https://github.com/efdefe4-droid/REDiff-AD

</details>

Abstract (英文): Forensic verification has converged on a binary "real vs. fake" label that confates fully synthetic, tampered, and AI-retouched images despite their very different consequences. We argue that GenAI manipulations decompose along two complementary channels: a camera channel of sensor fingerprints (destroyed by neural processing), and a semantic channel of scene-level coherence (disrupted by content edits). Each manipulation type leaves a distinctive two-channel signature. We instantiate this taxonomy in 2CAP (2-Channel Authenticity Protocol), pairing a contrastively-trained camera encoder with a frozen semantic encoder to serve two applications on a shared backbone: (i) four-class authenticity classification via cross-attention fusion with learnable reliability weights; and (ii) evidence generation, where an authentic image serves as a reference for a query and the two encoders produce per-channel alignment scores together with a patch-level saliency map. These signals are supplied as privileged context to a frozen Vision-Language Model (VLM) through an Observe-Generate-Refine loop, yielding explanations with localization without forensic fine-tuning. On a multi-source benchmark, 2CAP attains the best classification performance, including strong retouching detection where existing methods fail, and improves explanation quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Surgical video understanding is an important foundation for intelligent assistance in robot-assisted surgery. Similar surgical views can reflect different actions or procedural stages. Interpreting these views requires access to earlier observations, yet frame-centric prompting leaves this history unavailable. We introduce MemSurg, a framework that remembers surgical observations before reasoning over them. Its structured memory graph stores timestamped entity categories, locations, motion cues, and interaction summaries. Chronological links preserve recent context, while entity-associated links connect recurring observations across the procedure. Task-adaptive retrieval then supplies evidence-grounded prompts for scene and workflow reasoning, supporting both frozen inference and memory-conditioned supervised fine-tuning. Evaluation on robot-assisted prostatectomy and two complementary surgical benchmarks shows a 10.43-percentage-point gain over SurgRAW with GPT-4o. Memory conditioning adds 6.52 points under matched Qwen3-VL-8B fine-tuning, and an inference-time plug-in adds 7.04 points to EndoChat. These results support structured memory as a reusable source of procedural evidence for surgical video understanding. Our code will be publicly released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Data transformation pipelines shape the training distribution in computer vision, but designing them is a computationally expensive problem. We investigate whether large language models can enable efficient discovery by synthesizing executable transformation programs outside predefined augmentation spaces. We compare nine strategies under a unified training protocol, including standard augmentation policies, brute-force search, direct code generation with two LLMs, and performance-guided generation using LLMs fine-tuned on accuracy-ordered transformation pairs. When the best candidates are retrained for 20 epochs, LLM-generated transformation programs lead on every dataset, reaching 0.8877 accuracy on CIFAR-10, 0.5825 on CIFAR-100, and 0.9588 on SVHN, surpassing brute-force search over 6,000 candidates. The best transform on CIFAR-10 and SVHN comes from a fine-tuned generator using 270 candidates, while the best on CIFAR-100 comes from an off-the-shelf generator using only 10. This needs 22x fewer candidate evaluations in the generation step and 600x fewer for off-the-shelf generations, relative to brute-force search. Beyond transformation discovery, we analyze the reliability of the reduced-budget evaluation commonly used for augmentation search. Correlating one-epoch selection scores with 20-epoch outcomes reveals strong, weak, and absent predictive relationships depending on the dataset and architecture, with correlations ranging from 0.756 to near zero. A positive control indicates that range restriction alone is unlikely to explain the low correlations. Our results indicate that semantic synthesis with code LLMs provides an efficient alternative to search-based transformation discovery and highlight the importance of validating evaluation fidelity when optimizing data transformations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical Vision-Language Pre-training (VLP) leverages the semantic richness of radiology reports to learn 3D representations, yet current architectures remain fundamentally anatomy-agnostic. While clinical diagnostics rely on organ-specific context, standard encoders apply a uniform, dense parameterization to all volumetric patches, and existing Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routers gate on isolated local features without explicit access to the organ composition of the scan or crop. We propose Anatomy-Activated Mixture-of-Experts (A$^{2}$MoE), a framework that incorporates anatomical inductive biases within the model's computational path. A$^{2}$MoE introduces two key components: (1) a Histogram Representation Router (HRR) that conditions expert selection on global visual context and a predicted anatomical composition histogram, encouraging anatomy-dependent expert weighting; and (2) an Organ-Query Multi-Scale Projector (OQMSP) that utilizes learnable queries to extract organ-level embeddings for fine-grained alignment without requiring manual annotations at inference. By routing at the patch level rather than the token level, A$^{2}$MoE reduces routing complexity by orders of magnitude while satisfying load balancing by construction. Pre-trained on over 60,000 multi-phase CT scans, A$^{2}$MoE achieves state-of-the-art performance across segmentation, classification, and detection benchmarks. Furthermore, we demonstrate that our anatomically grounded inductive biases support robust generalization to MRI and ultrasound, establishing a scalable foundation for medical representation learning. The code is available at https://github.com/gmum/A2MoE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Small hand-tracking networks are deployed as 4- and 8-bit codes, and they should keep adapting to a user's camera after deployment, on the device and within roughly the memory of the model itself. Quantization-aware training cannot do this cheaply, because it keeps a 32-bit master copy of every weight together with its optimizer state, about 96 bits per weight. Training the codes directly with stochastic rounding needs no copy but injects rounding noise at every step, and the methods that remove this noise keep a residual buffer for every weight. We introduce sensitivity-gated accumulator caching (SGAC), which keeps residual state for only a small and changing set of weight groups. A set-associative cache of 8-bit accumulators collects the sub-step updates of the groups with the largest Fisher-weighted squared code step and writes them into the codes as whole steps; an evicted line is flushed by stochastic rounding, and a group without a line does not move until it is admitted. We prove that the write-back is unbiased and show, under a local quadratic model, that rounding a sub-step update costs loss of the same order as the update's own gain, so that rounding pays only where the expected gradient is large relative to the curvature. On a 383k-parameter hand-keypoint network, SGAC trains with 8.1 bits of state per weight. Scored on the 4/8-bit network that the device runs after adaptation, it has the highest mean accuracy of nine deployed low-bit networks on two simulated cameras. On twelve ImageNet-C corruptions it has the highest average of corrupted and clean accuracy of seven deployed methods, ahead of the PEQA-style, ECO-style and direct low-bit baselines with confidence intervals that exclude zero, and it matches an 8-bit accumulator kept for every weight with 56% of that state.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Plant organ segmentation on compact computing platforms requires accurate predictions and affordable geometric preparation. We develop and evaluate a multiscale edge based graph attention model that combines local shape descriptors, two neighborhood scales and a residual stem and leaf correction in approximately 78 thousand parameters. The engineering application is plant point cloud segmentation for precision agriculture, assessed through grouped accuracy experiments and physical Raspberry Pi 5 inference. Broad comparisons with hierarchical, graph and transformer baselines establish PointNeXt as the strongest external accuracy reference among the tested local recipes. A nested four variant ablation on a local maize collection separates geometric information from the combined multiscale configuration across seven outer groups and three seeds. The complete model achieves the highest mean intersection over union, 86.36%, although uncertainty and boundary errors qualify the contribution of each component. We then optimize full cloud principal component analysis, sampling and neighbor queries without changing learned weights or descriptor definitions. All 1,180 paired desktop comparisons on PlantNet and TomatoWUR preserve exact logits and predictions. Multiscale pipeline latency falls from 330.33 to 96.82 milliseconds on PlantNet and from 1,054.52 to 463.10 milliseconds on TomatoWUR. PointNeXt also benefits from optimized sampling and remains faster over the complete pipeline. These results support compact graph learning with reduced geometric overhead, while physical board measurements establish inference feasibility rather than energy efficiency or readiness for autonomous flight.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-task scene understanding models typically rely on modified vision encoders or complex decoders for cross-task interaction, which limits their flexibility and compatibility with off-the-shelf vision foundation models (VFMs). We introduce Virtual Task Prompting (VTP), which frames multi-task prompting as retrieving task-adaptive compositions from a shared virtual state, where both the state and routing process are optimized in an end-to-end manner. Concretely, VTP maintains an expanded set of virtual tasks alongside the backbone. At each layer, learnable routing matrices read task-specific compositions as input to interact with image tokens and write the processed results back, forming explicit pathways that capture the intricacies among tasks outside the encoder. Since VTP keeps the backbone clean, it integrates seamlessly with pretrained VFMs, advancing the state of the art on Pascal-Context and NYUD-v2. Stress test on Taskonomy with 13 heterogeneous tasks further shows VTP's robustness in extreme settings. Ablations and analysis indicate that VTP's gains stem from adaptive routing rather than increased capacity. The project page is available at https://jacky1128.github.io/VTP/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Adaptive tokenization aims to reduce the compute spent on uninformative image regions while preserving accuracy. Existing work relies on (i) fixed or frozen scoring rules, which cannot be trained toward the task loss, or (ii) discrete masks trained with straight-through estimators, which yield noisy gradients, while fully differentiable alternatives instead leave the pre-trained regular grid. We revisit adaptive tokenization from a regular-grid perspective and ask: can a two-scale quadtree be learned end-to-end without deleting a single token during training? We show that one coarse token and its four fine children can be made the two endpoints of a single continuous interpolation. We introduce Differentiable Virtual-Slot Partitioning (\dvsp{}), which keeps four \emph{virtual slots} per $2{\times}2$ region and blends their token features and attention mass with a single weight $α$, exactly reproducing the discrete grid at $α\in \{0,1\}$. On COCO detection with EVA-02-B, \dvsp{} retains $97.9\%$ of dense Box AP while removing $64.2\%$ of visual tokens, giving a $1.61\times$ end-to-end speedup, and it outperforms entropy-based APT, DART, and uniform lower-resolution dense adaptation at matched budgets; these gains depend on the conserved attention mass, whose removal costs $1.59$ Box AP. On a frozen Segmenter it also improves Cityscapes mIoU, and on frozen DeiT backbones it exceeds entropy selection at identical per-image token counts.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video understanding evaluations are intended to measure a model's proficiency in video perception and task solving. However, current VideoQA benchmarks struggle to isolate these capabilities due to their reliance on multiple-choice (MC) answer guidance, language priors, and fragile scoring pipelines. Disentangling these confounding factors reveals how much of a model's score is supplied by the evaluation context rather than earned through faithful perception. Across six VideoQA benchmarks of varying length, we find that accuracy is significantly above chance without any visual input. This is caused by shortcutting and option-prior reliance. We express these effects in the language of faithfulness: option-exploitation and answer-conditioned reasoning traces are unfaithful even when they are correct. Together, our results show that a VideoQA score is jointly determined by language priors, option timing, and how outputs and labels are converted into correctness. We produce a novel Option-Delayed Replay (ODR) mechanism that discloses options at controlled stages of an open-ended (OE) trajectory, to obtain objective MC scores from faithful option-free inference. Our work demonstrates that agentic methods with ODR and faithful inference can match option-exploiting performance. Finally, we formalize our diagnostic collection and ODR as a reproducible evaluation protocol, and we release a human-relabelled edition of LVBench (correcting $20.5\%$ of keys) to remove annotation noise as a confounding variable.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automated brain tumor classification from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) can support accessible medical image analysis, but conventional deep learning models often exceed the resource budgets of highly constrained edge-AI platforms. We present NanoNet, an ultra-lightweight convolutional neural network designed for resourceconstrained MRI classification and motivated by future edge-AI applications. Inspired by MobileNetV2, NanoNet substantially reduces network depth, channel capacity, expansion ratios, and final feature dimensionality, resulting in only five inverted residual blocks and 8,132 parameters. We further investigate dependency-aware structured pruning, knowledge distillation, and INT8 quantization-aware training for additional compression. Experiments on the BRISC 2025 benchmark show that NanoNet achieves 92.80% accuracy and 92.97% macro F1 with 8,132 parameters and 10.498 MFLOPs. Across three compression seeds, it exceeds the mean accuracy of 70%-pruned MobileNetV2 by 5.20 percentage points while using 60.2% fewer parameters and 9.8% less computation. Converted INT8 NanoNet achieves 89.70% accuracy and 90.22% macro F1 across three QAT seeds. Earlier TensorFlow Lite measurements indicate 27.7 KB of baseline model storage, motivating future edge-AI deployment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dynamic neural networks are a class of adaptive models in which the computation assigned to each input sample is determined dynamically, potentially reducing computational cost while maintaining similar performance. For image classification, the Resolution Adaptive Network (RANet) is a strong approach that progressively allocates computational resources across input scales based on sample difficulty. In this work, we introduce RANet with Attention, Distillation and Calibration (ADC-RANet), an enhanced version of RANet that improves performance across all computational budgets. Our approach combines three tightly connected components: bottom-up channel attention, top-down cascaded knowledge distillation, and overconfidence correction using MixUp, in a synergistic design that coordinates both pathways. Experiments on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet datasets show consistent gains while preserving the efficiency of RANet. Our code is available: https://github.com/jefersonseverino/ADC-RANet

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present SoftPAT3D, a physics-augmented framework for text-to-3D scene generation in which deformable objects, such as cloth, stuffed toys and ropes, are generated as physically valid, simulation-ready bodies and placed in intersection-free contact with the furniture that supports them, with one another and with themselves. Existing physics-augmented pipelines simulate rigid bodies only and cannot accommodate a generated deformable object, which text-to-3D generation returns as a geometrically invalid mesh, sometimes self-intersecting, that has no rest shape, no material model and no admissible initial state. These are the inputs a deformable simulation requires, and we construct each from information already in the pipeline. The rest shape comes from how the object is manufactured rather than from the generated physically inconsistent deformed configuration, and a material library assigns each deformable object its initial elastic parameters, which can then be optimized. The occluded support is regenerated and converted into a watertight collision mesh, each object is released from an optimizable placement above the supporting part that the prompt specifies, and the complete scene is driven to static equilibrium under one unified contact model, with completion verified by geometric checks and a vision-language model. When a scene fails the prompt, a grid search over placements corrects it, and the adjoint of a differentiable simulation of the same contact energy reaches the targets the grid does not, places several bodies jointly and recovers and optimizes material parameters. Multi-body scenes equilibrate in a single simulation, and the pipeline also runs from the text prompt alone. On all sixty-five prompts, SoftPAT3D produces no undesired interpenetration of any kind, whereas every other system does, and its scenes directly support scene editing and robotic manipulation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Compressing continuous human motion into a finite vocabulary of discrete tokens is now the first stage of many motion understanding and generation pipelines. Vector quantization partitions a latent space into Voronoi cells around a finite codebook, but the geometry of that latent space is inherited from the input pose encoding, and it is unclear which encoding admits an efficient discretization. We study four encodings that place skeletal motion in different geometric spaces: Gaussian joint heatmaps on the pixel lattice, which we show to be a morphological dilation of the joint point set by a quadratic structuring function; root-relative joint positions in Euclidean space; local joint rotations on $\SO$ through a continuous 6D embedding; and a redundant full kinematic encoding. For each, we train VQ-VAE tokenizers whose codebooks lie on the unit hypersphere, varying codebook size, temporal stride, and code dimension. Beyond reconstruction, we measure codebook utilization and the effective vocabulary size, i.e., the number of equiprobable spherical Voronoi cells with the same entropy as the observed token distribution. Rotation and position encodings keep converting additional codewords into effective vocabulary, whereas the full kinematic encoding saturates at a few hundred effective cells and heatmaps at fewer than sixty. The smallest code dimension gives the highest utilization for three of the four encodings, and the effect of temporal stride depends on the encoding. Encodings of the same pose family can thus yield effective vocabularies differing by an order of magnitude, a difference that reconstruction loss alone does not reveal, which points to input geometry as a first-order design choice in motion tokenization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Progress in 3D sign generation is constrained by the scarcity of high-fidelity 3D sign language datasets, a limitation that is particularly severe for under-resourced languages. We introduce MySign, the first motion-capture dataset for isolated signs in Bahasa Isyarat Malaysia (BIM). Its 5,000 samples cover 1,000 lexical items (glosses) and were produced by five Deaf native signers. Prior datasets that rely on SMPL-X parameters regressed from video provide pseudo-labels, which are often affected by depth ambiguity, inaccurate finger poses, and temporal jitter. MySign instead provides ground-truth SMPL-X sequences retargeted from recordings made with a motion-capture setup tailored for sign language; these sequences offer higher-fidelity motion, particularly in fine-grained hand and finger articulation. A controlled comparison on the same glosses and signers indicates that training on these sequences improves generation over training on such pseudo-labels. We establish MySign as a challenging benchmark for gloss-conditioned 3D sign generation: state-of-the-art models produce visually plausible outputs but fall short of the ground truth, most notably in fine-grained articulation, revealing persistent gaps in modeling articulated sign motion. In contrast, recognition baselines consistently achieve high lexical accuracy, indicating that the dataset reliably supports supervised learning. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/mysigner/MySign

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual In-Context Learning (VICL) enables promptable segmentation by conditioning RGB predictions on visual demonstrations, making it attractive for auto-labeling, but its robustness under query-only access and unknown deployment prompts remains underexplored. We study query-only, prompt-agnostic targeted attacks where the attacker observes only returned RGB outputs, without access to model internals or deployment prompts. Treating prompt-conditioned RGB rendering as a distinct attack surface, we introduce two appearance-space attacks: Color Hijack (CH) drives foreground colors toward the background to suppress visibility, while Task Hijack (TH) collapses instance-distinguishing colors toward a shared color assignment. To transfer across unseen prompts, we propose a Self-Prompt Surrogate (SPS) constructed from the clean query and its output. In a paired, matched-budget SegGPT comparison, SPS outperforms the Random-Prompt Surrogate (RPS), reducing CH instance foreground retention from 0.918 to 0.155 and TH palette-aware tolerance distance from 0.345 to 0.225. Across four VICL models on COCO and HierText, CH suppresses foregrounds across semantic-, instance-, and text-style outputs, while TH collapses instance colors under held-out deployment prompts. After palette-based decoding, TH also reduces the decoded instance count and mask retention while increasing instance merges. Finally, at 30% corruption, CH-corrupted annotations from a publicly accessible VICL-based auto-labeling pipeline reduce downstream YOLO-seg mAP50 from 0.761 to 0.281.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual in-context segmentation uses visual demonstrations to guide predictions for a new image. In SegGPT, we identify background color mapping deviation, where non-target regions sharing a background label receive different output colors, and part-to-parent expansion, where predictions extend beyond the requested part into its parent object. These deviations can occur even when the relevant regions remain distinguishable in internal features. Controlled changes to demonstration colors and requested targets support an account in which demonstrations guide role and color assignments over familiar semantic regions, influenced by learned semantic and color preferences. Building on these findings, we introduce Semantic Contrast Prompt Selection (SCPS). Its semantic contrast encoding assigns separate colors to the requested part and the rest of its object while keeping the remaining scene black. SCPS combines existing retrieval scores with quality estimates from labeled development images to select demonstrations and their binary or semantic contrast encodings. Averaged across seven retrieval methods on three benchmarks, SCPS improves target IoU by 0.054 and reduces leakage into the rest of the object by 22.7% relative to the original binary selectors. Our findings offer a new perspective on understanding and improving demonstration following in visual in-context segmentation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): High-fidelity LiDAR simulation remains a bottleneck for scalable development and validation of autonomous systems. Existing generators can synthesize realistic scans, but they are typically constrained to a single sensor configuration, offer limited control over structure and geometry, and drift in long-horizon 4D generation. We present CrossLiDAR, a unified transformer-based framework capable of generating controllable and spatio-temporally consistent multi-sensor LiDAR sequences. Our approach leverages a rectified flow objective with a Multi-Modal Diffusion Transformer (MM-DiT) backbone, within a compact latent space of range images. To support multiple LiDAR sensors, an Inter-sensor Module enables interaction across sensors, while a ControlNet-style branch provides structural conditioning (e.g., BEV priors or 3D bounding boxes). For modeling the temporal consistency, CrossLiDAR generates short $N$-frame sequences and extends them autoregressively to improve long-horizon coherence. Our model significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving an average improvement of 29.56\% in JSD and 15.36\% in MMD evaluated on nuScenes and the Waymo Dataset. CrossLiDAR bridges the gap to modern autonomous-driving simulation by enabling calibration-consistent multi-sensor LiDAR rollouts over long horizons for controllable, scenario-based testing.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Training vision-language-action (VLA) models to follow gameplay instructions across diverse games is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. We introduce a framework that automatically generates game environments and expert demonstrations. An LLM-powered agent uses game-building tools to construct executable environments and tasks targeting specified skills. Programmatic experts then use privileged simulator state to generate aligned instructions, rendered observations, and keyboard and mouse actions. We train our game policy, Script2Play, by post-training a 4B vision-language model with behavior cloning on 150.3 hours of synthetic Minecraft and Roblox gameplay, without human gameplay demonstrations or reinforcement learning. Evaluation on multiple public game benchmarks demonstrates that Script2Play follows gameplay instructions effectively and generalizes across game engines, highlighting the effectiveness of targeted synthetic supervision.

Abstract (英文): This paper proposes Graphene, a lightweight image super-resolution architecture with two complementary feature refinement mechanisms: Ising-model-inspired local feature refinement using an Ising Refinement Block (IRB) and stage-history-based feature refinement using a History Attention (HistAttn) module. The IRB reuses attention output features and pre-softmax attention logits to construct signed pairwise token couplings within each local window, applies unrolled mean-field updates, and refines features through a gated residual correction. These signed couplings allow alignment and opposition between latent spins to modulate local feature refinement. HistAttn attends over the current stage output and earlier representations at the same token position and adaptively blends the current stage output with a history-weighted feature aggregate without adding spatial pairwise attention. On Urban100 at $\times4$, Graphene and Graphene-light improve over IET and IET-light by 0.24 dB and 0.38 dB, respectively, with 34.3%/13.7% fewer parameters, 44.8%/44.4% fewer FLOPs, 78.4%/71.5% lower latency, and 76.0%/85.5% lower peak memory. Real-world SR comparisons further show that Graphene recovers fine details and preserves textures across diverse degradations. Code and reference checkpoints are available at https://github.com/skethz/Graphene

Abstract (英文): Surgical procedures are inherently partially observable, requiring surgeons to continuously infer evolving anatomical dynamics and future surgical states beyond the current observation. However, existing self-supervised learning methods primarily learn from observed surgical videos through masked reconstruction or predicting representations within the camera's current field of view, lacking the capability to explicitly model the out-of-sight surgical dynamics. Inspired by latent predictive world models, we introduce SurgWorld, a video-native, self-supervised framework for surgical representation learning, which treats a specified spatial or temporal shift as a pseudo-action and learns to predict the target latent representation from a partial observation. Specifically, we propose Out-of-Sight Dynamics Modeling (OSDM) as the pretraining objective for predicting representations conditioned on relative spatio-temporal shifts. We further regularize the learned latent dynamics from OSDM with a Geometric Visual Similarity (GVS) objective by aligning patch-level relational structures between predicted and target representations, preserving spatial geometry and local visual coherence for dense perception. These two objectives are integrated through an agglomerative training framework that consolidates complementary supervisory signals from multiple teachers to learn a unified latent representation of surgical dynamics and visual geometry. Extensive transfer learning experiments on 17 surgical benchmarks show that SurgWorld consistently outperforms existing vision foundation models across surgical workflow recognition, fine-grained action understanding, and dense visual perception. We further conduct tailored qualitative and quantitative analyses, demonstrating that SurgWorld effectively captures surgical dynamics beyond the directly observed context through latent prediction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Parameter-efficient image-to-video transfer adapts frozen image foundation models to video via lightweight trainable adapters. Existing methods insert the same adapter at every transformer layer, implicitly assuming all layers require equal temporal modeling. A layer-wise sensitivity analysis across four video-native model families and two pretraining paradigms reveals that this assumption is wrong: temporal sensitivity increases with depth, and the pretraining paradigm determines its growth profile. Vision-only models exhibit a gradual rise, while vision-language models show early suppression followed by late recovery.
  This finding motivates STRAP, a training-free adapter placement rule that derives a spatial-to-temporal transition boundary from a paradigm-matched video model. Spatial adapters are assigned before this boundary and temporal adapters after it, without modifying the adapter architecture itself. On four fine-grained action recognition benchmarks, STRAP improves accuracy by up to 11.8% over uniform placement, reduces trainable parameters by 29 to 48%, and lowers inference time by up to 10%. On standard benchmarks, it matches accuracy with fewer parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/th-nesh/STRAP.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Streaming 3D foundation models (3DFMs) estimate camera poses and dense geometry online, but they fall short of SLAM: their predictions are fixed once emitted, so loop closures cannot correct accumulated drift. With such models already providing strong geometric capability, a central question for SLAM becomes how to schedule their inference and how to keep its results as revisable state. We present Map2SLAM, which keeps LingBot-Map frozen, trains only a lightweight retrieval head, and builds SLAM on the model's inference interface, turning its predictions into observations of an external persistent SLAM state. The front-end tracks every incoming frame with the model's causal streaming inference and decides which frames are written to the persistent state as keyframes. The back-end asynchronously decides when and which past frames to re-observe, re-running the model's joint inference over them with fresh context; verified loop observations correct the trajectory online, and segment observations refine dense geometry after the sequence. All invocations share a single frame encoder pass per image, whose cached features also drive loop retrieval, so frequent re-invocation incurs no additional encoding cost. Map2SLAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on KITTI, VBR, and Oxford Spires, reducing mean ATE by over 40% compared with the best uncalibrated baselines, while maintaining real-time tracking at over 28 FPS at a resolution of $518\times154$ on an NVIDIA RTX 5090D GPU. Please visit our project page at https://robbyant-research.github.io/Map2SLAM/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In the extreme-low bitrate range below $0.01$ bpp, image compression becomes a constrained generation problem: the reconstruction depends on inferring plausible missing details while staying close to the input image. Autoregressive token models provide an efficient coarse-to-fine prior for this setting, but the transmitted early-scale tokens often leave object identity and texture underdetermined, which can lead to source-inconsistent reconstructions. We introduce Source-Anchored Visual Autoregression (SAVAR), a generative codec built on Visual Autoregressive (VAR) modeling. SAVAR transmits early-scale tokens and a compact caption. The tokens provide image-specific layout information, while the caption supplies global semantics. SAVAR reconstructs a partial VQ-VAE latent from the retained scales, maps it to the start-of-sequence initialization, and uses the same latent to condition the caption embedding before adaptive layer normalization, separating two roles that are typically driven by a shared condition in VAR-style generation. On the Kodak and CLIC2020 datasets in the extreme-low range, SAVAR reduces perceptual distortion relative to matched ARPC retained-scale operating points, lowering LPIPS by about $8$--$9\%$ and DISTS by up to $22\%$ at a lower bpp. It reconstructs a $512 \times 512$ image in about $218$ ms on one NVIDIA L20X GPU, retaining fast autoregressive decoding in the evaluated setting.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual chain-of-thought (visual-CoT), also known as "thinking with images", equips LVLMs with the ability to locate and use key visual evidence for reasoning. However, acquiring such visual-CoT capabilities requires costly post-training and tightly couples them to a specific backbone, making transfer to other LVLMs difficult. Existing cross-model transfer methods typically treat visual-CoT as a monolithic capability, using a unified representation that ignores the functional distinction between grounding and reasoning and introduces stage-irrelevant interventions. To address this problem, we propose Viscot-steer, a training-free activation steering framework for transferring visual-CoT capabilities across heterogeneous LVLMs. Specifically, Viscot-steer first disentangles visual-CoT into grounding and reasoning vectors and extracts them from the source-model layers associated with the corresponding capabilities. It then aligns the vectors with the activation space of the target model and steers the grounding and reasoning layers during bounding-box prediction and reasoning-trace generation, respectively. Across benchmarks covering high-resolution visual understanding, general visual perception and reasoning, and mathematical visual reasoning, Viscot-steer transfers capabilities from different visual-CoT models to architecturally diverse LVLMs and outperforms the strongest capability-transfer baseline by an average of 1.86 percentage points on the primary evaluation metrics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate lighting response is essential for realistic dynamic human relighting, because shadows, highlights, and color changes should follow the target illumination in both direction and relative strength. Current learning-based relighting methods can generate visually plausible videos, but they often exhibit imprecise lighting responses: shadows and highlights may drift away from the target direction, and the output brightness may not faithfully follow the relative intensities in the target environment, making the result closer to appearance harmonization than controllable relighting. To address this issue, we propose DynOLAT, a generalizable OLAT video prediction framework for dynamic full-body humans. Instead of directly synthesizing a single relit appearance, our method predicts temporally consistent One-Light-At-a-Time (OLAT) basis videos from flat-lit inputs, and then linearly combines the predicted bases according to the target environment map. This OLAT-based formulation makes the directional response explicit in the predicted bases, while the relative lighting strength is determined by the linear weights derived from the environment map. We further introduce a dynamic capture pipeline and the first large-scale dynamic full-body OLAT dataset, comprising over 24 million frames from 224 identity-outfit combinations, where different outfits of the same person define separate combinations. Experiments show that DynOLAT responds more faithfully to variations in target lighting direction and relative intensity than the tested baselines, enabling controllable video relighting while preserving fine appearance details and temporal coherence. The dataset and processing tools will be publicly released to foster future research.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Post-hoc explanation methods for video action recognition typically conflate spatial appearance and temporal dynamics into a single saliency or importance map. We propose a perturbation-based framework that disentangles these contributions into a spatial appearance mask ($M_a$) and a temporal motion mask ($M_m$). The two masks are jointly optimized against a frozen action recognition model through factored perturbation architecture, with nearest frame replacement to identify important temporal dynamics. A separation penalty discourages the appearance mask from spending budget on frames that the motion mask has already discarded. A ReLU-based area constraint penalizes explanations larger than an assigned budget. To evaluate these explanations, we introduce a separation protocol that sweeps spatial pixels and temporal frames independently, backed by proofs characterizing the proposed method. Across EPIC-Kitchens-55, EGTEA Gaze+, and Something-Something V2, our method attains the lowest deletion AUC against leading baselines while producing masks roughly five times sparser.

Abstract (英文): Interpretable glaucoma detection requires a transparent connection between fundus images, clinically meaningful biomarkers, and diagnostic predictions. Knowledge graph (KG) reasoning provides this structure, but performance measured using annotated biomarkers does not establish performance when only images are available. We present Auto-GlaKG, an automated framework that combines image-based biomarker prediction with fixed clinical-prior rules. A ResNet50 network predicts the cup-to-disc ratio (CDR) and six morphological signs using biomarker supervision, and a KG reasoner aggregates their signed rule contributions into a glaucoma score. On 689 fundus images evaluated using stratified, group-aware five-fold cross-validation, Auto-GlaKG achieves a pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.951 (95\% confidence interval: 0.931--0.969), with sensitivity of 0.970 and specificity of 0.779. The matched image classifier also achieves an AUC of 0.951, while the rule stage adds 0.2~ms per image. Replacing predicted CDR with its annotation increases fold-mean AUC from 0.953 to 0.995. The framework provides an explicit rule-level account of image-based predictions and identifies CDR estimation as a priority for validation using independently measured biomarkers.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Positron emission tomography (PET) is pivotal in medicine and healthcare, but requires high radiation doses for quality imaging. Although deep learning has shown promise for low-dose PET reconstruction/enhancement, existing methods primarily rely on fully supervised learning, which requires paired low- and full-dose scans that are clinically impractical to obtain. To overcome this fundamental limitation, we propose DuSS-PET, a novel self-supervised PET reconstruction framework that operates without any paired low- and full-dose data. Our method is built upon a computational Noisier2Noise (Nr2N) paradigm, featuring a Bernoulli corruption strategy that serves as an image-space approximation to emulate low-count noise patterns in image-derived pseudo-sinogram representations. The DuSS-PET framework integrates three core components: an adaptive Swin transformer for pseudo-sinogram restoration, a plug-and-play module for image enhancement, and a consistency-constrained SSL strategy that enforces agreement between the pseudo-sinogram and image representations. Extensive experiments on multi-center datasets demonstrate that DuSS-PET achieves superior reconstruction performance, outperforming state-of-the-art SSL methods and achieving comparable or even better results than fully supervised approaches across various dose levels. In particular, it exhibits exceptional generalization across different scanner vendors. This work is among the first self-supervised low-dose PET reconstruction framework that enforces consistency between reconstructed images and pseudo-sinograms, where the pseudo-sinogram generated in the image space serves as a solid computational consistency constraint for enhanced imaging without full-dose training scans. The source code will be publicly available at https://github.com/ladderlab-xjtu/DuSS-PET.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dichotomous image segmentation requires pixel-accurate foreground-background separation while preserving fine structures such as thin filaments, sharp edges, and intricate textures. BiRefNet addressed this challenge through bilateral references, combining image patches and gradient information, but its inward reference loses local spatial structure and lacks semantic guidance, while its outward reference relies on single-channel gradient attention. We recast this paradigm as controlled information recovery - locality determines what spatial information survives, semantic selectivity what information enters, and focused boundary supervision where refinement occurs - and propose BiRefNet-V2 to implement it: (1) locality-preserving and semantically guided detail injection through space-to-depth transformation, overlapping patches, and a Semantically Guided Gate; (2) a learned boundary stream with focused supervision that generates full-channel residual attention without requiring boundary annotations; and (3) a dual-branch decoder integrating depth-wise boundary convolutions with large-kernel attention. A modernized framework - LayerNorm, GELU, the Lion optimizer, and a DINOv3 backbone - cuts training from 600 to 50 epochs. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on DIS5K, camouflaged object detection, and high-resolution salient object detection across all 13 evaluation sets. Code is available at https://github.com/ZhengPeng7/BiRefNet-V2.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reconstructing interactable articulated assets from a few images is key to robotics and simulation, yet highly underconstrained. Existing optimization-based methods fit an articulated asset under a static 3D prior that scores each state independently, so per-state reconstruction errors leak into the estimated moving part and joint as false motion. Video diffusion models capture how objects move between states, but only implicitly in 2D. To this end, we present TransArt3D, an optimization-based framework that reconstructs an articulated 3D asset from two endpoint images by translating this video transition prior into explicit part structure and joint kinematics. It first lifts frames generated between the endpoints with a transition-coupled 3D consensus that applies the static 3D prior jointly across frames, stabilizing unchanging regions and initializing the parts and joint. A rectified-flow kinematic refinement then lets the video prior supervise a 3D motion field over the whole transition, while part assignment and a shared joint constrain the field throughout optimization, keeping per-state errors out of the articulation. Finally, a motion-aware canonical appearance fusion uses the recovered articulation to map both endpoint images onto one canonical surface, texturing regions that only one state reveals. On PartNet-Mobility, TransArt3D outperforms methods given the same two endpoints in geometry and joint accuracy, and surpasses methods given six observed states in articulated-state geometry, joint localization and appearance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated remarkable performance, yet their computational and memory demands pose significant challenges for deployment on mobile devices. Data-free quantization (DFQ) enables the compression of ViTs without access to their original training data. Existing DFQ methods often synthesize calibration samples by maximizing the entropy of patch similarity. However, similarity entropy describes the dispersion of similarity values without ensuring that the generated samples cover diverse feature directions, potentially limiting their effectiveness for quantization calibration. To address this limitation, we propose RankQ-ViT, a rank-guided DFQ framework for ViTs. We first observe that patch similarity matrices derived from Gaussian noise tend to have lower ranks than those derived from real images. Motivated by this observation, we introduce a block-aware self-contrastive objective that promotes feature diversity during synthetic data generation without explicitly computing matrix rank at each optimization step. We then select calibration samples based on their rank and semantic consistency and use them for block-wise reconstruction of the quantized model. Experiments on ImageNet, COCO, ModelNet40, and ShapeNet demonstrate the effectiveness of RankQ-ViT across multiple architectures and tasks, particularly under low-bit quantization. The code is available at https://github.com/Runqing-forMost/rankq-vit.git

</details>

Abstract (英文): Preoperative prediction of postoperative best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) can support prognosis assessment and individualized decision-making in cataract surgery. Multimodal learning can integrate preoperative optical coherence tomography (OCT) images, scanning laser ophthalmoscopy (SLO) images, and clinical text to characterize patient status. However, SLO images are often missing in real-world clinical data due to acquisition or data-management issues, resulting in incomplete multimodal inputs. To address this problem, this paper proposes a conditional diffusion-based method for SLO proxy representation recovery and fusion prediction. Instead of reconstructing raw SLO images, the proposed method formulates SLO missingness as proxy representation recovery in the latent feature space. Specifically, OCT image features and clinical text features are first fused to construct an OCT-text conditional representation, which guides the reverse denoising process of the diffusion model to estimate sample-specific SLO proxy representations. The recovered SLO proxy representation is then used as a substitute for the unavailable SLO modality and incorporated into the downstream multimodal fusion prediction network for postoperative BCVA prediction. Experiments were conducted on 2,141 preoperative multimodal cataract samples, with an SLO missing rate of 58.80%. The proposed method achieved an accuracy of 0.9714 under the criterion of an absolute prediction error within 0.1 LogMAR and a mean absolute error of 0.0423, outperforming representative postoperative visual acuity prediction and missing-modality learning methods. These results indicate that representation-level proxy recovery can alleviate the impact of SLO modality missingness on postoperative BCVA prediction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D visual grounding (3DVG) localizes language-referred objects in 3D scenes, with broad applicability in both indoor and outdoor environments. However, existing 3DVG research focuses on static indoor objects, limiting its use in dynamic outdoor autonomous driving. Motion descriptions, which capture the temporal dynamics of moving objects, offer a promising way to address this challenge. We therefore introduce MoT3DVG, a large-scale dataset for dynamic-aware outdoor 3DVG with temporally evolving motion descriptions. It contains 850 scenarios with 31,128 frames from nuScenes dataset, and provides 144,568 language prompts with motion-aware descriptions for dynamic objects across time. We further propose DynaVG, a novel framework that effectively leverages temporal cues for outdoor 3DVG. In addition to the standard modality-specific encoders, DynaVG introduces a Short-Long Integrated Dynamic Encoding (SLIDE) module before the language-point cloud alignment. SLIDE models both local motion cues and global temporal context via short-term window encoding and long-term window shifting. Extensive experiments demonstrate its state-of-the-art performance on MoT3DVG, validating the effectiveness of motion-aware encoding. Our work reveals open challenges and promising directions for future research in outdoor 3DVG.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Salient and camouflaged object detection (SOD and COD) both recover foreground support, yet most joint methods output only a binary mask and therefore cannot distinguish the type. USC12K distinguishes the two types, but its baseline USCNet still binds foreground existence to the type within a single normalization, so the failure source cannot be attributed. Our cross-task study shows that foreground localization transfers between SOD and COD, whereas type discrimination does not: a COD-only model recovers foreground on SOD images but its type judgement follows the training distribution rather than the foreground-background relation, so the two decisions require separate mechanisms. SegDis factorizes the output into an explicit foreground probability and a conditional type probability given foreground: FLR provides high-quality task-agnostic support, and RTA refines the SOD/COD assignment from the foreground-background relation without a task identifier. Because support is explicit, each ground-truth foreground pixel is classified as correct-type, type-swapped, or missed, so foreground-recovery and type-assignment errors are diagnosed separately. With PVT-v2-B2, SegDis attains 68.98% FG-mIoU and 78.35% mIoU on USC12K at about one-eighth of USCNet's parameters; CSCS is 5.75.

Abstract (英文): What if a face recognition system could learn from every encounter without a single gradient update? We present Gallery Self-Evolution (GSE), a training-free framework where the gallery itself adapts at test time. The core mechanism---dedup-FIFO curation---archives novel appearance samples after successful recognition while permanently locking the enrollment template, bounded at 30 embeddings per identity. On CFP-FP and IJB-C benchmarks, GSE closes the side-view gap by +23 points at 45° yaw and boosts TAR@FAR=0.001 by +9.4 points, all at CPU-level cost. No retraining. No GPU. Just a gallery that grows smarter every time it meets you.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aligning video generation models with human preference depends critically on the quality of the reward model that scores generated videos. Current video reward models, however, source their reward signal from a model's representational rather than generative capacity: they either repurpose vision-language models as pixel-space scorers, or train auxiliary scoring heads on top of frozen generator features. The generator's actual ability to predict velocities, denoise latents, and produce videos plays no role in scoring its own generations. We argue that this overlooks a structural property of offline preference learning. Any preference optimization objective that approximates an offline preference dataset mathematically targets a strictly defined KL-constrained optimal policy. By isolating the reward term from this analytical solution and mapping the exact generation log-likelihood to its flow-matching surrogate, the empirical reward emerges deterministically as the relative difference in continuous-time velocity-prediction errors. We formalize this equivalence into GenRM-Flow, a framework where a preference-tuned video generator inherently operates as a process-aware reward model directly in the noisy latent space. We further show theoretically that distinct fine-tuning objectives, whether contrastive like DPO and IPO or non-contrastive like RWR, are alternative optimization pathways toward the same optimal policy, each inducing a reward readout from the generator's velocity-prediction errors. Furthermore, the extracted process reward seamlessly substitutes external VLM scorers in FlowAWR. Operating purely on native latent outputs without auxiliary reward networks, this generative reward drives measurable improvements in downstream video generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent zero-shot image compression methods exploit the powerful priors of pretrained diffusion models to produce perceptually pleasing reconstructions without compression-specific training. However, image compression serves not only human viewing but also machine analysis, while existing zero-shot codecs have limited flexibility in meeting the diverse reconstruction requirements of these applications. In this paper, we propose Flex-DDCM, a framework for zero-shot task-oriented image compression via diffusion trajectory guidance. Building on Turbo-DDCM, we adapt the coded diffusion trajectory through task-guided noise selection, embedding the effects of task guidance into the compressed representation. Guidance terms can be applied individually or combined, with timestep-dependent strengths controlling their influence throughout denoising. This design enables task adaptation without introducing additional decoding complexity. Experiments demonstrate flexible trade-offs among pixel fidelity, perceptual quality, and downstream task performance, with competitive performance against trained task-oriented codecs and faster decoding than Diff-ICMH.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Machine unlearning has become increasingly important in face recognition, where regulations like the GDPR empower users to request the removal of their personal data from deployed models. However, most existing unlearning methods are designed for closed-set classification with small-scale models and training data, and therefore do not generalize to real-world face recognition systems, which operate in an open-set setting with large-scale models and data. In this work, we propose Rapid Activation Dampening (RAD), a fast and targeted unlearning method tailored specifically for large-scale open-set face recognition. RAD approximates differences in average neuron activations between forget and retain identities, identifying and selectively suppressing neurons that are most associated with the targeted identity through a sensitivity-controlled dampening mechanism. We further propose the Forget Score (FS), which addresses the mismatch between existing unlearning metrics and open-set problems by providing a probabilistic interpretation of forgetting directly in the representation space. Experiments across several recent large-scale face recognition architectures and datasets show that RAD achieves strong forgetting while preserving overall model utility and substantially improving unlearning speed. These findings establish RAD as a scalable and practical approach to machine unlearning in face recognition systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. SurgAgentGym evaluates whether generalist agents can improve surgical understanding at inference time through tool-mediated evidence use, without additional surgical training. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. This opens a path toward future studies of agentic surgical understanding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agentic autoregressive video generation combines semantic planning, sequential generation, and visual verification for controllable synthesis. Growing visual histories enlarge the key-value (KV) cache, increasing memory use and attention costs across candidate rollouts. Existing compression methods primarily optimize individual generators without the planner's semantic constraints, risking low priority for states supporting requested attributes or actions. We introduce SemanticKV, a training-free method for semantically aligned KV cache compression. To our knowledge, SemanticKV is the first KV cache compression method for agentic autoregressive video generation that explicitly uses planner-extracted semantic constraints to guide retention. Text cross-attention identifies constraint-related video positions, whose self-attention queries are collected into separate groups. Attention weights are averaged within each group and maximized across groups, giving each cached entry a high score when strongly used by any represented constraint. A single top-k selection retains high-scoring entries under the memory budget, leaving their keys, values, and positions unchanged. For fixed query groups and equal entry costs, this selection minimizes an additive upper bound on worst-group discarded attention. The method operates independently within each candidate without retraining or additional video generations. We evaluate four AR video backbones on three benchmarks for 5-, 30-, and 60-second generation. On Causal Forcing at a compression ratio of 0.7, SemanticKV obtains a VBench Total Score of 84.10, compared with 84.05 for the strongest compressed baseline and 83.99 for Full KV. It achieves a 1.22x speedup over Full KV and reduces KV-cache memory from 6.04 GB to 1.81 GB, a 3.35x reduction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Robotic manipulation requires representations that capture both scene geometry and its evolution during interaction. Existing approaches have largely treated these two aspects separately. World action models capture such dynamics by coupling action prediction with future video generation, but their reliance on iterative diffusion or rectified-flow inference leads to high computational cost, while their representations lack explicit spatial structure. Explicitly modeling scene geometry typically requires auxiliary modules that increase both parameter count and computational cost. Conversely, geometry-centric policies primarily recover observed spatial structure and often lack explicit supervision for anticipating future geometric changes during interaction. To address these limitations, we introduce ForeAct, which repurposes a pretrained video world model as a single-pass representation backbone for low-latency control. ForeAct directly processes the clean latent of the current observation in one forward pass, eliminating future-frame synthesis and iterative denoising while retaining the backbone's pretrained spatiotemporal priors. Rather than introducing a separate geometry backbone, ForeAct uses learnable tokens supervised by next-frame relative depth to shape the shared video representation through bidirectional self-attention. This compact interface turns the spatial knowledge already encoded in the backbone into a future-aware representation for action generation, with little parameter overhead. The lightweight geometry readout is used only during training and removed at deployment, enabling low-latency, geometry-aware control without depth input or an external geometry backbone. Without additional large-scale pretraining, ForeAct achieves 96.6% average success on LIBERO, 63.5% on SimplerEnv-Bridge, and a 74.4% success rate across 90 real-world rollouts.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Computed tomography (CT) restoration methods often use a single network for low-dose CT (LDCT), sparse-view CT (SVCT), and metal artifact reduction (MAR), despite the distinct physics of these degradations. We propose AgentCT, a vision-language agent that identifies the degradation and routes images to task-specific restoration models. A LoRA-adapted Qwen3-VL-4B controller predicts degradation type and severity, selects a specialist, and reassesses the restored image. The LDCT specialist uses an image-domain diffusion model, whereas the SVCT and MAR specialists combine projection-domain correction, data consistency, differentiable filtered back-projection, and image refinement. We evaluated the controller and specialists separately on patient-disjoint Mayo 2020 abdominal CT data across three severity levels for each degradation. The controller achieved 95.7% accuracy for degradation type and 93.9% for severity, with a 100% JSON parse rate. Using ground-truth degradation labels for routing, AgentCT achieved an overall PSNR/SSIM of 46.18 dB/0.984, compared with 44.93 dB/0.976 for AMIR and 44.29 dB/0.979 for TAMP, and attained the highest PSNR in all nine settings. For SVCT, its dual-domain specialist achieved 46.04 dB with 4.2 million parameters, compared with 44.43 dB for single-task AMIR and 43.57 dB for all-in-one AMIR with 23.5 million parameters. These results support degradation-specific CT restoration with controller-based model selection; evaluation under automatic routing and on independent clinical data remains necessary. Code is available at https://github.com/hao1635/AgentCT.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.

</details>
