Abstract (英文): Long-term satellite monitoring of rift propagation is essential for assessing Antarctic ice shelf stability and projecting sea-level rise. Currently, long-term tracking of rifts still relies on time-consuming manual interpretation, significantly affecting its feasibility for large-scale rift monitoring. Here, we present a novel framework for automatically tracking rift propagation, which fuses the fine-tuned visual foundation model (Segment Anything Model, SAM) and spatiotemporal correction. The SAM enables automated rift extraction using only a few samples without any manual prompts, while the spatiotemporal correction improves the precision of rift mapping for geometric measurements. We applied this framework to automatically monitor key rifts on the Amery Ice Shelf using 768 Landsat 8/9 and Sentinel-2 images from 2015 to 2025, achieving an F1 score of 98.6% across 11 independent scenes. Rift length and width measurements were in agreement with manual interpretation, with R2 values of 0.979 and 0.999, respectively. Furthermore, our extended monitoring revealed recent structural changes within the rift system on the Amery Ice Shelf. This study offers a new way for automated rift tracking with the foundation model for the first time, having significant potential for long-term monitoring of pan-Antarctic rifts.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modelling spatial interactions remains a core issue in geographic information science. Faced with the emerging, non-stationary spread of epidemics, the effects and uncertainties of spatial interactions are amplified. Limited by reliance on a single spatial interaction prior, existing models perform well in specific scenarios but suffer from prediction drift across multi-phase epidemics, thereby hindering real-time and dynamic policy adjustments. To address this limitation, we propose an ensemble approach driven by spatial interaction, built upon a foundational metapopulation model. Using the 2022 Shanghai Omicron outbreak as a case study, we evaluated the proposed ensemble model against 14 mechanistic and machine learning baselines. In multi-phase prediction tasks, the proposed ensemble model achieves the best overall performance with mean ranks of 3.25 for RMSE and 4.00 for R2, surpassing all baselines with mean ranks ranging from 5 to 15. Furthermore, it improves mean predictive accuracy by 29% to 72% compared with the non-ensemble models, while reducing the prediction error interval by 10% to 84%. Finally, we generated model-based projections across multiple counterfactual scenarios to explore responses to changes in intervention timing and intensity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate river discharge estimation is essential for water cycle modeling, water hazards prevention, water ecosystem protection and water resource management. However, conventional ground-based gauging networks are often sparsely distributed and insufficient to capture the complex hydrodynamic characteristics within large and heterogeneous river basins. With the rapid advancement of remote sensing technologies, particularly the launch of the Surface Water and Ocean Topography (SWOT) mission, new opportunities have emerged for large-scale and continuous river discharge estimation. In this study, we develop and evaluate a SWOT-based river discharge estimation framework for the Yangtze River by integrating SWOT RiverSP hydraulic observations organized on the SWORD river network with Satellite Embedding V1 data in a random forest model to capture both hydrodynamic and geomorphological characteristics. The framework is evaluated using temporally separated training and testing observations from nine gauging stations along the Yangtze River mainstream, with station-specific nonlinear empirical models used for comparison. The main conclusions are as follows: 1) The random forest model substantially improves discharge estimation relative to the empirical formulation, with a maximum NSE gain of 0.4372 and an NRMSE reduction of up to 0.1175; 2) The model demonstrates reliable performance across the nine gauging stations, with all stations achieving NSE ≥ 0.72 and the overall performance reaching NSE = 0.8910 and NRMSE = 0.0664; 3) The spatially explicit discharge estimates show strong agreement with reach-scale results (correlation ≈ 0.9), indicating that water level exerts primary control on discharge, while channel width and geometry significantly modulate discharge responses in morphologically complex reaches. These results demonstrate the potential of combining SWOT hydraulics with satellite foundation embeddings to improve discharge estimation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) offer a scalable approach for annotating mental health and crisis-related social media content, but their reliability in safety-critical tasks remains unclear. This study evaluates LLM-based crisis-severity annotation of veteran-related social media posts against annotations from eight Veteran Subject Matter Experts (SMEs). Using 149 tweets and five severity categories, we evaluate 15 open-source and 10 proprietary LLMs across eight prompt variants, examining annotation performance, inter-model agreement, prompt sensitivity, and high-severity crisis detection. Human reviewers showed higher ordinal than nominal agreement (α = 0.494), indicating that disagreements often occurred between neighboring severity levels. In contrast, selected LLMs demonstrated substantially higher inter-model consistency, with ordinal agreement (α) reaching 0.8336 under the baseline prompt. Proprietary models generally showed better agreement with expert annotations and greater stability than open-source models. GPT-5.6-Terra achieved the best overall performance, while the open-source Qwen2.5-7B showed the strongest performance in identifying Acute Crisis with Suicidal Ideation cases. However, higher model consistency did not necessarily correspond to agreement with human judgments or reliable detection of rare high-severity cases. Label-flipped demonstration experiments also revealed model-specific sensitivity to misleading in-context examples, while explanatory demonstrations did not consistently improve performance. Overall, LLMs show promise as scalable complements to expert annotation, but require careful evaluation before use in high-stakes mental health applications.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LoRA offers a parameter-efficient adaptation strategy, supporting multi-user workloads via customized adapters on a shared backbone. However, serving concurrent requests with different adapters introduces substantial computation and memory-management overhead. We ask the research question: Do all requests need their adapters throughout the whole generation process? Our motivational study reveals that employing LoRA adapters at every decoding step is unnecessary, releasing them early has limited impact on accuracy while substantially accelerating generation. Although skipping adapters early offers theoretical workload reduction, translating these computational savings into concrete throughput gains requires tailored GPU kernels. We present LoRazor, a multi-LoRA serving framework that combines early adapter release with active-row aware GPU kernels. LoRazor determines when to permanently release a request's adapter and continue decoding with the backbone alone. By dynamically resizing LoRA kernel launches as adapters are released, LoRazor trims idle GPU blocks. Across two backbones and four tasks, LoRazor achieves up to 3.02x request throughput and 2.53x output-token throughput over vLLM multi-LoRA, with a mean relative task-accuracy decrease of 2.89%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Children and adolescents are increasingly interacting with generative AI and large language models (LLMs), yet little is known about how these interactions may influence their development, beliefs, and behavior. Existing research has relied heavily on surveys, while direct study of children's interactions with LLMs presents substantial challenges related to safety, privacy, consent, and experimental control. We introduce AYO (AI Youth Open Science), an open-source platform designed for safe and systematic research on children's interactions with conversational AI. AYO provides a child-friendly interface, configurable age-based safety guardrails, researcher monitoring, and privacy-preserving data practices. These capabilities enable cross-sectional, longitudinal, and experimental research on how children use and respond to LLMs and how AI design may shape trust, epistemic judgment, curiosity, and other aspects of development. AYO provides the infrastructure to generate evidence that guides safer AI systems, educational practices, and policies for children growing up with AI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Language Models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks and harmful-content prompts, particularly under adversarial distribution shifts where static alignment pipelines fail to generalize. Although introspective methods such as STAIR strengthen refusal consistency, they depend on fixed self-evaluation policies that cannot rapidly adapt to evolving manipulation strategies. To overcome these limitations, this paper introduces META-IR, a meta-introspective reasoning framework that enhances safety alignment through meta-learning-driven adaptation. It employs a dual-loop architecture: an inner introspective loop that performs structured risk analysis, self-assessment, and reflective correction via SI-MCTS, and an outer meta-learning loop that optimizes the introspection policy across diverse jailbreak task families to enable rapid generalization to unseen attacks. A dynamic jailbreak simulation module further exposes the model to progressively harder adversarial prompts, enabling continual self-improvement without human supervision. Experiments across multiple models (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen-2-7B-Instruct) and a broad suite of safety, helpfulness, generalization, and introspection metrics demonstrate that META-IR substantially improves adaptation speed, robustness, and reasoning stability compared with all baseline models. These results establish META-IR as an effective pathway toward building LLMs capable of sustained, adaptive safety alignment under dynamic and evolving threat environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The rapid expansion of AI-driven data center construction has outpaced public engagement: an estimated $64 billion in U.S. data center projects were blocked or delayed by local opposition between May 2024 and March 2025, yet the concerns driving this resistance remain poorly characterized. Prior engagement methods such as surveys and hearings are introduced late in the planning process, and no study has systematically mined social media to measure community stance at scale. To address this gap, this study presents an LLM-powered opinion-mining framework and applies it to 798 Reddit posts and 102,328 associated comments spanning a decade. An open-source large language model, guided by a Chain-of-Stance prompting framework and three domain-specific labeling rules, classified stance at 83% of the human inter-reviewer agreement ceiling. Opposition dominated discourse volume rather than disapproval: against posts outnumbered favor posts 2.6:1 and generated 8.5 times more comments, and this gap widened significantly over 86 weeks. Topic modeling of opposition arguments resolved into four reproducible clusters, resource consumption and scarcity (42.8%), contested siting processes and secrecy (34.2%), political opposition to data centers (13%), and political and corporate accountability (8%). Engagement analysis revealed that posts with emotional or provocative political framing attracted the most engagement, while key technical concerns (e.g., water, power) were dispersed across lower-engagement comment threads. These findings give local leaders, developers, and policy makers a quantified, continuously updatable basis for prioritizing concerns and policy levers, supporting a shift from opposition to negotiated, verifiable partnership for data center development.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study homeopathic learning, a phenomenon where poisoned training data with little effect on its own can create a hidden backdoor once clean, contrasting data is added. In our experiments, a model fine-tuned only on a "poison" dataset associated with a trait T (such as speaking German or being misaligned) does not learn T. Surprisingly, adding similarly formatted "clean" data not associated with T causes the model to learn T. This effect requires that the poison data have an identifying "trigger" feature that is absent from the clean data. We demonstrate this effect in a variety of settings: subliminal learning of backdoor behaviors, inoculation prompting of reward hacking transcripts, generalization from archaic bird names, and an MNIST classifier with mislabeled data. We propose a simple regression model as an explanation. In the poison data, the task and trigger always co-occur, and regularization favors attributing T to the task. Clean data without the trigger causes T to be attributed to the trigger instead. Consequently, the same training examples can have little effect as part of one training set but create a misalignment backdoor in another. This finding exposes a potential limitation of safety methods that audit or filter training data in isolation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): What is the value of AI-involved art, and to whom does it belong? We begin to answer these questions using data from an in-person, juried AI art exhibition. Our study involved artists, lawyers, and computer scientists (N=209) as either creators or evaluators of AI-involved artworks. We found that nearly half of evaluators reported willingness to buy an artwork, with pricing correlating with perceived artistic value---creativity and visual appeal---as well as the effort necessary to create it. In contrast, three-quarters of creators reported willingness to sell at prices correlating only with effort rather than artistic value. Both groups viewed prompting users and AI developers as deserving rewards and rights, leaving training data artists behind. Disciplinary training shaped these views: participants with legal training favored distributed reward models. These findings have implications for future research on human-AI creative collaboration and policy debates regarding AI-involved works.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have continuous deficiency in spatial reasoning tasks that require \emph{mental spatial transformation}, such as reasoning under hypothetical changes in viewpoint or position, known as perspective-taking tasks. Existing approaches either scale linguistic reasoning or augment VLMs with static geometric representations, but neither directly enables active transformation of the represented scene. World models offer this capability, yet existing systems typically communicate through synthesized RGB images, introducing a latent-to-pixel-to-latent bottleneck. We introduce \emph{WorldMind}, a framework for test-time scaling of spatial reasoning through latent world simulation. Given a query and observation, the VLM selects useful spatial transformations, while a world model executes them and exposes aligned intermediate latent features directly to the VLM. The resulting latent evidence is incorporated into subsequent reasoning steps, enabling iterative spatial simulation without repeatedly decoding imagined states into pixels. To optimize this interaction, WorldMind further employs process-verifiable reinforcement learning that rewards reasoning progress only when the associated action and latent transition are valid. WorldMind thus scales spatial reasoning by allocating additional inference-time computation to active world simulation rather than generating more language or pixels. Across VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench, and MindCube-Tiny, WorldMind achieves an average improvement of 16.0% over the base VLM and 8.3% over the strongest RGB-based world simulation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Empowered by Large Language Models (LLMs), deep research systems integrate online information into research reports through multi-step web searching and information synthesis. Evaluating these reports presents a significant challenge due to their long context, high information density, and domain specificity, necessitating the use of AI evaluation as a substitute for prohibitively expensive human assessment. However, few studies have examined the effectiveness of existing reward models or LLMs in understanding and evaluating such research reports. To bridge this gap, we introduce DeepResearch RewardBench, the first benchmark dedicated to evaluating reward modeling for research reports. We collect diverse report candidates from multiple agents or tool-augmented LLMs. These candidates are scored using query-specific golden rubrics, and we ensure data quality and difficulty through a combination of multi-round filtering and human verification. Our benchmark comprises preference datasets formatted as pairs, triplets, and quadruplets to test ranking capabilities across varying levels of difficulty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that while current reward models handle general coherence effectively, their performance significantly degrades when evaluating research reports. Generative LLMs acting as judges demonstrate comprehensive superiority in this domain, with comparative evaluation proving more effective than direct scoring methods. We anticipate that this benchmark will serve as a vital testbed for the evaluation and advancement of research agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing LLM post-training quantization techniques determine precision before inference begins, yet the precision a model requires varies dynamically across prompts, layers, and inference phases. We present AutoEMatic, a full-stack co-design that adapts the precision of weights, activations, and the KV cache at runtime. A lightweight policy selects each layer's precision at every prefill and decode step from two runtime signals: the entropy of the next-token distribution and the magnitude of each layer's update to its hidden states. This runtime adaptation is made practical by AutoFP, a multi-scale floating-point format that derives 2-, 4-, and 6-bit precisions from a single NVFP4 base format. We realize AutoEMatic on two targets: GPU kernels that execute AutoFP on NVFP4 Tensor Cores, and an accelerator whose vector MAC datapath executes all AutoFP formats natively. Across reasoning and agentic benchmarks, AutoEMatic improves accuracy by up to 21.7% over NVFP4 and matches group-scaled FP6 accuracy on most benchmarks, using 4.9 bits per weight versus 4.5 for NVFP4. On GB300 GPUs, AutoEMatic retains approximately 90-98% of NVFP4 decode throughput across the evaluated configurations. Furthermore, the AutoEMatic accelerator reduces latency by 1.6-2.3x and energy by 7-23% compared to an accelerator with NVFP4-only datapath at iso-accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Independent verification organizations (IVOs) have emerged as a popular, bipartisan solution for facilitating high-quality independent evaluations of AI technologies. But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Today's LLM-based agents are vulnerable to deception by users and other agents. Defending against deception requires Theory of Mind (ToM) reasoning to infer the beliefs and goals of others as well as modeling tactics based on communication theory. To enable training models to detect deception, we adopt the well-known deception game, Avalon. We present a corpus, AVCORP, of 5,000 annotated utterances from 250 games of Avalon. AVCORP includes (1) 37 tactics mapped to a 4x4 behavior matrix, and (2) reasoning traces capturing abductive hypotheses, suspicion levels, and nested ToM beliefs. We train Qwen3-8B on AVCORP and evaluate it on 25 held-out games and 20 games played by humans. On held-out games, our best trained detector outperforms its untrained baseline and frontier models (GPT-5.6 Sol, Gemini 3.1 Pro, DeepSeek-V4-Pro), matching Claude Opus 5.5. On human games, every trained detector exceeds the untrained model in F1 score across all rounds.

Abstract (英文): Medical ultrasound (US) enables real-time, non-invasive diagnosis, yet building US-based AI systems is limited by data scarcity, costly expert annotation, and limited cross-center generalizability. Privacy constraints and instance-level reporting hinder the collection of diverse abnormal cases and frame-level labels, while repeated retraining for new centers increases computational cost. We introduce EvoDiff, a generative active learning framework that integrates diffusion modelling with information-theoretic objectives, evolutionary search, and curriculum-based sampling to select informative samples across heterogeneous US datasets. EvoDiff formulates sample selection as a generate-to-retrieve process, in which diffusion-based optimization in feature space guided by information gain produces synthetic probes, and only the real unlabeled samples retrieved by these probes are annotated and used for downstream training. Across six benchmarks covering fifteen datasets and 16,696 images, we evaluate EvoDiff on convolutional network and transformer-based foundation model. EvoDiff approaches fully supervised performance with substantially fewer labels, outperforms active learning baselines, and improves foundation model generalization under cross-domain external validation on unseen cohorts. A reader study with six radiologists indicates the potential to support clinical annotation workflows, with a model-based estimated 24% to 84% reduction in annotation workload at matched downstream performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) increasingly support Web-based services that must balance response quality and inference cost across heterogeneous requests. Conventional routing selects one model per prompt, while fixed ensembles cannot adapt their composition and cost to individual requests. We introduce PISA, a prompt-adaptive, cost-aware single-model router, and PISA-E, its extension to ensemble routing. At the core of both methods, PISA combines soft cluster memberships with model-specific predictors to estimate individual model capabilities. Building on these estimates, PISA-E jointly selects ensemble composition and cardinality under an explicit cost--quality preference that can be adjusted at inference time without retraining. A tractable ensemble-success surrogate and efficient candidate selection enable this extension without ensemble-specific training. Across four LLM routing benchmarks under in-distribution (ID) and out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions, PISA achieves the highest macro-average cost--performance hypervolume among the evaluated single-model routers in both settings. Meanwhile, PISA-E achieves the highest macro-average task performance among the evaluated ensemble approaches when prioritizing response quality, surpassing the strongest competitors by 2.09 and 2.87 percentage points under ID and OOD conditions, respectively. At selected lower-cost operating points, it also exceeds the strongest ensemble competitors' macro-average task performance while reducing average inference cost by approximately 36% and 25%, respectively. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of prompt-adaptive capability estimation for both single-model routing and selective ensembling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) inference is constrained by GPU memory. This constraint is mitigated by expert offloading, which stores part of the model's weight in host memory and executes on demand. Though MoE architecture has computation sparsity, more GPUs are needed for serving increasingly larger frontier models. However, existing expert offloading systems overlook this need and fail to scale performance with additional GPUs. We present AdaOffload, a general and efficient multi-GPU hybrid expert execution framework that effectively handles heterogeneous hardware and workloads. We co-design GPU-CPU parallelism and GPU-CPU co-execution for decode and prefill with online workload observation that adapts to different hardware, workload, and batch size heterogeneity. We evaluated AdaOffload on five different platforms with varying GPU capability (L4, Pro6000, B200), number of GPUs, CPU capability, and NUMA topology on three different frontier open-weight models (284B to 753B). AdaOffload achieves $1.9\times$ to $15.5\times$ throughput speedups across different models over state-of-the-art systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mathematical understanding depends on the route from hypotheses to conclusion: how the parts of a proof function and which dependencies carry the argument forward. This structure can persist when wording or notation changes, while a small alteration to a hypothesis or inference can produce a different argument beneath similar prose. Formal verification can establish that a rewrite proves the same theorem, but not whether it follows the same mathematical route. Comparing informal proofs, especially those produced or revised by generative models, therefore requires reconstructing which claims, dependencies, and reasoning steps are preserved, reorganized, or replaced.
  We present ProofDiff, a system for comparing informal mathematical documents. It is designed to support human review, not to certify mathematical correctness. Given two documents, ProofDiff independently constructs a source-grounded argument map for each and proposes correspondences between the resulting maps. Each map captures the document's claims and reasoning steps and records the typed relations that connect them. An integrated human-review layer allows reviewers to revise the maps and correspondences while preserving an auditable history. Comparing the two argument maps provides a basis for inspecting whether the documents follow the same mathematical route and where their claims or dependencies differ. We demonstrate ProofDiff on four illustrative pairs of AI-generated proofs, ranging from a detailed proof and its concise revision to proofs using different mathematical strategies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A phone screen can be wrong in ways that need no reference: a label that runs off the screen, letters that touch, a line that breaks one character before its end, one button with sharp corners among round ones. We ask whether a model can box every such fault on a screenshot, given no target design and no reference image. Such a critic could serve as a cheap first pass over generated pages before a designer reviews them, but only if it stays silent on clean pages. ScreenProof poses this target-free critique over three families of faults: general faults, local style faults (one element breaks the style its page sets) and text layout faults (letter spacing, line height, line breaks). A data engine makes each fault in page code and records the changed element's box, and hard negatives change every sibling alike. No frontier judge is reliable: frontier models flag 18-62% of clean real screenshots and 18-82% of clean generated pages, find 47-72% of text layout faults, and rarely find a foreign icon (5-19%). Our small critic ProofJudge (Qwen3.5-9B with LoRA), trained on code-made pages with one to four faults each and keeping a finding only when it is confident, reaches F1 0.48, the highest of all judges (Gemini 4 Argon 0.37; open models at most 0.15; 0.46 even without the confidence cut), and is the only fault-finding judge within 15% false alarms on clean pages (2% real, 9% generated), the property such a first pass needs, at about a hundredth of Argon's cost. It finds the most general faults (59%) but fewer local style faults than Argon (25% against 41%) and fewer text layout faults than the API judges (43%; Gemini 4 Argon 47%). Against a named brand the task is harder still: models know a design language about as well as its public documentation states it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Context selection can reduce input length during long-input inference but can remove evidence required to answer a query. We introduce CoRA-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating evidence preservation under shared token budgets. We compare head and tail truncation, BM25, dense retrieval, dense retrieval with reranking, LLMLingua-2, and Provence across BABILong, LOONG Financial Spotlight, a custom NoLiMa diagnostic, and LongMemEval. Evidence-preservation results reveal task-dependent tradeoffs: BM25 achieves 0.921 supporting-fact recall on BABILong at 128K, dense retrieval and dense reranking each achieve 0.908 needle recall on NoLiMa at 64K, and dense retrieval achieves 0.993 answer-bearing-turn recall on LongMemEval at 64K. Evidence, token retention, and complementary reader evaluations use the same selected contexts. Reader evaluations use dataset-native answer metrics and three runs on the same questions, with paired bootstrap confidence intervals. At a 32K budget, BM25 achieves 46.20% BABILong answer accuracy and dense retrieval achieves 71.13% LongMemEval accuracy. The results show that method rankings vary across evidence types and between evidence recall and downstream accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The phrase recursive self-improvement (RSI) refers to the idea of an AI system designing and creating another, better AI system, which in turn designs and creates an even better one, and so on. While the idea has a long history, it has always been an abstract, futuristic idea -- until recently. Today's frontier AI has attained a set of abilities, including in programming, mathematics, research, tool use, and working coherently over extended periods of time, that make RSI seem plausible. AI companies are starting to pursue it, but many of the involved people are also expressing concerns, asking whether humanity should really set this in motion and under what conditions. I will discuss the situation, argue that we do not even have clear frameworks for thinking about the likely outcomes of RSI, and do a quick game-theoretic analysis of how to choose whether to initiate RSI when another party is making the same choice.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLMs are increasingly used to support decisions that require moral consideration. While LLMs perform well on morality benchmarks from psychology and philosophy, producing appropriate outputs on these benchmarks (moral performance) does not establish that an LLM weighs competing moral values at stake in a decision (moral competence). We test a minimal, necessary condition for such weighing: that an ordinal ranking can represent LLM moral choices. Such a ranking requires transitivity. To assess transitivity, we presented LLMs with pairwise trade-offs among moral value violations in vignette choice sets and extracted resulting choice probabilities from first-token distributions. Tested against three stochastic transitive choice models, most choice sets admitted weak or unstable orderings. Aggregating an LLM's responses, a common practice in LLM moral evaluation, produced transitive structure that matched no individual choice set. Evaluations of AI morality should establish that choices are structured before asking whether they are aligned.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Purpose: To develop and validate a scalable adversarial evaluation framework for assessing the robustness of large language models (LLMs) that generate patient-friendly summaries of radiology reports.
  Methods: We introduced a clinically grounded adversarial framework that uses controlled term substitutions and impression removals across intensity levels. Using a radiologist-validated adversarial dictionary, we generated adversarial variants of CT and MRI reports. Six LLMs summarized original and adversarial impressions using 2 prompt styles, yielding 18,000 evaluation pairs. Robustness was evaluated with an automated LLM-as-a-judge framework scoring factual faithfulness, sensitivity to adversarial edits, certainty fidelity, and hallucination rate. Failure mode analysis quantified vulnerabilities in incorporating clinical modifiers. Agreement with two radiologists was assessed on 105 cases.
  Results: GPT-5-mini achieved the highest factual faithfulness and certainty fidelity under both adversarial edits. Under term substitution, hallucinations occurred in 28.1-30.1% of GPT-5-mini summaries, compared with 35.3-36.4% for Claude Sonnet 4.5 and 41.4-41.9% for MedGemma. Impression-removal edits were handled more robustly than term substitutions. Even for the highest-scoring summarizer, substitutions of anatomical terms were not reflected in 23.7% of summaries, diagnostic certainty in 22.3%, and descriptor terms (e.g., "substantial") in 21.2%. Judge-radiologist agreement (macro-F1: 0.55-0.56) was comparable to inter-radiologist agreement (0.53).
  Conclusions: Adversarial evaluation exposes clinically meaningful failure modes, including missed anatomical modifiers, altered diagnostic certainty, and omitted severity descriptors.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Safety-aligned language models are known to suffer degradation in refusal behavior following domain-specific fine-tuning, even when the fine-tuning data is entirely benign. The predominant mitigation strategy, preference optimization on the fine-tuned model, is computationally expensive and requires domain-specific preference data that is often unavailable in practice. We propose SPECTRA, a training-free, weight-level method that restores refusal behavior by transferring the base model's refusal direction into the fine-tuned model's weights via an activation-aware, low-rank update, requiring no gradient-based optimization or domain-specific safety data. The low-rank design restricts the intervention to the subspace most causally active on harmful inputs, leaving benign behavior largely undisturbed by construction. Empirically, SPECTRA substantially improves refusal behavior across a comprehensive safety evaluation, while preserving general coherence, domain-specific knowledge, and producing negligible change in false refusal rates.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can generate personalized recommendations without task-specific training, but changing a protected attribute while holding interaction history fixed can alter the semantic content of recommended items. We study fairness control when model weights and full-vocabulary logits are unavailable. We propose Fair- Rec, an item-level decoding framework that uses an offline-calibrated semantic-disparity threshold and controls candidate selection as recommendation lists are generated. A hard variant filters candidates against the threshold; a soft variant trades off candidate likelihood and disparity while adapting a penalty using observed violations. Under an explicit uniform enforceability assumption, we establish a pathwise O(1/T) upper bound on the excess empirical violation rate of the soft decoder. Experiments on MovieLens-1M and Amazon Movies & TV using Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B-Instruct show that the soft variant lowers measured counterfactual disparity relative to zero- =shot recommendation, UP5, and FACTER, while retaining competitive HR@10 and NDCG@10.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Knowledge graphs need validated relational triples, and gold benchmarks of such triples are often built by several annotators. Most text annotation platforms focus on span tagging and compute agreement on categorical labels. They are not designed for ordinal quality grading of triples that have already been extracted. We present TripleGrader, an open-source web platform for grading subject-predicate-object triples on a 0 to 10 ordinal scale. In TripleGrader, each triple is scored independently, and peer scores stay hidden until the grader has rated it. Graders then discuss entries in threaded comments and propose revisions, which the session owner approves and publishes as new versions. For agreement, the platform computes quadratic-weighted Cohen's kappa for two raters and a quadratic-weighted Fleiss-type kappa for three or more. Graded sessions and the final benchmark are exported in common formats. We evaluated the platform in three ways. We ran 72 automated tests, including 18 workflow scenarios through the REST API. We also injected 10 error types to check whether prescribed corrections could be applied while preserving unaffected triples. On two small corpora with existing gold benchmarks, we then used the platform to curate the triples extracted by four large language models into each benchmark. Only 10% to 32% of the candidate triples could be kept unchanged, and 53% to 71% needed corrections, partly to match the benchmarks' naming conventions. Per model, 11 to 29 missing triples had to be added. On these corpora, the extracted triples therefore needed substantial human review. The source code is available at https://github.com/razz0n/triple-grader-tool.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents increasingly drive systems research and rely on simulators to evaluate candidate designs at scale. Simulators are imperfect by design: their modeling limitations can distort predictions yet leave the recommended design correct, or change which design should be selected. We introduce Siris to test whether LLMs can tell these cases apart, judging from their systems understanding when a limitation invalidates the simulator's recommendation. By perturbing a simulator, Siris constructs workload pairs with the same candidates, limitation, and simulator recommendation. The perturbation changes predictions on both workloads, yet a reference simulator supports the recommendation on only one. Evaluating thirteen frontier LLMs on LLM serving design problems, we find that identifying a limitation does not ensure a correct choice, and even the strongest models sometimes abandon valid recommendations. Self-correction through a review turn helps these models restore valid choices but introduces new errors in several others. These findings motivate evaluating systems understanding through design decisions under imperfect simulators.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automated thin-section classifiers increasingly combine plane-polarised and cross-polarised light (PPL and XPL), two complementary views central to petrographic identification. Classification accuracy alone does not reveal whether model errors reflect mineralogical relationships, how predictions depend on each optical view, or whether attribution maps identify regions that influence model decisions. Here, we examine these complementary aspects of the released LITHOS dual-stream vision transformer using outputs from an earlier evaluation of 105,802 expert-annotated grains across 25 categories. Classification errors frequently followed the label taxonomy: 72.6\% of misclassified plagioclase, microcline and sanidine grains were assigned the generic Feldspar label, while 87.1\% of quartz-family grains received a label within that family, compared with 66.6\% assigned their exact label. Replacing the XPL input with its per-channel spatial means produced a greater reduction in true-class probability than equivalent PPL replacement in 20 of 25 categories, but not in the three quartz categories. Five explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) attribution procedures generated visually distinct maps for identical inputs. In a superpixel-deletion analysis involving 30 grains from 29 thin sections, method-minus-random contrasts ranged from $-0.043$ to $0.057$, with all 41 simultaneous 95\% confidence intervals including zero. Together, these findings show that error structure, modality sensitivity and attribution behaviour capture distinct aspects of model performance that cannot be established from classification accuracy or visual inspection of attribution maps alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Feature caching accelerates Diffusion Transformers by reusing intermediate computations across denoising timesteps, yet existing methods share a fundamental blind spot: they treat the standard reference denoising trajectory as the optimisation target, approximating its per-step states and thus inheriting its output as an implicit quality ceiling. We argue that this target is arbitrary -- what truly matters is the ideal result, not the intermediate path taken to reach it. We propose IdealCache, a training-free framework that abandons step-wise imitation of the reference trajectory. Instead, it anchors caching on a per-input ensemble of ideal trajectories constructed from candidate target results, aggregates their geometry into a single graph, and discovers optimal non-uniform schedules via a constrained shortest path on this graph. By decoupling from the reference process, IdealCache breaks the performance bottleneck of trajectory-approximation; our schedules can attain near-lossless fidelity in generation and even surpass on task-specific metrics (e.g., maintaining higher semantic consistency in image editing). Without retraining, IdealCache achieves 6.35x speedup on Qwen-Image, 5.84x on Qwen-Image-Edit, and 4.32x on HunyuanVideo, with competitive or superior quality across tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recommender systems are transitioning from static retrieval engines into interactive assistants grounded in physical environments. However, current studies largely neglect the combined complexities of spatial navigation, visual grounding, and multi-turn social interaction. To bridge this gap, we introduce SituRecBench, an interactive benchmark for evaluating frontier vision-language models (VLMs) as situated recommendation assistants within high-fidelity 3D environments. We reformulate recommendation as a situated assistance process in which an assistant interacts with a user while sharing the user's egocentric view. Our benchmark simulates real-world shopping environments (e.g., supermarkets and home furnishings stores), requiring assistants to elicit preferences, ground recommendations in the environment, and guide the user to relevant locations through dialogue. The benchmark evaluates dialogue quality, recommendation utility, and navigation effectiveness through a unified interactive protocol. Extensive experiments reveal persistent challenges in spatial grounding and preference elicitation, highlighting limitations in their ability to integrate global contextual information with local observations. SituRecBench provides a rigorous foundation for advancing the development of situated assistants in the real world.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate indoor localization on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging due to the tension between modeling fidelity and computational efficiency. Wi-Fi Channel State Information (CSI) enables low-cost deployment using existing Wi-Fi infrastructure but is heavily distorted by multipath propagation. Transformer-based solutions mitigate these effects but their quadratic complexity limits real-time edge deployment. Ultra-Wideband (UWB) provides geometrically precise ranging yet requires dedicated infrastructure. We present MambaLoc, a cross-modal distillation framework that improves the accuracy-efficiency balance in CSI-based localization. MambaLoc uses a linear-time Selective State Space Model (Mamba) as a lightweight CSI student, guided during training by a UWB-Transformer teacher. A Feature Alignment Module bridges the representational gap between heterogeneous RF modalities, allowing the CSI model to inherit UWB-derived geometric structure without UWB hardware at inference. A probabilistic Gaussian Mixture regression head models multi-modal spatial ambiguity from multipath effects, avoiding the physically implausible averaging of deterministic regression. Evaluations on a real-world dataset show MambaLoc achieves a median localization error of 0.230 m, reducing error by 22.2% over a standalone Mamba baseline, while requiring 65.1% fewer FLOPs and 58% lower inference latency than Transformer-based approaches.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Most temporal graph learning methods reduce future prediction to discriminative inference over past interactions, focusing on edges or node labels for a largely persistent set of nodes. This view breaks down when new nodes and edges appear, since the future graph becomes a new object with changing size, composition, and structure. We address this gap by framing temporal graph prediction as an inverse topology problem. Instead of predicting edges directly, we first predict a multiscale topological descriptor of the future graph and then reconstruct a plausible future snapshot that realizes this descriptor under inductive constraints. This approach makes global structure and node churn explicit prediction targets and produces forecasted graphs that downstream tasks can evaluate without retraining. Across 14 temporal graph datasets, we evaluate our method, TOPOGED, on node, link, and graph property prediction and compare it against state-of-the-art temporal graph models. TOPOGED significantly improves node-level forecasting accuracy over the strongest baseline, with a macro average of 0.49 versus 0.05. It also outperforms baselines in 60% of graph-structure metric evaluations and substantially increases macro-average edge prediction metrics, from near-zero to 0.12. Our results show that topology-guided graph forecasting can predict inductive future snapshots whose structure supports multiple downstream evaluations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When disasters strike, they not only damage infrastructure, buildings, and the economy, but also affect the mental health of affected populations. Research on the mental health effects of disasters has expanded rapidly and has become an important, well-funded area of psychology, public health, and related social sciences. However, the overall structure of this research landscape has not been systematically characterized. Using large language models, we conducted a review of all articles related to terms 'disaster' and 'mental health' indexed in PubMed. 7,859 articles' metadata were retrieved using the NCBI API, and then Claude Sonnet was used to extract structured variables from the abstracts. The results indicated that earthquakes and epidemics were the most commonly studied disasters while many other disasters such as heat and cold waves have received less attention. PTSD, depression, and anxiety were well investigated but many other mental health issues (e.g., aggression, suicide) are relatively ignored. Despite enjoying relatively large sample sizes (median = 452, IQR = 142 - 1,568), the field rarely utilizes machine learning algorithms. Hypothesis significance testing, reviews, qualitative methods, and structural equation modeling are respectively most common methods while agent-based modeling, machine learning, casual inference, and NLP each appear in less than 1% of articles. Studies are limited to a small number of countries, particularly the United States, Japan, and China, while many disaster-prone regions remain substantially underrepresented. This LLM-assisted computational map provides a broad overview of the disaster mental health literature while highlighting important methodological gaps, with particular emphasis on the underutilization of computational methods that could help advance the field.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Big techs and researchers increasingly claim that their training datasets are too large to inspect for bias and ethical problems. However, in the race to build ever-large models, they have relied on automated web scraping and no human curation. Datasets built this way absorb the stereotypes, exclusionary norms, and historical prejudices of their sources, and the models trained on them can reproduce and amplify those patterns. Because manually auditing billions of samples is humanly impossible, we need practical, effective methods to identify biases in these datasets. This paper studies the feasibility of ethically auditing large datasets through small samples and proposes a practical method for identifying structural biases by applying AI Ethics Tools (AIETs) to random samples. We applied Datasheets for Datasets, Data Statements, Data Nutrition Labels, and Data Cards to a 1% sample of Framed Multi30K, and Datasheets for Datasets to two 0.00006% samples of relaion2B-en-research-safe. Our results show that evaluating small samples with AIETs can reveal ethical problems, structural biases, and technical inconsistencies. Although industry practice assumes that identifying problems requires analyzing the entire dataset, we show that a random subset supports a viable qualitative analysis without overwhelming computational or human resources. Demonstrating that a dataset is biased does not require examining every instance: if a bias appears in a random sample, it is a flaw of the creation process. The AIETs' questions acted as provocateurs, evoking different perspectives and analysis methods for the same dataset, but the analysis and its results depended fundamentally on the human factor. We hope these findings contribute to urgent public discussions about ethics, transparency, accountability, and AI regulation by offering a practical pathway for responsibly auditing massive datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM-based agents extend LLMs with tool calls, memory, planning, and retrieval to act in external environments, but they are vulnerable to attacks at different workflow steps. Existing defenses, including prompt engineering and post-training alignment, primarily improve LLM-only safety and often overlook risks that arise from multi-step execution. External verification adds guardrails to check each step separately, but it typically ignores how risks can propagate across steps. We argue that we should consider risk transitions across dependent steps through the agent workflow, rather than separate verification on a single step. In this paper, we propose \textbf{T}ail-risk-aware \textbf{A}gent \textbf{S}afety (TAS), a tail-risk-aware safety framework that models agent workflow safety as a sequence of dependent safety states and constrains the rare but extreme risks (i.e., tail risk) with a shared budget. TAS first models safety-state transitions sequentially along the workflow as an MDP to trace risk propagation, then measures a tail-sensitive risk to target rare but high-cost risks (i.e., tail risk), and constrains the risk with a shared budget to derive a step-wise safety strategy. By constraining tail risk rather than the overall average, TAS suppresses rare high-cost failures while preserving utility in normal cases. Experiments show that TAS achieves better performance compared with eleven baselines across three benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed through conversational interfaces, yet strong single-turn performance does not imply reliability in multi-turn settings. Multi-turn assistance requires a model to maintain an evolving representation of user requirements and to reason over it before complying with later requests. Failures can therefore be subtle: models may fail to recognize a changed constraint or hidden conflict, or recognize it without enforcing it in the final response. Existing benchmarks primarily test fully specified prompts, long-context recall, or controlled domain environments, leaving open-domain requirement reconciliation undermeasured. We introduce TurnFlow, an interactive benchmark for evaluating whether models preserve, revise, and act on user requirements across turns. TurnFlow combines adaptive user simulation with verifiable judging, allowing conversations to unfold naturally while keeping hidden requirements and pass/fail criteria controlled. In our evaluation, nine LLMs achieve overall pass rates from 42.86% to 59.59% on 490 scenarios. Performance varies substantially across challenge types: models perform well on Stateful Multi-Constraint Following but remain considerably less reliable on Scope Creep and Goal Shift. These results reveal persistent limitations in reasoning over evolving user requirements across turns.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Semantic response caching reuses a cached response for a semantically similar query to improve LLM inference efficiency. However, query similarity alone can lead to unsafe reuse when safety conditions change. Existing approaches improve cache safety through stricter similarity checks, context-aware validation, or response verification, yet they may still miss safety conflicts between the cached response and the new request or introduce unnecessary verification overhead. We argue that safe and efficient semantic caching requires a reuse policy that makes the cached response's safety conditions explicit and uses them to guide the reuse decision with a balance between safety and utility. In this paper, we propose SafeCache, a safety-aware framework that separates candidate retrieval from safety approval and formulates semantic response reuse as a safety-conditioned routing problem. SafeCache matches each cached-response condition against the new request and aggregates the per-condition mismatches into an overall risk score. A separate contradiction guard prevents explicit conflicts from being diluted by strong matches. It further estimates matching uncertainty and calibrates the routing policy under a required unsafe-reuse target and a verifier-call budget to choose among direct reuse, selective verification, or regeneration. Experiments show that SafeCache achieves the better safety-utility trade-off among the evaluated baselines, enabling safer response reuse at comparable cache utility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Speculative decoding efficiency depends on the number of consecutively accepted draft tokens, yet token-level training objectives do not directly optimize this quantity. We introduce D-REAL (Drafter Reinforcement of Expected Acceptance Length), a post-training method that directly optimizes expected acceptance length for a fixed decoding context. D-REAL weights position-wise draft-target distributional overlap by prefix survival probabilities along a draft-sampled prefix, faithfully capturing the sequential dependence of verification on earlier proposals. By accounting for the parameter dependence of the proposal distribution, we derive an unbiased gradient estimator for this single-round objective under stochastic verification. We evaluate D-REAL on DSpark and DFlash2 with Qwen3-8B as the target model across ten benchmarks. Starting from baselines whose mean acceptance length has plateaued, one epoch of D-REAL post-training consistently improves the benchmark-averaged mean acceptance length by up to 0.23 tokens across both architectures under greedy (T = 0.0) and stochastic (T = 1.0) sampling, translating into up to a 9.4% end-to-end serving throughput improvement.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) aims to discover informative patterns in large datasets. For transactional data, Association Rule Mining (ARM) is an established approach that facilitates EDA by efficiently generating candidate patterns. Beyond efficient mining, identifying informative rules has also been extensively studied through both objective and subjective interestingness measures. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer new opportunities for assessing subjective interestingness using world knowledge. Nevertheless, relying solely on LLMs raises concerns about scalability and statistical reliability. Building on prior ARM research, we propose a hybrid framework, DiSARM, that leverages the world knowledge of LLMs to assess the subjective interestingness of rules, while maintaining statistical reliability and promoting diversity among the selected rules. Across three transactional datasets, experiments demonstrate that DiSARM effectively combines statistical reliability with surprise and diversity, offering a promising approach to ADE from transactional data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) rollouts interleave model reasoning with tool calls in persistent workspaces. Scaling these rollouts incurs substantial memory costs for concurrent environments, while slow tool calls delay entire rollout batches. We present BunshinRL, an agentic RL rollout system that exploits shared environment state and recurring tool calls through three techniques: memory sharing across environments, tool-prefix caching, and speculative tool execution. These techniques build on BunshinFork, a novel live whole-workspace forking mechanism for containers. BunshinFork reconstructs process trees, shares memory copy-on-write, and preserves file, communication, and GUI state, enabling cached-state reuse and rollback after incorrect speculation. We implement BunshinRL and BunshinFork through Linux kernel module and CRIU modifications. BunshinRL reduces peak memory by up to 43%, total memory consumption by up to 51%, and end-to-end rollout latency by 23%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study decentralised drone formation, without a central planner, under the conditions that make it hard: nobody knows how many drones are alive, failures are silent, radio is short-ranged and lossy, and actuation is noisy. In our controller, ant colony optimisation (ACO) allocates drones to picture positions through claims that behave as reinforced, evaporating pheromone and are gossiped between neighbours, so evaporation doubles as failure detection. A particle swarm optimisation (PSO) law flies the drones, a noise-buffered Voronoi filter keeps them apart, and PSO also tunes the controller offline. We prove that claims of destroyed drones vanish within bounded time, that conflicts between drones that exchange tables are resolved consistently, that survivors cover the picture to within a factor of two, and that drones cannot collide while their safety cells are feasible. On held-out seeds and with two tuned configurations, the controller formed the picture in 95-99% of runs in the hardest regime, restored it after a silent loss of half the swarm in 96-100% of runs at normal difficulty, transferred unchanged to eight unseen shapes and to 300 drones, and overlapped in 5 of 21,088 runs. A CBBA-style baseline that shares the evaporating claims but uses argmax without swaps was at least as reliable as ours once tuned with the same budget, and repaired faster, whereas ours generalised better to unseen shapes. The benefit thus comes from the pheromone dynamics and from tuning, not from ACO's stochastic construction or PSO's social terms. A centralised oracle is faster.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents need to choose actions whose consequences unfold over multiple future steps, yet poor decisions do not reveal whether the model failed to represent those consequences or failed to use them. Mechanistic interpretability has studied internal representations of environment state and plans, but sequential decision making also requires evaluating how candidate actions change future outcomes. Reinforcement learning formalizes this as action value. We study this computation in Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, and GPT-OSS-20B across a partially observed Grid environment and WebShop, a simulated web shopping environment. We find that the relative values of candidate actions are linearly decodable from intermediate hidden states, including at states where the policy prefers a worse action. We call this gap between value representation and policy choice value-policy dissociation. To investigate how represented value relates to action selection, we compare direct interventions along the value readout direction with Natural Value Transport (NVT), a transformation learned from hidden state changes that naturally result from differences in action value. While readout-direction interventions have limited and inconsistent effects, NVT more consistently influences action preferences, particularly at later layers. We also test a closed-loop controller that uses the value readout as a sensor and NVT as an actuator, observing improvements in task outcomes. These results suggest that LLM agents can represent useful prospective value without reliably translating it into policy choice.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement-learning-trained agents for retrieval-augmented generation retrieve content (passages, knowledge-graph fragments, or hyperedges) and supervise training with outcome rewards on the final answer. Across these paradigms the reasoning route through retrieved content never leaves the language model's hidden state, conflating declarative knowledge with the procedural knowledge that recurs across queries of the same logical type; content-only methods consequently plateau as reasoning depth grows. We introduce PatternBloom, an agentic RAG framework that shifts the unit of retrieval from content (what to read) to logic (how to reason). A first RL stage scores the agent's constructed evidence graph by a frozen oracle's information gain through the Information-Density Reward (IDR), a size-normalized mutual-information surrogate. High-reward trajectories are distilled into a Graph Pattern Memory (GPM) of type-abstracted reasoning skeletons, and a second RL stage uses the Pattern-Augmented Reward (PAR) to couple policy to memory, turning procedural reasoning into a learnable, externalized structural prior. A 7B PatternBloom outperforms $27$ baselines by $+15.0$ Avg-OOD EM, with the gain compounding with reasoning depth precisely because procedural knowledge, once distilled, is reused rather than re-derived at every query. We release our code at https://github.com/EllenYiGe/PatternBloom.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents are increasingly used for open-ended tasks, very common in research scenarios where they are required to explore, investigate, or act curiously. At inference time, an LLM is a fixed map from context to next-token distribution, the pretrained weights determine the function, and only its conditioning changes as context accumulates. Mechanisms like temperature, best-of-N, tree search, or RAG vary how the distribution is sampled or what it is conditioned on, but do not update the agent's model of the environment. We argue that current LLM agents do not explore curiously; they explore stochastically. From how curiosity is understood in the RL literature, it requires a predictive structure over how the environment responds to the agent's actions, which improves online from current interaction, drives action selection through its predicted improvement, and persists beyond the context window. Additionally, in its open-ended form, the agent should intrinsically originate what to investigate. Thus, we propose a framework defining curiosity in LLM agents, reserving the term for systems that satisfy it. In doing so, we identify the research directions its implementation opens up.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large-scale retail supply chains need sales forecasts for specified products, future dates or planning windows, and regions under the conditions available at the forecast origin. Regional sparsity, changing environments, volatile sales, and unfamiliar business situations make these dimensions difficult to model together. We introduce TimeHF 2.0, a multimodal, multivariate, and multi-region collaborative forecasting framework that makes flexibly expressed business knowledge usable in numerical prediction. A coverage-aware directed encoder integrates regional sales and applicable covariates. A semantic alignment module transfers language-model hidden representations of natural-language plans and domain experience into target-specific forecasting context. Learning against sales observations connects these meanings to numerical evidence, allowing business users to supply experience, future plans, and changing circumstances without defining a new numerical feature for each description. Text-conditioned future queries and shared/routed experts adapt prediction to the supplied context. An ordered-distribution head allocates fine resolution to routine fluctuations and coarse resolution to extreme sales movements, while daily/window learning connects forecasts to cumulative planning quantities. Online parallel forecasting at JD yields relative accuracy improvements of 18.25% and 17.94% for national daily and planning-window sales, and 19.44% and 18.99% for DC daily and planning-window sales, over the original production deep learning baseline. Component comparisons, loss-design results, and product-type analyses examine the roles of regional information, covariates, business knowledge, context-dependent processing, and robust planning-oriented learning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Today's large language models (LLMs) increasingly interact with time: AI agents schedule tasks, coordinate with people and other AI systems, and even decide how long to think. Yet LLMs only ever encounter time secondhand---in descriptions rather than experience. Does this hinder their reasoning and decisions that involve time? Our paper traces this question from a model's knowledge to its actions through four successive evaluations. We find that models know the rules of time, which can be read, but struggle to predict durations, which must be lived; while they are highly accurate (92.0-97.5\%) on a collection of questions built from the theorems of various time-related literatures, they underestimate their own completion times by up to 99.5\% and fail to identify which tasks will take them longer to run. And while they are better at estimating durations of human tasks, their duration judgments change based on how an event is worded, magnifying similar human biases by up to 1.3$\times$. Moving from estimation to action, we find that some LLMs disregard time costs completely when answering user queries, ignoring a 1,200-fold increase in wait time entirely. Together, our experiments reveal new challenges posed to LLMs as they navigate a dimension they cannot directly perceive, and highlight how their blind spots can even be simple cognitive abilities that humans take for granted.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inherit strong textual reasoning capabilities from their language backbones and text pretraining, but often fail to apply the same level of competency to visual inputs. We show that this degradation cannot be attributed to only perception failures, by evaluating models on semantically-equivalent text and rendered-text image inputs: models can accurately read text in the images and recover near-text performance when provided their own transcriptions, but just cannot reason well on the images directly. We characterize this discrepancy as a modality reasoning-access gap, where models fail to route perceived visual content into existing reasoning machinery accessible with text. To bridge this, we propose TIGER (Text-to-Image Gap-targeted training for Enhanced Reasoning), which first mines synthetic modality counterfactuals where a model succeeds on a text problem but fails when provided the corresponding rendered image, and post-trains the model on the image input via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), along with SFT and DPO variants. Importantly, although trained exclusively on synthetic rendered-text images with no multimodal annotations, TIGER results in model performance improvements across diverse real-world visual benchmarks including geometric diagrams (MathVerse), multi-disciplinary sciences (EMMA, MMMU), complex charts (ChartQAPro), and embodied spatial reasoning (RoboSpatial-Home). TIGER also complements existing state-of-the-art RLVR visual reasoning methods, yielding further performance gains. Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/minhngh/Tiger.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Angle Power Spectrum (APS) and Power Delay Profile (PDP) are receiver-facing spectra that govern beam selection and wideband equalization. Most ray-tracing benchmarks provide supervision as variable-size multipath component lists. This format does not match practical receivers, whose angular and delay resolutions are limited by array aperture and system bandwidth. As a result, learning targets are ambiguous and evaluations can change with reconstruction conventions. We introduce UrbanADPSMap, a benchmark that converts ray-traced supervision into fixed-grid APS and PDP targets by explicitly modeling bandwidth-limited and aperture-limited response kernels. By fixing the receiver-domain kernels, discretization, and validity mask, UrbanADPSMap removes reconstruction-induced target ambiguity and stabilizes downstream evaluation across methods. Built on this benchmark, we propose SpecFlowAR, a coarse-to-fine generator based on multi-scale autoregressive flow matching. The model first captures coarse dispersion structure and then refines beam-relevant peaks and low-power tails. The number of function evaluations controls the sampling budget, enabling latency and quality to be adjusted for dense spectrum queries. Experiments on the test set show that SpecFlowAR reduces BPL@5 to 2.03 dB under a unified NFE=80 budget, while improving beam utility and reducing errors of 3GPP dispersion statistics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When agents organize records into folders, they may correctly group the excerpts of a design proposal yet place the proposal under the wrong owning group. Existing agent benchmarks do not directly measure such higher-level errors. We introduce InfoOrgBench, a benchmark of 150 tasks in which agents organize scattered records from meeting recaps, issue threads and design proposals into hierarchies. Integrity checks verify that every record is preserved, and a Structure Score measures agreement with the source hierarchy at every level, independent of folder names and order. We evaluate 45 systems that pair nine models with five agent harnesses. The best system scores 83.2 out of 100, but no system exceeds 78 on meeting recaps or issue threads. Model choice affects scores far more than harness choice: model means span about 27 points, whereas the average effect of each harness is below one point. More importantly, parent-level relations have higher error rates than leaf-level relations in all three domains. In design proposals, nearly half of the outputs analyzed by level make exactly this kind of error. These results show that evaluating information organization requires measuring higher-level relations separately from record preservation and local grouping.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The design of manufacturing systems relies on discrete-event simulation (DES) models that experts still build in tool-specific interfaces. AI agents can now write simulation code, but every model they write from scratch is new, unverified software. We present SimuLean, an open-source 3D DES simulator on the Unity engine designed to be programmed by AI agents: its modelling semantics are verified once, in the platform, and the agent builds and studies one model through domain-level commands, in the 3D scene or headless; physically simulated durations are calibrated for fast headless studies. We evaluated it on 18 manufacturing modelling tasks, from single machines to published production lines. A frontier agent built valid models in 93 of 108 attempts (86%), in about two minutes each; on the benchmark's interface version, two frontier agents built 105 of 108 models through domain commands alone, so that each comes with a record of how it was built. Every residual failure was silent - a model that builds and runs but answers wrongly - and only comparison with reference models revealed them all. In an industrial case, one agent working alone rebuilt, verified and studied a robotised welding workshop from a published study, in SimuLean and in SimPy. Comparing them with the published, expert-built model exposed an error in it; against the corrected model, the agent-built models agree within 1%. One agent can already build complete, realistic and traceable models for the design of manufacturing systems; independent reference models are what make them trustworthy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) inference serving is undergoing rapid growth and large-scale deployment, motivating us to rethink how the inference process itself can be leveraged to enable efficient task-specific LLM adaptation. In this paper, we propose a forward-only approach for efficient LLM adaptation with forward-only passes in LLM inference serving. We exploit the angle concentration of activations induced by each singular value decomposition (SVD) component to measure its contribution-dispersed or concentrated-to the angle concentration of the hidden states. Based on this, our forward-only search then efficiently identifies the weight matrix with the highest dispersion that merits rank reduction. We then selectively remove the higher-order components and retain the lower-order components in SVD. Empirical results across diverse datasets demonstrate the competitive accuracy of our forward-only approach, while theoretical analysis shows lower peak memory and greater speedup than the gradient-based approach and greater speedup than the exhaustive search approach. The experiments further present the robustness and generalization of our forward-only approach to various LLMs with up to 57B parameters. The code is included in the supplementary material.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When an LLM agent's backend model is upgraded or replaced, operators typically reload the same persona, memory, and rules onto the new model and assume the agent will behave as before. We show this assumption requires empirical validation-and that whether identity is preserved depends on the specific model pairing. Across 5,760 compliance-testing runs in 12 regulated domains with three open-weight models, we find that some transitions produce significant behavioral drift while others do not, even when the agent's external state remains identical. Crucially, this drift is invisible to verbal verification: every model claims full policy compliance, yet actual decisions under pressure differ across models. The same external state can reorder which model is most compliant-a large positive effect on one substrate, negligible on the other two. Practitioners should treat model transitions as behavioral changes requiring action-level empirical testing, not as transparent infrastructure operations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents for first-principles materials simulation. We report empirical findings from DFT-Agent Bench, a benchmark of 200+ Quantum ESPRESSO tasks spanning relaxation, electronic structure, equation-of-state, and elastic-tensor pipelines over multiple material tiers. Each task carries a QE-derived ground truth with public-reference cross-checks where available, and a provenance audit checks whether the required QE execution contract was met. Under one execution harness, we compare a near-bare ReAct baseline with a bundled v5.1 scaffold on two models. The complete scaffold improves bands+DOS by +0.08/+0.11 and d-electron vc-relax by +0.17 on both models, while high-scoring relaxation and equation-of-state cells leave little room for improvement. The elastic result is model-dependent (0.18 to 0.40 for deepseek-v4-pro and 0.62 to 0.59 for claude-opus-4-8). A fixed 100-task analysis with retrieval disabled supports the aggregate pattern: the fully frozen DeepSeek comparison gains +0.17, while the Opus comparison gains +0.10 against a historical pool baseline (p < 0.001). The provenance gate catches incomplete or unmarked executions that can receive high rubric scores; it is an execution-contract check, not a test for intentional fraud. These results characterize the evaluated DFT workflows. The anonymous review artifact contains the frozen diagnostic slice, prompts, scorer, and solver revisions: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/231e31re91287h/README.md.

</details>
