Abstract (英文): Deep reinforcement learning (RL) is often used to decide where Internet of Things (IoT) tasks with different latency limits run in the edge-cloud continuum, yet some methods give every missed task the same penalty and few compare their agents with an optimal policy. This benchmark study builds four offloading systems small enough to solve exactly by dynamic programming and measures reward and learner against the optimum. A bonus for a met limit makes each miss cost its penalty plus the bonus, which shifts the class weights of published per-class rewards. A class-blind penalty stays 65% to 118% above the optimal cost even with a perfect learner, while a corrected per-class penalty comes within 0.13% of the optimum. With a per-class reward, tuned proximal policy optimization (PPO) agents come within 9% to 11% of the optimum in the smallest system, and the corrected one beats a tuned reservation rule in 9 of 10 seeds. In larger systems the agents stay 41% to 76% above the optimum, and a tuned rule beats their mean. Training ten times longer does not close this gap, and a PPO-Lagrangian agent with reachable targets does no better than the per-class reward.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Driven by increasingly complex real-world applications, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has evolved from textual pipelines to multimodal settings, comprising two complementary dimensions: knowledge organization and retrieval enhancement. However, existing methods still face challenges of high construction cost, context window limitations, and the lack of unified training across offline and online knowledge sources. To address these challenges, we propose MMGraph-Agent, a unified multimodal RAG framework based on cache-inspired multimodal knowledge hypergraphs. MMGraph-Agent enables extremely lightweight hypergraph construction with zero API cost and dynamic memory-based retrieval that alleviates long-context issues. Experiments across offline, online, and joint retrieval settings demonstrate consistent improvements in performance, efficiency, and architectural unification. Abstract (英文): Cellular world models aim to predict how cells respond to genetic perturbations, analogous to world models in reinforcement learning. Perturbation response models are evaluated almost exclusively by global Pearson correlation between predicted and observed transcriptomes. We show this metric is dominated by thousands of non-responsive genes and severely inflates apparent performance. On the Replogle K562 screen (509 unseen perturbations, 5000 genes), GEARS achieves global Pearson 0.977, but its delta Pearson is only 0.076. A trivial baseline predicting mean training delta achieves 0.267, which is 3.5x better than GEARS. Our primary contribution is methodological: we identify and quantify this delta gap as a systematic evaluation artifact, and propose delta-space evaluation as the primary benchmark. We provide two post-hoc corrections (RWR on co-expression graphs and kNN matching) and show that 2.8x of the remaining gap lies in perturbation representation geometry, not decoder capacity. We further show the gap is partially addressable end-to-end: delta-aligned training (lambda = 3.0) raises delta Pearson to 0.179 on Replogle (2.35x over GEARS) and surpasses the trivial baseline on direction accuracy (0.680 vs 0.567) and gene recovery (1.52 vs 1.19); combined with kNN it reaches 0.217. An amplitude-calibration variant (eta = 0.3) further raises gene recovery to 2.16 while reaching 0.218. Critically, these representations transfer zero-shot across cell lines: from Replogle K562 to unseen Norman A549, the delta-aligned model reaches 0.328 on 741 shared genes, 4.7x a trivial baseline and 2.1x standard GEARS. This is the first systematic quantification of the delta gap, and the first diagnostic framework localizing the residual gap to perturbation representation geometry via an oracle upper bound.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Document parsing benchmarks are often treated as stable measures of model quality, yet rankings may depend substantially on benchmark design choices. In this work, we present RankShift, a systematic study comparing OCR and vision-language models (VLMs) under controlled variation in dataset composition, matching strategy, and scoring methodology. Using OmniDocBench as a case study, we evaluate the ranking stability of 16 models across 3 dataset configurations, 6 matching strategies, and 3 scoring metrics. We quantify rank stability using pairwise rank correlation, top-k churn, and pairwise reversal rate. Our results show that the interaction between matching strategy and ground truth format is a substantial but understudied driver of instability: scoring against OmniDocBench's JSON annotations versus its Markdown rendering yields a mean Kendall's tau of approximately 0.33 under region-level matching strategies, with individual pairs as low as tau = 0.23, compared with tau = 0.77 when each page is scored as a single concatenated string. Dataset composition produces comparable instability across page-type strata (mean tau = 0.48) and layout-type strata (mean tau = 0.49), while the cross-dataset shift from OmniDocBench to Real5's Scanning reconstruction of the same pages is more stable (tau = 0.80). Metric choice is the most stable dimension, with mean tau of approximately 0.78 across NED, BLEU, and METEOR. These findings indicate that leaderboard position is not solely a property of model capability, motivating leaderboards that report not only headline scores but also the robustness of rankings across reasonable design choices.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Post-hoc merging of public LoRA adapters is an attractive gradient-free path to continual adaptation, but reliable measurement of catastrophic forgetting in this setting depends on infrastructure that prior work has not addressed. We treat each adapter merge as a sequential task in a continual-learning setting, with the instruction-tuned base model's capabilities as Task 0, and study forgetting on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Public adapter hubs contain modules that fail basic provenance checks: silent base-version mismatches and near-zero delta norms whose inclusion inflates measured interference by ~10pp. We propose a two-check audit (base-model match, non-trivial delta norm) and a leakage-free probing protocol as preconditions for any forgetting study in this setting. On an audited 4-adapter set, naive merging causes -10.5pp on GSM8K and -18.3pp on HumanEval, while Task Arithmetic at lambda=0.5 sits within a standard error of the unmerged baseline. A coordinate search including negative coefficients identifies the math adapter as probe-optimal at lambda=-0.5, outside the reach of positive-only mergers, suggesting forgetting has structure that negation-aware merging may exploit. The same protocol replicates on Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, where 2 of 4 candidate adapters fail the audit and the audited merge reproduces the same merge-method ranking on GSM8K, supporting that our findings are not Llama-specific.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Retinal screening programmes need to know which automated decisions can be accepted without review. This paper presents RetinaGuard, an agentic triage framework in which quality, diagnostic, uncertainty, verification and explanation agents gather evidence for each fundus photograph, a learned failure predictor estimates the probability that the decision is unsafe, auxiliary tools are invoked only when their expected value of information exceeds their cost, and the acceptance threshold is certified by conformal risk control. On the RFMiD test split, RetinaGuard was compared with confidence and calibrated-uncertainty referral at matched referral budgets and equal certified safety, using two RETFound primary models of different strength. With the weaker model (macro-AUROC 0.896), verifier disagreement added significant information, RetinaGuard captured 119 of 280 unsafe decisions at a 20% budget against 88 for lowest-confidence referral, and at a nominal 5% unsafe level it accepted 37.0% of cases against 10.3%. With the stronger model (0.925) the verifier added nothing beyond uncertainty and the advantage shrank to a few percentage points. In both settings its coverage at the nominal 5% level stayed near 37.7%, gating cut computation by about 30%, and a quality agent reduced unsafe acceptances under most image corruptions. The benefit of agentic evidence gathering thus depends on how much the primary model's own uncertainty already reveals about its errors.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Selective unlearning is increasingly important for the safety and compliance of large language models. Harmful or memorized content is often entangled with useful knowledge and difficult to enumerate. We use the model's own failures to identify candidates for unlearning. We propose Hallucination-Guided Unlearning (HGU), which uses hallucinations as diagnostic signals. HGU localizes unsupported spans via evidence and internal consensus, traces a sparse set of high-responsibility MLP neurons, and edits them with compact gates under a utility-preserving KL constraint. On TOFU, HGU achieves the best MU-FE trade-off, with Avg scores of 0.7221 (forget05) and 0.7699 (forget10), and the highest SepS H-Avg (0.4382/0.4715) under mixed forget/retain prompts. On the WMDP auxiliary task, HGU yields the strongest forgetting (e.g., 14.2 on Economics) while improving Retain (52.7) and MMLU (57.2). These results suggest that tracing hallucinations enables targeted, post-hoc unlearning that better balances forgetting efficacy and general utility without full retraining.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluations of AI systems usually report properties of a model, while decisions to deploy concern a configured system: a model together with prompts, tools, permissions, data, safeguards and an operating context. We ask how evidence about technical failures, deployment-control effectiveness and real-world consequences can be connected to defensible authorization decisions throughout the lifecycle of a deployed system. We present R-BED (Risk-Based Evaluability Design), a specification-level methodology with a fixed 6+4+3 architecture of thirteen diagnostic dimensions (technical failure, deployment controls, consequence and impact); a failure-mediated expected-loss identity that is kept separate from intended-behavior and misuse pathways; bounded evidence statuses with independence levels; a control assurance ladder; five authorization dispositions governed by eight rules; and a Continuous System Evaluation (CSE) layer in which evidence carries a configuration pin, an expiry and declared dependencies, so that a change invalidates evidence in proportion rather than wholesale. We work through a synthetic refund-agent walkthrough, with a deterministic script that reproduces every number, to show how a task-success view can miss a consequential control weakness that the procedure identifies as missing evidence. The contribution is a reproducible methodological specification. Most components are standard practice integrated into one evidence-to-decision record; the Evidence Validity Register, the authority envelope and the tier rules are proposals. None has been empirically validated, and we state seven hypotheses for independent testing.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pre-training is where every foundation model begins; bringing recursive self-improvement (RSI) there would let AI improve the process that creates AI. Yet public RSI work has so far targeted only post-training, which starts from an existing base model, runs trials costing GPU-hours, and returns verifiable scores. Pre-training breaks all three conditions at orders-of-magnitude higher cost: a run consumes hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours over months and cannot be repeated; its objective, post-training headroom, cannot be read off any single score; and what must improve is not a model but the whole production system, from data pipelines and training infrastructure to the teams that run them. To our knowledge, we are the first to apply RSI to an entire frontier pre-training run from scratch and to report it in detail. RSI does not stop at frontier pre-training, but it changes form: since no end-to-end loop can close over a single unrepeatable run, we decompose it into smaller loops that can, each repairing a step the full run breaks. The result is 801-30B-A3B-Base, a 30B-A3B MoE trained from scratch on 11.8T tokens in three months by a newly formed team. We release it as a base model; the lightweight SFT reported here (6B tokens, no RL) only probes post-training headroom. As a base model, 801-30B-A3B outperforms Qwen3-30B-A3B and Nemotron-3.5-Lightning-30B-A3B, trailing only the larger Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, and keeps this position under identical SFT; with lightweight SFT alone, it achieves the highest average on four agentic benchmarks against fully post-trained models, including Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Since post-training RSI presupposes a strong base model, bringing RSI into pre-training acts on the foundation every later stage builds on. We open-source 801-30B-A3B-Base and the lightweight SFT data at https://github.com/GAIR-NLP/801-30B .

</details>

Abstract (英文): A brain is in one state at a time, yet most of what decides its next state is absent from it: the patterns its world never produces, the statistics of a lifetime, a goal that lies ahead. In mechanics a wire acts on a bead only through a force that appears when the bead presses on it, and Lagrange computed such forces with one multiplier for each constraint. We propose that a brain follows the same mechanics. Everything a circuit knows, is told or wants is a constraint on its single state and acts through its own multiplier, which accumulates the violation and pushes the state back. Neurons and synapses carry the multipliers, each with its own time constant: inhibitory interneurons carry what never happens, adaptation the cost of staying in one state, synapses the statistics of experience, and feedback to apical dendrites the future. From this hypothesis follow Dale's law, the reciprocal wiring and balance of excitation and inhibition, the paradoxical responses of cortex and the frequencies of brain rhythms. Belief becomes the time one trajectory spends in each state, learning becomes sleep proposing laws and waking refuting the false ones, and planning becomes a wave from the goal meeting a wave from the present. Connectionism and symbolism are the limits of frozen and of instantaneous multipliers, and between them the time a circuit runs becomes the depth of its computation. A machine built on these principles, trained without gradients on 1000 solved puzzles, solves all 422,786 Sudoku-Extreme test puzzles with one trajectory each. It rediscovers the rules of Sudoku by sleeping and waking, plans shortest paths in mazes of 64 times the training area, and tracks permutations exactly on strings 25 times longer than in training, where Transformers fail. A mind, on this view, is life that learns its own constraints.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Stroke is a disorder of the circulation, yet the haemodynamic state that immediately precedes it has rarely been observed, because continuous physiological recordings before a stroke are seldom available. Photoplethysmography (PPG), recorded by every pulse oximeter and by hundreds of millions of wearable devices, captures the peripheral pulse beat by beat, but its morphology is largely discarded. Here we ask whether pulse waveform morphology encodes haemodynamic signatures of impending stroke. Using in-hospital stroke as a natural experiment that provides continuous PPG before physician-adjudicated stroke recognition, we studied intensive-care (MIMIC-III, $n=176$) and emergency-department (MC-MED, $n=154$) cohorts and described each beat with interpretable descriptors of pulse timing, amplitude, contour and variability. At a nominal 360-min horizon, morphology separated pre-recognition from stable windows with $F_1$ scores of 0.941 and 0.989 (sensitivity 0.998 and 0.980), with models transferred unchanged to the second cohort, and outperformed heart rate alone on identical windows. The signature was dominated by systolic crest time and its change from each patient's own baseline, feature attribution was concordant across cohorts (Spearman $ρ=0.865$), and morphology recorded in the first two hours of care stratified the subsequent clinical course ($F_1$ 0.714 and 0.691). In high-risk patients without stroke, 29% and 14% of windows were positive, but five-window persistence rules limited alerts to 17% and 2% of patients. The results demonstrate that the routinely recorded pulse thus carries haemodynamic imprint of the period preceding stroke, positioning pulse morphology as a candidate continuous cerebrovascular biomarker.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown promise as interactive agents, yet precise, programmatic 3D graphics editing remains difficult to train because existing systems largely rely on closed-source model APIs and lack deterministic, state-verifiable execution environments. We present BlenderFORGE, a trainable data-environment-optimization framework for open-weight MLLM agents that edit explicit Blender scenes through Blender Python (bpy) code generation. BlenderFORGE targets four fundamental state-verifiable editing task families: object placement, shape-key editing, lighting adjustment, and material modification. It integrates three components: (1) a scalable perturbation-based data generation pipeline built from curated assets and large-scale 3D scene repositories; (2) an isolated Blender Sandbox for deterministic code execution, structured state export, and closed-loop reward computation; and (3) a two-stage training pipeline that uses offline teacher trajectories for cold-start Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and sandbox-computed rewards for multi-turn tool-agentic Reinforcement Learning (AgentLoop-RL). Experiments across the four task families show that the resulting open-weight Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B BlenderFORGE agents substantially improve over their base models and achieve competitive performance with several zero-shot proprietary MLLM baselines on selected metrics. Additional cross-benchmark evaluations and camera-reframing experiments provide evidence of transfer beyond the original training setting.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Search allows a robotic policy to evaluate candidate futures before committing to an action. Motivated by test-time planning for vision-language action (VLA) models, we study selective search over action chunks in PushT using a frozen Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) policy. Beam search generates perturbed alternatives around the policy prediction and scores them through rollouts from cloned simulator states. Across 50 evaluation episodes, beam search raises task success from 0% to 18% and increases average cumulative reward from 39.44 to 103.2. We introduce One Step Ahead (OSA), a gate that simulates the nominal policy chunk and invokes full search only when its predicted coverage drop exceeds a threshold. In the reported ablations, OSA preserves or improves success while reducing runtime by approximately 3x relative to full beam search. Ablations over tree depth, branching factor, beam width, and replanning frequency show that larger search budgets do not consistently improve performance. The results support selective test-time search for action-chunking policies; evaluation on language-conditioned VLAs and physical robots remains future work.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hierarchical Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (H-JEPA) enable long-horizon visual control but rely on costly test-time gradient descent for planning. Our analysis reveals that iterative optimization consumes up to 97.7% of episode runtime and that low predicted rollout costs do not necessarily translate into successful control. We further identify a planning-horizon asymmetry between long-horizon top-level composition and short two-step subordinate planning. To address these challenges, we propose WARP-JEPA, a hierarchical amortized planning framework that replaces online gradient optimization with learned multi-candidate trajectory proposers while keeping the world models frozen. WARP-JEPA combines a lightweight world-model-in-the-loop causal Transformer at the top level with parallel residual MLPs at subordinate levels. The proposers are trained using model-based rollout costs and best-of-K behavior cloning, enabling efficient candidate selection without test-time gradient optimization. Across 30 frozen H-JEPA models on four benchmarks, WARP-JEPA achieves 9.6-18.8x faster episode inference on long-horizon tasks (2.9-3.0x on Cube) and improves task success by up to 33.3 percentage points. These results demonstrate that amortized planning can substantially improve both inference efficiency and control performance without retraining the world models. Code is available at https://github.com/hideinsewer/WARP-JEPA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In this work we proposed a architecture designed for the classification of ASD in toddlers using eye-tracking data, addressing the critical need for early and precise detection. The proposed model incorporates FMMix, a feature-level augmentation technique that bolsters the model's learning capacity, and in this work we proposed the ATHENA, which captures intricate feature relationships via an adaptive hypergraph framework. Evaluated on two datasets dataset of gaze coordinates-the model achieves state-of-the-art performance, with AUC scores of 99.80% and 99.86%, respectively. It also exhibits high accuracy of 98.45% and 98.56%, sensitivity 98.01% and 98.10%, and specificity 98.74% and 99.02%, respectively. Demonstrating its superior ability to identify subtle gaze patterns associated with ASD, these results significantly surpass existing methods, highlighting the model's potential for clinical ASD screening. Future efforts will focus on validation across broader populations and integration with multi-modal data to further refine diagnostic accuracy.

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language Models have had massive leaps in recent years. Yet, when tested on high-level cognitive tasks like pattern completion, abstract logic, and relational reasoning, they still fall short. This limitation stems from the models' lack of ability to find out underlying rules and maintain constant intermediate steps, rather than merely deciding on the correct option. To research these shortcomings, this study analyzes visual reasoning performance throughout a multilingual evaluation framework encompassing the BİLSEM, MM-IQ, Visual Puzzles, and PuzzleVQA datasets. We evaluate various data mixing strategies and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) methods. We observed that a correct answer does not always mean the reasoning chain is valid. Because of this, we adopt an LLM-as-a-Judge approach to assess the quality of reasoning beyond final answer accuracy. Our experimental results reveal that baseline models often exhibit more stable reasoning compared to fine-tuned counterparts. Most importantly, we observe that SFT can lead to model collapse, manifesting as infinite text loops or empty outputs. The study concludes that current VLMs still struggle with abstract visual reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Conflicting stakeholder objectives make it difficult to coordinate investments in urban resilience. We model conflict-driven urban design as a non-zero-sum digital board game in which stakeholder agents allocate scarce resources and balance individual payoffs against shared urban development and resilience objectives. We use multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) to train policies and examine their behavior across three settings. On a 4 x 4 board, learned policies reach about 84% of the total return found by a simulated annealing benchmark. On an 8 x 8 board with spatial and resource constraints, trained agents favor projects that improve resilience in self-play. In a 12 x 12 simulation initialized from historical maps of Tatsumi, Tokyo, trained agents achieve a mean total score of 452.1, compared with 357.3 for a random-policy baseline across 10,000 simulation runs. Gains in adaptability, sustainability, flood resistance, and greenery come with reductions in vitality and density. A human-in-the-loop interface supports replay and lets participants take over stakeholder decisions, providing a testbed for exploring these trade-offs in participatory urban planning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tree decision diagrams (TDDs), recently introduced by Capelli et al. (SAT 2026), are representations of Boolean functions. They generalize OBDDs by replacing variable orders with vtrees. The width of a TDD counts the distinct subfunctions across each vtree cut. Counting subfunctions is blind to linear structure. We introduce algebraic tree decision diagrams (ATDDs) by replacing this count with the rank of the cut matrix. The maximum of these ranks over all cuts, the factor rank, is exactly the width of the ATDD. Every TDD can be converted into an ATDD of no greater width. An ATDD is a tree tensor network in canonical form. ATDDs are exponentially more succinct than TDDs, and quasi-polynomially more succinct than structured deterministic DNNFs. Our main result is that this gain in succinctness does not cost canonicity. ATDDs are the first strict canonical form for a rank-based representation over arbitrary vtrees. For each function and vtree there is exactly one ATDD, over the rationals and without any genericity assumption. The canonicity admits a local characterization. Whether a tree tensor network is the ATDD of the Boolean function it computes can be checked in polynomial time from its tensors alone. Equivalence checking becomes a test of syntactic equality. The operation set includes polynomial-time model counting, weighted model counting, and a test whether a tree tensor network represents a Boolean function.
  Capelli et al. asked for the complexity of changing the vtree of a TDD and suggested local vtree changes. We answer the question. After a rotation of the vtree at one node, the new ATDD differs from the old one in two tensors only. Vtree change runs in output-polynomial time both for TDDs and for ATDDs.
  We formalize and verify our core results in Lean. We provide a prototype implementation of TDD and ATDD compilers as a first empirical validation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an important way to improve how language models reason, solve problems, and use tools. But getting it to work in practice is not always straightforward. Training can fail because a model receives no useful reward, learns to exploit the reward system, stops exploring, or appears to improve when the evaluation itself is misleading.
  In this book, we give a practical introduction to reinforcement learning post-training for language models, focusing on what works, what fails, and why. We begin with the foundations of post-training, explain GRPO and the training loop step by step, and then follow five training programs covering math reasoning, search agents, training stability, mixture-of-experts models, and software engineering tasks. Each program shows the problems encountered, the changes made, and the lessons learned.
  The book also examines what reinforcement learning actually changes inside a model. The remaining chapters provide a practical reference for reward design, evaluation, training tool-using agents, debugging common failures, reproducibility, rollout engines, and hardware. Written for researchers and practitioners working with open models, the book aims to help readers understand how to train models, recognize when something goes wrong, and decide what to try next.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Running a language model involves more than its trained weights. The same model can receive very different benchmark scores, produce different answers, or cost more to serve depending on how it is evaluated and run. In one experiment in this book, changing only the maximum response length moved accuracy on a math benchmark from 0.08% to 93.18%.
  This book explains how inference settings shape the results we report and the systems we deploy. We begin with evaluation, including generation limits, answer extraction, scoring methods, and quantization. We then examine why outputs can change across batches, attention backends, and caching, even when sampling is disabled. The next chapters cover serving performance, including batching, memory capacity, latency, throughput, and cost. We also study structured outputs, tool calls, reasoning budgets, and long-context tasks, showing what these controls and benchmarks do and do not establish.
  The book concludes with practical guidance for recording inference runs, checking evaluations, estimating capacity, and comparing systems fairly. Written for researchers and engineers working with language models, it aims to make inference results easier to understand, reproduce, and trust.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Natural images are often conjectured to lie on a low-dimensional manifold within the high-dimensional pixel space. Recent work leverages this to build self-contained pixel-space diffusion models without specialized machinery or pretrained components. A plain Transformer on raw image patches suffices, provided it predicts the clean image rather than the noise and compresses each patch through an aggressive bottleneck. The mixing operator, however, has remained softmax attention, inherited from sequence modeling rather than derived from denoising. For Gaussian noise, the optimal denoiser admits a canonical expansion in polynomial functionals of the observation, the Wiener--Volterra series. We prove that softmax attention, and any normalized mixing rule (including linear attention), cannot realize any polynomial map beyond an affine one. In contrast, Volterra layers realize this class directly and, on a data manifold, achieve approximation rates governed by the manifold dimension rather than the ambient pixel dimension. We therefore propose the architecture these results prescribe; raw image patches passed through a bottleneck embedding, followed by cascaded low-rank Volterra layers, which carry no activation function because each layer is nonlinear in its own right. To our knowledge this is the first pixel-space diffusion architecture whose mixing operators are Volterra layers throughout, with no attention, no convolution, no pretrained components, and no activation function on the residual path. On ImageNet $256 \times 256$ and $512 \times 512$, the resulting model is competitive with self-contained pixel-space diffusion at matched parameter and FLOP budgets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Much of the automation handed to language-model agents is procedural: each step needs little intelligence, but a long unattended run must follow a skill or SOP stated once at the start. We show that agents drift from such procedures over long runs, and that restating the instructions or resuming a stopped agent only moves the drift to another shortcut. We study an executable finite-state machine (FSM) that keeps the workflow state outside the model's context and enforces it at three points: tool calls, state transitions and the end of the agent's turn. In a pre-registered experiment on 200-account SOP-Bench batches, Claude Haiku 4.5 with the FSM decided on average 139 accounts correctly and by the procedure, against 48 for the strongest baseline (95% CI of the difference [46, 130]), and kept this rate to the end; across three models, the gain tracked how much the plain agent drifted. Because the runtime, not the model, supplies this long-horizon reliability, the FSM can let small models carry procedural work: Haiku 4.5 with it averaged more such decisions than plain Claude Sonnet 4.6 at 38% of the cost. Analyses on SWE-bench Verified, $τ^2$-bench, and staged and cross-context workflows further suggest that reliable long-horizon automation can come from stronger execution control, not only stronger models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit approximate recall: they retain coarse associations about past events while losing fidelity on specific details. Past research on LLMs has quantified different models' forecasting ability in real-world events using prediction markets, but has not studied how losing said fidelity impacts their forecasting abilities. For example: while an LLM correctly recalls that the answer to: ''Did Anna Sawai win the Best Drama Actress in the 76th Emmy Awards?'' is ''Yes,'' it incorrectly states the event date as September 22 rather than September 15. In this paper, we introduce a novel metric called the Recall Fidelity Score to study how imprecision in internalized knowledge propagates to forecasting capability on future events. On resolved historical markets, we find that higher local recall fidelity around semantically similar past events predicts lower forecasting error on resolved markets, and the same signal predicts smaller distance from open markets prices.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Detectors of AI-generated text are usually tuned and tested on passages of several hundred tokens, yet many real cases are short. We study score-based detection as a function of passage length. We give a closed form for how the false-positive rate of a threshold set on long text changes as length falls, and a formula for the number of tokens a detector needs to reach a target true-positive rate. We calibrate thresholds within length bins with conformal p-values, and we add a bin-level rule that abstains when the detector cannot be informative at that length. On synthetic scores the formulas agree with simulation, and binned calibration holds the false-positive rate at its nominal value at every length. On three zero-shot detectors (log-likelihood, log-rank, Binoculars), three corpora and two open-weight generators, binned calibration gives a mean false-positive rate of 5.0% in every detector-length cell from 10 to 200 tokens, averaged over 300 random document-level resplits. A single threshold set at 200 tokens gives 29% for Binoculars at 10 tokens and about 1% for log-likelihood, as the closed form predicts. No detector reaches an 80% true-positive rate, and the best, Binoculars at 200 tokens, reaches 59%, so the rule abstains in every cell at that floor and releases only the 200-token bins at floors of 0.3 and 0.5.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents are increasingly capable of discovering new mathematical results, yet their reasoning and generated proofs can be difficult to understand and verify. We introduce an interpretable framework for AI-guided mathematical discovery, focused on improving quantitative bounds on analytic constants. Our key insight is that existing proofs often contain slack in intermediate lemmas, which can be tightened without reconstructing the entire argument. To exploit this structure, we represent proofs as partially-formalized Lean blueprints: human-interpretable dependency graphs of definitions, lemmas, and theorems. An AI agent then iteratively refines the Lean code while preserving the blueprint's dependency structure, allowing local improvements to automatically propagate to a verified final bound. This interpretable structure allows humans to inspect the agent's refinements and understand how local improvements propagate to the final theorem. For Grothendieck's constant ($\mathcal{K}_G$), we obtain $$
  \mathcal{K}_G \leq
  \fracπ{2\log(1+\sqrt{2})} - 10^{-17}, $$ an explicit numerical improvement over Krivine's 1977 bound. For Korenblum's constant ($\mathcal{K}_K$), we obtain $$
  \mathcal{K}_K \geq 0.356565, $$ improving Wang's 2025 bound of $0.3554$. Our experiments demonstrate the potential of both partial formalization and iterative agentic refinement for interpretable mathematical discovery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) improves multi-hop question answering, but deployed systems face two costs that research comparisons rarely measure: building the graph, and removing documents from it. We present Lightfoot, a GraphRAG system in which every entity, link, fact and synonym edge is a deterministic function of per-passage extractions carrying chunk-level provenance. Indexing makes one language-model call per passage, retrieval makes none, and deleting a document is a database operation with no model calls. On the full 11,402-passage MuSiQue corpus we verify that, after deleting 10% of the documents, the graph is identical to a fresh index of the surviving documents. We compare Lightfoot with HippoRAG 2, LightRAG and fast-graphrag under identical extraction, answer and embedding models on MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA. Lightfoot builds the cheapest index on all three datasets, 26-31% below HippoRAG 2 and 3-6x below LightRAG and fast-graphrag. Against HippoRAG 2 it is significantly better on MuSiQue retrieval (+2.8 points recall@5 and +4.3 recall@10 on a held-out split) and on HotpotQA answers (+7.7 points exact match), and significantly worse on 2WikiMultiHopQA (-3.9 recall@2, -8.4 F1). Deleting the same documents took LightRAG 2,187 model calls and 3.7 hours; fast-graphrag has no deletion path. Code and per-question results: https://github.com/udayallu-ml/Lightfoot-GraphRAG

</details>

Abstract (英文): Method preconditions specify when a method may be applied in hierarchical task network (HTN) planning, but their meaning differs under two established semantics. The SHOP planners, like the classical HTN formalism of Erol, Hendler and Nau, require the precondition to hold immediately before the method's first action. HDDL, the input language of the International Planning Competition's HTN track, instead introduces an auxiliary action to check it; under partial order, unrelated actions may invalidate the precondition before the method starts. HDDL's designers adopted this weaker semantics because the first-action reading could not easily be compiled. Subsequent work has repeatedly identified its compilation under partial order as an open question, with existing transformations limited to total order. We give a compilation of the first-action semantics into standard partially ordered HTN planning without method preconditions using only two additional facts, thereby resolving a longstanding question in the HTN planning community.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative agents are game characters whose plans, conversations, and memories are written on the fly by a large language model (LLM). The original Generative Agents demonstration used cloud inference in a custom simulation. We present GARP, a platform for real-time generative agents inside Godot, and describe the Character Behavior System used in GARP 2. The complete loop runs on local hardware using a 3B-parameter open-weight model and no cloud service. Alongside the original planning, memory, and reflection, GARP gives each agent an emotion system driven by LLM appraisal, four graded personality axes drawn from the Big Five that set simulation parameters as well as prompt text, six needs whose urgency grows over time, and pairwise relationships updated after every conversation. The system narrows 50+ candidate actions to typically 3-8, constrains model responses with JSON schemas, and surfaces important memories in decision prompts. A layered optimization strategy (an adjustable game clock, decisions taken ahead of need, multi-tier caching, prompt compression, and fully asynchronous execution) keeps the game responsive while the model thinks. In a nine-scenario evaluation, 25 of 27 runs passed log-based checks. Both failures occurred in the birthday party scenario, where one guest omitted the event from their daily plan.

Abstract (英文): Random perturbations around a shared checkpoint can produce experts for distinct tasks, but shared origin does not ensure that their gains survive composition. We formulate expert-pair selection for joint base-relative retention, measured by strict joint success and worst-task gain with unconditional abstention accounting. An exact finite-difference decomposition and an arbitrarily small orthogonal counterexample show why proximity and orthogonality alone are insufficient. We instantiate this formulation with quality-constrained spectral selection, a fixed-budget framework combining cross-task screening, layerwise pair geometry, separate validation, and Base fallback. Across six checkpoints, 21 task pairs, and five independent perturbation pools, the procedure improves strict joint success over Top-1 averaging throughout and leads the four-method model-scale comparison on four of six checkpoints. For the five models with at least 1.5B parameters, matched quality-only, global-cosine, and spectral ranking attain 49.1%, 50.5%, and 53.1% joint success. Under this matched protocol, the spectral selector also yields the highest worst-task gain and Weak Pareto rate and the lowest regression rate. Mean worst-task gain reaches +0.61 percentage points, while 17.1% regression and 13.0% abstention show that composability remains conditional. Project page: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Thickets-Merge/.

Our first result is an upper bound: an oracle gate deciding per prompt whether to invoke SC reaches 109-174% of always-SC's accuracy at 23-30% of its compute in all 12/12 cases, so at N=5 always-SC is dominated everywhere on our grid. That margin is exactly the rate at which SC flips correct greedy answers to wrong, here 3-10.5% of prompts, which on Qwen-Q4 x MATH-500 exceeds the wrong-to-right rate and leaves always-SC 3 pt worse than always-greedy. Our second result is a deployable approximation: a gate over greedy-pass signals skips 45-99% of SC calls, with bootstrap CI half-widths under 5 pt, removing 36-79% of always-SC compute. Accuracy retention at those operating points has point estimates of 98.1-98.8%, which we report as point estimates because their CIs are wide (lower bounds 76-86%) over an SC accuracy of only 15-36%. The evidence covers N=5 on two <=1B families and two math benchmarks, which is the scope we claim. Code is available at https://github.com/heywanrong/SiftSC.

Abstract (英文): Frontline sales staff spend much of their day re-coordinating quotes after customers change quantities, ask for discounts or new payment terms, or costs move. We present LoMASA (Local Multi-Agent Sales Assistant), a training-free multi-agent system that runs entirely on one desktop computer. A 35B mixture-of-experts model (System-1) makes each decision in a single forward pass and reads its confidence from the probabilities of the answer options. A 27B dense model (System-2) writes customer replies and escalation briefs and is consulted when System-1 is unsure. Code computes every price, margin and option, and hard pricing rules cannot be overridden. On a test split of 500 simulated episodes with 1512 decisions built from public supply-chain data, the salesperson has to decide only 19.5% of decisions: 38.0% are settled automatically, with a median latency of 2.67 seconds, and 42.5% are escalated to a manager by the hard rules. On the automatically settled decisions LoMASA is as accurate as a rule baseline (0.894 vs.\ 0.894) and makes fewer commitments whose margin later falls below the floor (2.61% vs 4.53%); the price is additional escalations to the manager. Option probabilities are well calibrated without any training, unlike confidence stated in words by the same model. We also report where the design does not help: the 27B model rarely changes a decision.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-context applications, long-lived agents, and multi-agent systems require large language models (LLMs) to process and retain extensive information. In these settings, the KV cache can exceed GPU high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity, necessitating offloading to host memory, where CPU-to-GPU transfers can dominate inference latency. While KV cache compression can reduce data movement, query-agnostic compression that guarantees accurate attention for arbitrary queries is extremely challenging, if not impossible. We introduce VerKV, a hybrid approach that provides $(ε, δ)$ guarantees for approximating attention on any given query. VerKV maintains a compact set of query-agnostic pivotal tokens on the GPU, together with a token-level sketch of the full KV cache, while retaining the complete cache in CPU memory. We introduce a novel stochastic sampling procedure, PoissonComp, to select the pivotal tokens. At inference time, our proposed KVFetch algorithm uses the pivotal tokens and the sketch to estimate the relative error of the resulting attention computation. If the estimate satisfies a user-specified $(ε, δ)$ tolerance, inference proceeds without accessing host memory. Otherwise, VerKV performs query-aware retrieval to fetch additional tokens from the CPU, refining the attention computation to meet the required tolerance. Thus, VerKV enables attention with bounded approximation error while minimizing CPU-to-GPU transfers. We show that PoissonComp outperforms state-of-the-art approaches to KV cache compression. Combined with KVFetch, it achieves near-dense performance (90-100% of dense quality at 32K context length, averaged over RULER and LOFT), using Qwen3-4B, Qwen3.5-9B and Ministral-3-8B, while reducing the GPU memory footprint by 8-32x and keeping CPU-to-GPU memory fetches within 2-23% of the cache per generated token, depending on the model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) perform well on general coding tasks, but their capabilities in application-layer embedded software development remain underexplored. Existing embedded benchmarks primarily focus on hardware-in-the-loop validation in simulated environments. We introduce RealEmbedBench, a benchmark of 5,142 samples covering three application-layer tasks: code generation and completion, state machine modeling and implementation, and cross-OS code translation. We evaluate six representative LLMs and find substantial performance differences across tasks and models. Project context, especially the local code window, significantly affects code generation and completion. Models perform reasonably well at state machine modeling but struggle to translate these models into executable code. Cross-OS translation performance varies substantially by direction. Translation from Harmony to RT-Thread is particularly challenging, with an average pass rate of 0.1667. These findings suggest that developers should consider local code context when using LLMs for code generation and completion and select models according to the translation direction. They also motivate the use of more real-world state machine implementation data in LLM training to narrow the gap between modeling and executable code.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.

Abstract (英文): A driving foundation model must learn reusable decision knowledge from rare critical events and apply it under strict runtime constraints. We introduce DriveFoundry, a closed-loop framework improving iteration efficiency under data and computation budgets by coupling structured causal data refinement with adaptive inference. The Data Engine builds traceable scene-candidate records linking scene evidence and factor-decision relations to candidate trajectories and outcome assessments. Candidate comparisons and counterfactual annotations clarify how scene factors affect behavioral feasibility and preference. Verified model failures guide VLM-assisted annotation and expert correction, refining training data and annotation systems. The model engine, Xiaomi-Drive-o1, unifies Perception, Causation, Action, World, and Value within a shared vision-language backbone to enable cross-task knowledge transfer. Block-wise latent refill passes candidate-specific evidence and predicted visual consequences between task blocks, connecting scene understanding, planning, and evaluation. A learned value-of-computation head selects tasks and allocates latent tokens based on expected benefit and cost, adapting inference to each decision. On PhysicalAI, the 9B model achieves the lowest ADE@1 across evaluated splits and horizons among tested models. In AlpaSim, it outperforms Alpamayo 1.5 across all metrics and achieves lower close-encounter and off-road rates than the 34B Alpamayo 2 Super, while trailing in progress and AlpaSim scores. For the 0.8B variant, joint training reduces 5 s minADE@6 by about 54% without altering the inference path or token budget. Adaptive inference matches full reasoning performance using only 28% of tokens. Real-vehicle tests confirm runtime feasibility, supporting a unified paradigm for learning from critical experience and dynamically allocating computation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Purpose: To estimate how often tool-using large language model (LLM) agents record clinically relevant false or unsupported information in simulated radiology documentation and how much safeguards applied before each action reduce these errors.
  Materials and Methods: In this simulation study (October 2026), 48 synthetic, radiologist-reviewed base scenarios covered finding communication, tumor board preparation and follow-up management. Forty-three also had a misleading-information variant (91 variants). Six LLM agents (Claude Sonnet 5.5, GPT-6.1-Sol, GLM-5.3, MiniMax M3, Gemma-4-31B, Qwen3.8-27B) completed each variant with permissions only, deterministic rules, and rules plus a locally run LLM checker (1638 episodes). The primary outcome was at least one executed high-severity violation per episode. Risk differences were estimated per agent at base-scenario level and, exploratorily, pooled across agents, with analyses of document access and blocks.
  Results: With permissions only, 7.3%-27.1% of episodes had a high-severity violation (pooled across agents, 19.4%). Pooled, it was 16.7% with rules (-2.8 percentage points; 95% CI: -6.1, 0.5) and 13.2% with rules plus the LLM checker (-6.2; 95% CI: -9.5, -3.0). Adding the LLM checker to rules changed it by -3.5 (95% CI: -6.3, -0.7). No difference between agents was detected (P = .43), and task success did not decrease detectably. In misleading variants, agents opened the altered document in 83% of episodes regardless of error, and 58% of erroneous statements cited it. Most blocked actions (64%-68%) would not have caused a violation and were returned for revision.
  Conclusion: Layered safeguards reduced but did not prevent high-severity errors. Errors after misleading information reflected misjudged rather than missed information.

</details>

Abstract (英文): While Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) accelerates isolated algorithm and kernel synthesis, distributed LLM training depends on tightly coupled launch settings, operator kernels, and communication schedules, where single edits often hit out-of-memory (OOM) boundaries, compiler conflicts, measurement jitter, or silent numerical drift. We present InfraRSI, the agentic harness that turns open-ended human commands into verified training optimizations via a five-stage workflow: init locks editable scope and a fixed task contract over workload, loss, and timing invariants to prevent reward hacking, doctor profiles baseline phases and calibrates cluster noise, run co-optimizes launch knobs and code patches before re-verifying top candidates against the baseline and logging findings to a cross-hardware experience bank, and report and apply audit source paths, track failed-trial costs, and commit promoted changes. Rather than stepping greedily from a single incumbent, InfraRSI initializes from diverse experience-seeded configurations and fits global execution-time and censored peak-memory models across all trials, including OOMs. Using these models, it prices per-setting memory costs, revises miscalibrated LLM priors from live runs, and pairs memory-hungry optimizations with memory savers and verified code patches. Across four single- and multi-node dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLM workloads, InfraRSI achieves a speedup of up to $2.75\times$ and lifts mean Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) from $17.4\%$ to $30.0\%$, outperforming existing tuners and coding agents while transferring experience across GPUs. As it improves training infrastructure efficiency, InfraRSI forms a two-level Recursive Self-Improvement loop---revising priors online within runs and distilling experience across clusters---to push foundation model scaling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents acting across web applications and online services often need multi-turn corrections to align their work with users' underspecified goals and preferences. In practice, users may identify problems in the current outcome without specifying the desired result, so learning user intentions requires interpreting each correction against the agent's preceding and revised outcomes. These expectations are often connected to a particular context, making it hard to know when they apply and how to ensure the agent later acts as the user expects. To this end, we propose LiveAsset, an intent harness that learns reusable intent assets from corrective interactions and uses them for runtime control of agent execution. LiveAsset jointly interprets each correction with the agent's original and revised results to infer user intent, incrementally updating the assets while preserving earlier requirements that remain applicable. The learned requirements guide agent execution from task entry and provide criteria for runtime verification, which uses the explanations to assess applicability before each reply is delivered and directs the agent to revise the result to address unmet requirements while preserving those already satisfied. Following a learning phase in which simulated users progressively reveal requirements through multi-turn corrections, we evaluate LiveAsset using same-task replay of the original requests on π-Bench, PERMA, and Vita and cross-task transfer on PAST-Bench and SkillEvolBench. Experiments show that LiveAsset better fulfills requirements learned from corrective interactions and improves performance on these benchmarks compared with memory, skill, and rule-based harness baselines. Code is available at github.com/landian60/LiveAsset.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A tool-using language model must decide whether to retry a failed call, correct it, or stop and tell the user. The error text alone may not be enough: the same feedback can lead to different actions depending on where it appears in the conversation. We isolate this delivery problem by varying placement, guidance, retained history and turn structure. Two studies with plans fixed before data collection, across 13 open models from 3B to 70B parameters, compare the next action and subsequent task completion, with exact deterministic reruns. A new user turn that retains the failed exchange avoids the severe stopping failure observed in Llama models when that exchange is removed. It is non-inferior to history rewriting in pooled synthetic comparisons, but is not best for every model. We implement this form as an opt-in recovery layer within a deterministic inference engine and evaluate it on public benchmarks and a coding agent. On ToolMaze, it raises success on explicit errors by 14.2 points with 7-8B models and 10.5 points with 32-70B models; Loud/Quiet, 24 multi-turn tasks with visible failures and silent corruption, scored by final-state agreement, shows no measurable gain. A client given the exact fault kind performs better still. The findings support delivery as a distinct factor in recovery, not an engine-exclusive solution or a universal retry policy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In financial backtesting and other point-in-time tasks, external inputs can be restricted by date, but model parameters may retain financial facts disclosed later, introducing parametric look-ahead bias. We introduce FinCutBench, a continually updated monthly financial knowledge benchmark for measuring how far a fixed model's financial knowledge extends over calendar time. The current dataset contains 9,000 Chinese and English four-option multiple-choice questions from January 2023 to September 2026, covering Chinese and U.S. markets and four categories of financial information. We construct monthly knowledge profiles using the approximate months in which the tested facts became public. After a profile passes the decline test and knot checks, we define the FinCut Boundary as the first month when the fitted profile reaches its lower level, and report the stability of the estimated date under resampling. Boundary dates and localization precision vary across models. Using the independently measured Transition Start and Boundary as fixed references for downstream sample grouping, we find clear relative declines after the Boundary in U.S. asset ranking and earnings recall for some models, while Chinese-market ranking estimates vary across models and all eight disclosure-period-paired forecast-error comparisons have unadjusted $p$ values above 0.10. The FinCut Boundary thus provides an empirical reference for the temporal scope of parametric knowledge in point-in-time financial evaluation, supporting evaluation-window selection and result interpretation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can write the weights of small neural networks directly, without gradient descent. This weight-space programming creates policies for sequential decision-making from a task description alone, but nothing guarantees that a written policy behaves as intended. We combine weight-space programming with probabilistic model checking. Given an environment modeled as a Markov decision process and a requirement in probabilistic computation tree logic (PCTL), an LLM writes all weights of a policy network, optionally repairs them on a dataset of optimal actions, and receives model-checking feedback until the requirement holds or a round limit is reached. The model checker Storm computes, rather than estimates from simulations, the probability that the policy satisfies the requirement, so every returned policy is verified. With two LLMs on three environments, the created policy of each of the 18 runs attains the optimal probability, before any dataset or model-checking feedback. Four of six policies programmed for two transporter jobs also complete three jobs with probability 1, so they generalize to a task that the LLM never saw. The pipeline also programs a policy that satisfies a complex safety requirement expressed with the PCTL until operator in all six runs. In one of them, dataset repair raised the accuracy to 98.4\% but broke the requirement, and one round of model-checking feedback restored it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a scanpath descriptor discriminates a task, it is rarely clear what the discrimination rests on. We propose a perturbation profile for trial-level descriptors. Five transformations each destroy one stated property while preserving a listed set of invariants: fixation order, duration-position coupling, the duration distribution, orientation and handedness, and fine spatial structure. The classifier is refit, and the drop in participant-held-out AUC is the pipeline's sensitivity to that property. Because refitting lets the learner use redundant information, this describes the refitted pipeline, not what a fitted classifier used. Intervals come from a participant bootstrap that refits; in simulations at the sample sizes analysed, where the target was zero they excluded zero at a pooled rate of at most 2.5%, and they covered the target in 99.6% of 2720 cases. We profiled seven descriptors on twelve binary problems from three public datasets: reading (SB-SAT, 95 participants), program comprehension (EMIP, up to 159) and video and game viewing (GazeBase, 60). Permuting fixation order raised the AUC of a descriptor built on mean saccade amplitude on three problems (by up to 0.23), because permutation turns amplitude into a measure of spatial spread. A "comprehension" contrast that pooled two programs reached AUC 0.70 with a spread-only baseline and fell to 0.47 within one program: the profile located the confound in stimulus layout. On native versus non-native readers, recurrence was sensitive to order and to jitter at the scale of text lines but not of characters, while a two-feature duration and amplitude descriptor reached the same AUC (0.65). Abstract (英文): Data-driven remaining useful life (RUL) estimators for turbofan engines are accurate but opaque, and their internal features typically encode remaining life rather than any specific component's state. This paper presents an interpretable genetic fuzzy tree that predicts RUL from cruise-condition sensor means on N-CMAPSS while exposing a per-component diagnostic layer inspectable rule by rule. The tree follows the engine hierarchy: sensors to component health, then to bounded component damage, spool damage, and RUL, so each level answers a different maintenance question. Training is staged so the prognostic objective cannot reshape the diagnostic layers: health leaves are fitted to physical targets and frozen, a deterministic fuzzy damage map with fleet-referenced thresholds is imposed on each component, and only the aggregation is optimized for RUL. It consumes 13 cruise-mean scalars per cycle rather than a trajectory, which removes any sequence model. On the native split it reaches 8.44 cycles RMSE on the multi-fault subset and 11.51 across all modes with 333 free parameters, 411 total rules, 32 to 12,000 times fewer than compiled baselines. An exhaustive sensor search bounds which components are diagnosable under the tested cruise-mean design, and the rule base is structured to admit formal verification, which this study does not discharge.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Digital programmatic advertising operates through complex, multi-hop supply chains. The opacity of these intermediary networks leads to significant efficiency losses, often referred to as the ``ad-tech tax.'' While current industry practices utilize heuristic Supply Path Optimization (SPO) to trim inefficient routes, they fail to optimize the equilibrium of the entire network. This paper extends previous work on Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) intermediation models by applying them to traceable programmatic supply chains mapped via the OpenRTB SupplyChain object. We introduce multi-attribute utility functions and Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) to account for substitutable routing (OR nodes) and percentage-based fee structures. Furthermore, we outline a computational methodology and experimental framework using public \texttt{schain} and \texttt{sellers.json} data to benchmark optimal Nash social welfare against current uncoordinated market outcomes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We built a hybrid multi-agent trading system that combines large language model (LLM) analysts with momentum-based portfolio construction and rule-based execution. Company-news and macroeconomic agents return structured reports to a coordinator that sets stock weights and cash reserves. We evaluate the portfolio engine using its broker-recorded paper account, which operated with deterministic fallbacks, and compare LLM-enabled configurations through retrospective replays. From March 11 to August 28, 2026, the account returned 34.5%, compared with 14.4% for the S&P 500 fund SPY over the same dates. This raw return advantage came with a larger maximum drawdown. Reconstructed policies also finish above matched SPY and QQQ references when assessments are used in the same session or deferred to the next. Design comparisons examine cash authority, selective invocation, and response contracts. A constant-cash control gives a return estimate close to stance-based sizing, and shorter report contracts improve observed parsing but can change recommendations.

Abstract (英文): The use of generative AI in political campaigns has drawn public concern and policy attention. Public concerns range from distrust of candidates to skepticism about the authenticity and accuracy of political advertising, including AI-generated images and video. Nearly half of U.S. states have enacted laws that prohibit, or require disclosure of, "materially deceptive" AI-generated content (AIGC) in elections to guard against these concerns. This study measures how candidates and their campaigns produce and circulate AIGC during an election cycle. We develop two measures that estimate not only wether campaigns use AIGC, but how much they produce (magnitude) and in what forms (modality). We apply these measures to a novel dataset of text and images from the campaign websites, X accounts, and emails of 12,977 candidates for U.S. political office from 2023 to 2025. We report three findings. First, detectable AIGC from campaigns increased significantly across modalities over the observation window, and campaigns that use AIGC frequently do not disclose it. Second, incumbents' campaigns produced significantly less AIGC than challengers' campaigns. Third, candidates in states with AIGC disclosure or prohibition laws do not produce measurably less AIGC than candidates in other states. Our method and findings have implications for transparency and trust in elections, and on state laws that regulate AI use in campaigns. Because current state laws largely target mainly "materially deceptive" content, we identify additional policy mechanisms that could increase electoral transparency and improve trust as AIGC becomes more prevalent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A deployed language model does not merely answer questions. It spends computation, consumes time, consults evidence, exposes memory, invites or avoids human review, and sometimes makes mistakes whose consequences are radically unequal. Yet LLM systems are still commonly compared as if the relevant object were a single model and the relevant outcome were a single benchmark score. This paper argues that the unit of analysis should instead be the deployment policy: the mechanism that decides when to retrieve, when to remember, when to call a larger model, when to verify, and when not to answer at all. We introduce risk-adjusted deployment efficiency (RADE), a system-level objective that scores the utility a deployment delivers net of inference, retry, oversight, and priced expected failure cost, while treating harms that cannot be priced as hard budgets alongside constraints on latency, privacy, and resources. The framework brings selective routing, retrieval and governed memory, efficient specialization, uncertainty estimation, abstention, and human escalation into a common account. We then examine five deployment regimes in which the same nominal LLM capability leads to different rational architectures, and propose a reporting protocol based on risk-coverage behavior, end-to-end cost, update drift, memory exposure, evidence quality, and escalation load. The central claim is modest but consequential: frontier capability should be treated as a resource to allocate, not a default state to maintain. A safe and sustainable system is not the one that always uses the strongest model; it is the one that spends capability where the reduction in risk or increase in utility justifies the cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Continual instruction tuning often preserves old skills with task-private modules. Inference still requires selecting the right module from the prompt alone. We introduce Task-Isolated Latent Adapter Continual Training (TILA-CT), a replay-free method that makes each task module self-indexing. Each task receives a latent memory and adapter bank in a frozen LLM. During adaptation, the memory gathers causal-prefix information and injects task-conditioned context. For access, its persistent state provides the routing prototype used by a shared prompt-only router. On the 8-task CT-Pipeline stream, TILA-CT achieves 65.19 acquisition and 63.98 final macro score, with 1.38 points of average forgetting. A middle-reversed order gives 65.38 acquisition and 64.17 final macro score. Across 6 backbones, final scores remain within 1.5 points of acquisition. Ablations support memory-derived routing keys and causal gathering. Final macro routing accuracy is 93.9\%, and oracle routing recovers 1.21 final macro points. These results separate preserved task computation from learned access to it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Expert analog layouts differ from auto-generated ones in density and regularity, not only in legality: experts make a succession of discrete, hierarchical physical decisions and revisit each after extracted electrical results, while most existing automated flows act on a physical search space fixed before the controller runs. We present CASCoDe, a closed-loop LLM agent framework for analog netlist-to-layout design that formalizes those expert decisions as a hierarchical discrete action space-sizing selection, structural floorplan, local layout refinement, routing-attempt selection-on which the agent runs feedback-driven tree search from a circuit netlist to a verified GDS. The agent authors the circuit-specific floorplans and scripts, and an execution layer of interface adaptations over adapted tools make every action land at physical implementation and be judged by DRC, LVS, and extracted-RC simulation. On 20 SKY130 amplifiers spanning single-, two-, and three-stage topologies, CASCoDe is the only flow that delivers a DRC- and LVS-clean routed layout when meeting design specifications for every circuit. It reduces area by 59-85% (mean 71%) against a conventional P&R flow on identical PCells, raises mean device density from 0.39 to 0.74 against APPlace, MAGICAL, ALIGN and ICPI. A layout that beats every baseline in area and legality appears after 11 minutes of agent time on average, and ablations show that the structured action space and the feedback-guided tree search each reduce area and search cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many major scientific advances come from approaching a problem with a new or novel perspective. While large language models (LLMs) with internet-scale knowledge seem poised to take such perspectives, their consistent generation remains elusive. Instead, models are drawn to dominant views and preexisting, in-field bias. To explore the potential of fresh eyes to increase creativity, this paper proposes naive LLMs, models that are made intentionally ignorant of a specific topic. Through deprivation in training or intentional forgetting, naive models are unburdened by existing dogma, allowing them to be free to make new associations and conjectures. These naive models can then be paired with a stronger mentor model that can refine their unique perspectives into concrete proposals. For the 1B and 7B models tested in this paper, naive models consistently produce seed ideas that are more novel and useful than baseline counterparts, and mentor refinement improves the ideas even further. Thus, contrary to the prevailing tradition of scaling up training data, we demonstrate a case where less information is actually more powerful, in particular when the aim is to discover genuinely outside-the-box solutions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The Pravda Network is a Russian state-aligned media aggregator, with over 110 websites and more than 15 million articles published since 2023. Given its prolific and repetitive output, emerging scholarship argues that the Pravda Network was built to influence AI systems. We conduct a large-scale audit of leading AI chatbots to understand: (1) the retrieval rates of Pravda Network articles, (2) the conditions of retrieval, and (3) whether chatbot responses affirm or caveat this content. We build a 10,000 prompt corpus from sampled Pravda Network headlines varied along two dimensions (headline rewording and user skepticism) and query 11 AI chatbots via the web interface (n=104,997 completed responses). Overall, we find susceptibility to Pravda Network retrieval to be infrequent---appearing in 7.3% of responses---and highly platform dependent---with three chatbots responsible for 94% of retrievals (You.com, Claude, and Copilot). Among the frontier models, Claude is an outlier: ChatGPT, Gemini, and Meta AI retrieved no Pravda Network articles, likely indicative of a domain-level filtering policy. All 11 chatbots, however, retrieved other Russian state-aligned media to some degree (median=13.56% of responses). When Pravda Network articles are surfaced, caveat rates vary widely by platform (from 5.3% to 76.2%), with You.com providing the fewest caveats. We also identify the tactic of headline washing: where 9.92% of sampled Pravda Network headlines attribute their reporting to other, often more credible, media outlets, while the articles themselves are republished from Russian state-aligned media. We discuss headline washing's potential to launder state media as independent reporting. With this audit, we provide a systematic analysis of AI chatbots' susceptibility to Russia's latest attempt to manipulate the information environment.

</details>
